A mathematical survey on Fourier type integral transform and their offshoots: windowed Fourier transform, wavelet transform and Stockwell transform
Bivek Gupta, Amit K. Verma

Abstract
This comprehensive review paper delves into the intricacies of advanced Fourier type integral transforms and their mathematical properties, with a particular focus on fractional Fourier transform (FrFT), linear canonical transform (LCT), quadratic phase Fourier transform (QPFT), and their associated offshoots: windowed Fourier transform, wavelet transform, and Stockwell transform. In the pursuit of a deeper understanding of these transformations, we explore their convolution properties, shedding light on their capacity to define windowed, wavelet and Stockwell transforms in the realm of Fourier, fractional Fourier and quadratic phase Fourier transforms. This review also expands its purview to the realm of uncertainty principles. Several uncertainty principles, like Heisenberg, logarithmic, local, Rényi uncertainty principles, etc., within the context of fractional Fourier, linear canonical, and quadratic phase Fourier transforms, as well as their derivative offshoots are presented in the paper both for the functions of complex as well as quatenrion valued. In particular, the counterpart of several important inequalities of classical Fourier transform are also presented in details for the quaternion case. This article also reviews that multiresolution analysis that has been developed in the literature so far.



Keywords
: Fourier Transform, Short-Time Fourier Transform, Wavelet Transform, Stockwell Transform, Time-Frequency Resolution, Uncertainty Principle, Multiresolution Analysis, Sampling Theorem, Convolution, Quaternion.

AMS Subject Classification
: 42A38

The Fourier transform (FT) is a spectacular mathematical analysis tool that has long been acknowledged as having the greatest significance to contemporary science and technology developments. There have been numerous mathematical advances in the field of Fourier analysis over time, which have had major effects on the traditional FT. In the ever-expanding domain of signal processing and analysis, the quest for deeper insights, finer resolutions, and more comprehensive interpretations remains unceasing. This review paper embarks on an exploration of advanced signal transformation techniques that transcend the classical boundaries of the Fourier domain. We delve into the profound world of signal transformation, examining the Fractional Fourier Transform (FrFT), Linear Canonical Transform (LCT), Quadratic Phase Fourier Transform (QPFT), and their derived offshoots: the Windowed Fourier Transform, Wavelet Transform, and Stockwell Transform. These transforms offer a sophisticated toolkit that transcends the conventional transforms. The Fractional Fourier Transform, for instance, introduces fractional orders, allowing us to analyze signals with dynamic and complex frequency content, such as chirp signals and those influenced by dispersion. The Linear Canonical Transform unifies diverse mathematical transforms into a unified framework, providing a comprehensive approach to handling signals with intricate spatiotemporal characteristics. It transcends the limitations of individual transforms, making it a valuable asset in fields as diverse as optics, signal processing, and quantum mechanics. The most recent edition, and the generalization of all, i.e., Fourier transform, fractional Fourier transform and the linear canonical transform, the Quadratic Phase Fourier Transform refines our understanding of signals by focusing on the often-overlooked quadratic phase components. This transformation is indispensable for applications like radar, sonar, and image processing, where precise characterization of signals with varying phase characteristics is paramount.

The introduction of the windowed Fourier transform, wavelet transform, and Stockwell transform arises from the inherent properties of the classical Fourier transform, specifically its use of a global kernel. Because of the global kernel, Fourier transform fails in giving local properties of signal. To overcome these limitations, the Windowed Fourier Transform (WFT) was introduced. It incorporates a window function that acts as a localized filter to select a specific segment of the signal. By applying the Fourier transform to this windowed segment, the WFT allows for the analysis of signal characteristics within this localized region. This approach provides more detailed and localized information about the signal’s frequency content. The Wavelet Transform further extends the concept of localized analysis. Wavelets are a family of functions that are adjustable in scale and position. By using wavelets as the analysis function, the Wavelet Transform offers both time and frequency localization. It can capture high and low-frequency components with varying time resolutions, which is particularly useful for understanding transient and non-stationary features in signals. Meanwhile, the Stockwell transform is designed to address signals with rapidly changing frequency components. It employs a windowed approach to capture local characteristics of the signal in the time-frequency domain. The Stockwell Transform, like the WFT and Wavelet Transform, enables a localized view of the signal’s frequency content, facilitating the analysis of non-stationary signals. The introduction of the windowed Fourier transform, wavelet transform, and Stockwell transform is driven by the need to go beyond the global view of the classical Fourier transform. These techniques provide localized analysis, offering a more detailed and nuanced understanding of signals, particularly those with complex, non-stationary, or transient characteristics. They are indispensable tools in signal processing, offering the ability to extract specific features from signals in diverse applications, from image analysis to audio processing. Similar, limitation of the fractional Fourier transform, linear canonical transform and the quadratic phase Fourier transform as the Fourier transform in the fractional Fourier domain, linear canonical domain and the quadratic phase Fourier domain, results in the development of their offshoots as windowed Fourier transform, wavelet transform and the Stockwell transform, in the realm of the fractional Fourier transform, linear canonical transform and the quadratic phase Fourier transform.

Apart from the function of complex-valued (
\mathbb{C}
-valued), researchers are also actively involved in studying these transforms for quaternion-valued (
\mathbb{H}
-valued) functions. Indeed due to non-commutativity of the quaternion multiplication, extension of the complex Fourier transform to quaternion valued functions can be at least of three different types, left sided, right sides and the two sided. The non-commutativity of quaternion multiplication has given rise to distinct variants of quaternion Fourier transform including windowed Fourier transform, wavelet transform, and the Stockwell transform for the quaternion valued functions. These quaternion-based transforms consider the non-commutative properties of quaternion algebra and provide unique insights into signals characterized by both magnitude and phase components. The quaternion Fourier transform extends traditional Fourier analysis to quaternion-valued signals, offering a unified framework for time-frequency analysis, while windowed quaternion Fourier transforms focus on localized time-frequency features within quaternion signals. QWTs and the QSTs enable multi-resolution analysis of quaternion signals, capturing both amplitude and phase information facilitate the study of non-stationary signals in the quaternion domain. These specialized approaches have found applications in diverse fields, particularly in multidimensional and multichannel signal analysis, where the traditional Fourier transforms fail in doing so.

This review paper is intended to discuss the important mathematical properties of the fractional Fourier transform, linear canonical transform, quadratic phase Fourier transform and their associated offshoots: windowed Fourier transform, wavelet transform and Stockwell transform for the functions of complex-valued and and the quaternion-valued. In what follows, we mention the theme of this review paper point wise.

•

This article is devoted to the survey of the convolutions and the corresponding convolution theorem associated with the fractional Fourier transform, linear canonical transform and the quadratic phase Fourier transform both for complex and the quaternion-valued functions.

•

To survey the convolution based joint time-frequency analysis tools like wavelet transform and the Stockwell transform in the Fractional Fourier domain, linear canonical domain and the quadratic phase Fourier domain, both for the complex and the quaternion valued functions.

•

Multiresolution analysis (MRA) are the fundamental tools in the construction of a wavelet. Several MRA are developed in the literature in the last few year, associated with the FrWT and LCWT. This paper collects the MRA developed so far in the literature.

•

Important inequalities like Hausdorff-Young inequality, Pitts inequality are of fundamental importance in Fourier analysis. This review paper is also devoted to survey the counterpart of these inequalities for the FrFT, LCT and the QPFT, both for the function of complex as well as quaternion valued functions. Also, we survey the Lieb’s inequality for the v in the content of the FrFT, LCT and the QPFT.

•

Several qualitative and quantitative uncertainty principle for the Fourier transform has been explored in the literature for the functions of complex valued. We survey the Heisenberg, local, logarithmic and Rényi (in particular Shannon) uncertainty principles and their variants for the FrFT, LCT and the QPFT for the complex and quaternion valued functions.

•

We also see the boundedness of the WT, FrWT and the LCWT in the various function spaces like Sobolev space, Morrey space, BMO space, Hardy space, generalized Sobolev space,etc.

Through this exploration, we aim to provide a comprehensive resource for researchers, engineers, and mathematicians interested in advanced signal processing and analysis. By elucidating the mathematical foundations and properties of these transforms and the associated uncertainty principles, we endeavor to advance the understanding and application of these tools in a wide range of scientific and technological domains.

Several sections comprise our survey. Each section is devoted to a single integral transform and its corresponding essential results. Before we move to the section-wise survey, we recall some basic definitions and results.

1
Preliminaries

Let
\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the
n
-dimensional Euclidean space with the euclidean norm
|\cdot|,
i.e., for
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

|\boldsymbol{x}|=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{2}_{i}}.

We define
|\boldsymbol{x}|_{p}=|x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}|
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{0}=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|\boldsymbol{x}|_{p}\neq 0\}.

For
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}),

\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}=(x_{1}+y_{1},x_{2}+y_{2},\ldots,x_{n}+y_{n}),

\boldsymbol{x}\boldsymbol{y}=(x_{1}y_{1},x_{2}y_{2},\ldots,x_{n}y_{n}).
If further
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{0}
, then
\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{\boldsymbol{y}}=(\frac{x_{1}}{y_{1}},\frac{x_{2}}{y_{2}},\ldots,\frac{x_{n}}{y_{n}}).

Definition 1.1
.

For
1\leq p<\infty,

L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a Banach space of all complex valued measurable function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|f(\boldsymbol{t})|^{p}d\boldsymbol{t}<\infty,

with norm defined by

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|f(\boldsymbol{t})|^{p}d\boldsymbol{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

In particular,
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a Hilbert space, where the inner product inducing the norm is given by

\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\boldsymbol{t})\overline{g(\boldsymbol{t})}d\boldsymbol{t},

where
\overline{g(\boldsymbol{t})}
is the conjugate of
g(\boldsymbol{t}).

Definition 1.2
.

Let
A
be an arbitrary set and
X
be a Hilbert space of complex valued functions defined on
A
with the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{X}.
Then a complex valued function
K
defined on
A\times A
is called a reproducing kernel of X if it satisfies the following condition:

For any fixed
q\in A,
we have
K(\cdot,q)
is in
X
and
f(q)=\langle f(\cdot),K(\cdot,q)\rangle_{X}
for all
f\in X
.

2
Quaternion Algebra and some important notions

The field of real and complex numbers are respectively denoted by
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{C}.
Let

\mathbb{H}=\{r=r_{0}+ir_{1}+jr_{2}+kr_{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}r_{0},r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\in\mathbb{R}\},

where
i,j
and
k
are the imaginary units such that they satisfy the following Hamilton’s multiplication rule

ij=k=-ji,\penalty\ jk=i=-kj,\penalty\ ki=j=-ik,\penalty\ i^{2}=j^{2}=k^{2}=1.

For a quaternion
r=r_{0}+ir_{1}+jr_{2}+kr_{3},
we call
r_{0}
the real scalar part of
r,
and denote it by
Sc(r).
The scalar part satisfies the following cyclic multiplication symmetry

\displaystyle Sc(pqr)=Sc(qrp)=Sc(rpq),\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ p,q,r\in\mathbb{H}.

(1)

We denote the quaternion conjugate of
r
as
\bar{r}
and is defined as

\bar{r}=r_{0}-ir_{1}-jr_{2}-kr_{3}.

The quaternion conjugate satisfy the following

\displaystyle\overline{qr}=\bar{r}\bar{q},\penalty\ \overline{q+r}=\bar{q}+\bar{r},\penalty\ \bar{\bar{q}}=q,\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ q,r\in\mathbb{H}.

(2)

The modulus of
r\in\mathbb{H}
is defined as

\displaystyle|r|=\sqrt{r\bar{r}}=\left(\sum_{l=0}^{3}r_{l}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(3)

and it satisfies
|qr|=|q||r|,\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ q,r\in\mathbb{H}.

Each quaternion can be split by

q=q_{+}+q_{-},\penalty\ q_{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(q\pm iqj).

By the real components
r_{0},r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\in\mathbb{R},
we have

\displaystyle r_{\pm}=\left\{r_{0}\pm r_{3}+i(r_{1}\mp r_{2})\right\}\frac{1\pm k}{2}=\frac{1\pm k}{2}\left\{r_{0}\pm r_{3}+j(r_{1}\mp r_{2})\right\},

(4)

where
q_{\pm}
is orthogonal in in the sense that
[r_{+}\overline{r_{-}}]_{0}=0.
It is to be noted that
r_{+}
and
r_{-}
lies in the planes spanned by
\{i-j,1+ij\}
and
\{i+j,1-ij\}
respectively. These planes are orthogonal which spans the whole quaternion. This leads to the following modular identity
[
98
]

\displaystyle|r|^{2}=|r_{+}|^{2}+|r_{-}|^{2}.

(5)

A quaternion valued function
h
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be written as

\displaystyle h(\boldsymbol{x})=h_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})+ih_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})+jh_{2}(\boldsymbol{x})+kh_{3}(\boldsymbol{x}),\penalty\ \boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

where
h_{0},h_{1},h_{2}
and
h_{3}
are real valued function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
If
1\leq q<\infty,
then the
L^{q}-
norm of
h
is defined by

\displaystyle\|h\|_{L^{q}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|h(\boldsymbol{x})|^{q}d\boldsymbol{x}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{3}|h_{l}(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}d\boldsymbol{x}\right\}^{\frac{1}{q}}

(6)

and
L^{q}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a Banach space of all measurable quaternion valued functions
f
having finite
L^{q}-
norm.

L^{\infty}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the set of all essentially bounded quaternion valued measurable functions with norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\mbox{ess\penalty\ sup}_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|.

(7)

Moreover, the quaternion valued inner product

\displaystyle(f,g)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{g(\boldsymbol{x})}d\boldsymbol{x},

(8)

with symmetric real scalar part

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[(f,g)+(g,f)]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}Sc\left[f(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{g(\boldsymbol{x})}\right]d\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=Sc\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{g(\boldsymbol{x})}d\boldsymbol{x}\right)

(9)

turns
L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to a Hilbert space, where the norm in equation (
2
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\sqrt{\langle f,f\rangle}=\sqrt{(f,f)}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(10)

Definition 2.1
.

A function
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
is said to be
\epsilon-
concentrated on a measurable set
T\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d},
if

\left(\int_{T^{c}}|f(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}_{1}d\boldsymbol{x}_{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\epsilon\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

If
0\leq\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{2},
then most of the energy is concentrated on
T
and
T
is indeed and essential support of
f.
If
\epsilon=0,
then
T
is the exact support of
f.

3
Fractional Fourier Transform (FrFT)

For a suitable complex valued function
f
of real variable we define the FT by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}f)(\xi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)e^{-it\xi}dt.

(11)

It is known that the operator
\mathcal{F}
is a homeomorphism on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
and has
\{e^{-in\frac{\pi}{2}}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
as the eigen values with corresponding eigen functions
\{e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{2}}H_{n}(t)\}_{n=0}^{\infty},
where
H_{n}
is the Hermite polynomial of degree
n,
i.e.,

H_{n}(t)=(-1)^{n}e^{t^{2}}\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}e^{-t^{2}},\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{N}.

Renaming the operator
\mathcal{F}
in equation (
11
) as
\mathcal{F}_{\frac{\pi}{2}},
we see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\frac{\pi}{2}}\left[e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{2}}H_{n}(t)\right](\xi)=e^{-in\frac{\pi}{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\xi^{2}}H_{n}(\xi).

(12)

Namias (
[
141
]
), considered the operator
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha},
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\left[e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{2}}H_{n}(t)\right](\xi)=e^{-in\alpha}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\xi^{2}}H_{n}(\xi),\penalty\ \alpha\in\mathbb{C}

(13)

obtained by replacing
\frac{\pi}{2}
by
\alpha
in the eigen value equation (
12
).
Any function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
can be written as

\displaystyle f(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}}H_{n}(t),

(14)

where
a_{n}
is obtained, using the orthogonal property of the Hermite polynomials, as

a_{n}=\frac{1}{2^{n}n!\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}}H_{n}(t)f(t)dt.

Taking
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
on both sided of equation (
14
) and using equation (
13
), results in

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\xi)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}e^{-in\frac{\pi}{2}}e^{-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}}H_{n}(\xi)

(15)

The calculation of
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
using (
15
) is usually not practical. Namias
[
141
]
obtained the following integral representation of the
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
using the Mehler formula of the integral representation of Hermite polynomials

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\xi)=\frac{e^{i\left(\frac{\pi}{4}-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}}{\sqrt{2\pi\sin{\alpha}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}i\xi^{2}\cot{\alpha}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\frac{it\xi}{\sin{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{2}it^{2}\cot{\alpha}}f(t)dt

(16)

and called it the fractional Fourier transform (FrFT). McBride et al.
[
134
]
pointed out that the
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
in (
15
) and
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
in (
16
) are not the same for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{C},
because the former has period
2\pi
while the later has period
4\pi.
Thus equation (
16
) is not, after all, an integral representation of the original
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}.
Also problem arises when
\sin\alpha
becomes zero or negative. The right hand side of (
16
) makes sense only when
\alpha\neq n\pi,\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{Z},
and we need to specify which branch of
(\sin\alpha)^{\frac{1}{2}}
is used when
\sin\alpha<0.
So they considered a new family of FrFT
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha},\penalty\ \alpha\in\mathbb{R},
given by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\xi)=\frac{e^{i(\frac{\pi}{4}\tilde{\alpha}-\frac{\alpha}{2})}}{\sqrt{2\pi|\sin\alpha|}}e^{-\frac{i\xi^{2}}{2}\cot\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\frac{it\xi}{\sin{\alpha}}-\frac{it^{2}}{2}\cot{\alpha}}f(t)dt,\penalty\ \tilde{\alpha}=\sgn\alpha,0<\alpha<\pi,

(17)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{0}f)(\xi)=f(\xi)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\pi}f)(\xi)=f(-\xi)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha+2\pi}f)(\xi)=(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\xi),\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ \alpha\in\mathbb{R}

and proved that the transforms
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha},\penalty\ \alpha\in\mathbb{R}
are homeomorphisms on the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}),
along with the index law

\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\mathcal{F}_{\beta}f=\mathcal{F}_{\alpha+\beta}f,\penalty\ \forall\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R},f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}).

Kerr
[
112
]
study the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
theory of
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
by defining

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\xi)=\frac{e^{i(\frac{\pi}{4}\tilde{\alpha}-\frac{\alpha}{2})}}{\sqrt{2\pi|\sin\alpha|}}e^{-\frac{i\xi^{2}}{2}\cot\alpha}\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{-R}^{R}e^{\frac{it\xi}{\sin{\alpha}}-\frac{it^{2}}{2}\cot{\alpha}}f(t)dt,\penalty\ \tilde{\alpha}=sgn\alpha,0<|\alpha|<\pi

and proved that for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R},

\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
is a homeomorphism on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\mathcal{F}_{\beta}f=\mathcal{F}_{\alpha+\beta}f,\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ \alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R},\penalty\ f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{0}f=f,\penalty\ \forall f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f-f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\rightarrow 0\penalty\ \mbox{as}\penalty\ \alpha\rightarrow 0,\penalty\ f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

i.e.,
\{\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\}
is a strongly continuous unitary group of operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
Almeida in
[
7
]
gave the kernel of the FrFT in the following form

K_{\alpha}(t,\xi)=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{1-i\cot}{2\pi}}e^{\frac{i}{2}(t^{2}+\xi^{2})\cot\alpha-it\xi\csc\alpha},&\mbox{if}\penalty\ \alpha\neq m\pi\\
\delta(t-\xi),&\mbox{if}\penalty\ \alpha=2m\pi\\
\delta(t+\xi),&\mbox{if}\penalty\ \alpha=(2m+1)\pi,m\in\mathbb{Z}.\end{cases}

(18)

He obtained some important properties of the kernel like
K_{\alpha}(t,\xi)=K_{\alpha}(\xi,t),

K_{-\alpha}(t,\xi)=\overline{K_{\alpha}(t,\xi)},

\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{\alpha}(t,\xi^{\prime})K_{\beta}(\xi^{\prime},\xi)d\xi^{\prime}=K_{\alpha+\beta}(t,\xi)
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{\alpha}(t,\xi)K_{\alpha}(t,\xi^{\prime})dt=\delta(\xi-\xi^{\prime})
and also gave the formula for the FrFT of translation and modulation i.e.,

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(f(t-\tau))\right)(\xi)=(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\xi-\tau\cos\alpha)e^{i\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}\sin\alpha\cos\alpha-i\xi\tau\sin\alpha}

and

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(f(t)e^{i\nu t})\right)(\xi)=(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\xi-\tau\sin\alpha)e^{-i\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}\sin\alpha\cos\alpha+i\xi\nu\cos\alpha}.

Almeida
[
8
]
obtained the expression of the FrFT of the product and the convolution of two functions in the space
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{W},
where
\mathcal{W}
is the Wigner algebra consisting of functions which are the FT of functions in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}).
The expression of the FrFT of the product and convolution of
f,g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{W},
are given respectively by the following :

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(fg)\right)(\xi)=\frac{|\csc\alpha|}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{i\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}\cot\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f\right)(\nu)\left(\mathcal{F}_{\frac{\pi}{2}}g\right)[(\xi-\nu)\csc\alpha]e^{-i\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\cot\alpha}d\nu

(19)

and

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(f\ast g)\right)(\xi)=|\sec\alpha|e^{-i\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}\tan\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f\right)(\nu)g[(\xi-\nu)\sec\alpha]e^{-i\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\tan\alpha}d\nu,

(20)

where the convolution
\ast
is defined by

\displaystyle(f\ast g)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)g(x-t)dt.

(21)

This can equivalently written as

\displaystyle(f\ast g)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)(\tau_{t}g)(x)dt,

(22)

where
\tau_{t},
is the time shifted operator, given as
(\tau_{t}g)(x)=g(x-t).

Several methods can be adopted to extend the integral transform to a class of generalized functions. Important among them is

•

Embedding or kernel method: The kernel of an integral transform is embedded in a suitable space of test functions. The dual of a test function space is a space of generalized functions. The integral transform of the member of this dual space is defined as the function obtained by taking the action of the member on the kernel of the transform. Precisely, If
\mathcal{A}
is a space of test function with
\mathcal{A}^{\star}
as its dual, then with the assumption that the kernel of the integral transforms
k(t,x)\in\mathcal{A},
the integral transform of
f\in\mathcal{A}^{\star}
is given by
F(x)=\langle f(t),k(t,x)\rangle.

•

Adjoint Operator method: This method involves the construction of two test function spaces
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
such that the integral transform
\mathcal{I}
maps
\mathcal{A}
onto
\mathcal{B}.
The integral transform
\mathcal{I}f
of any generalized function
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\star}
is defined as an element of
\mathcal{A}^{\star},
given by
\langle\mathcal{I}f,\phi\rangle=\langle f,\mathcal{I}\phi\rangle,\penalty\ \phi\in\mathcal{A}.

L. Schwartz first applied this method to extend the FT to tempered distribution, dual of the test function space consisting of
C^{\infty}
function of rapid decay. This method can mainly be used when the kernel of the transform is not necessarily an infinitely differential function, as in the case of the Hilbert transform.

•

Algebraic method: This method is different from the one discussed above; it uses the algebraic structure of the integral transform. This method results in the space of generalized functions, the elements of which are called Boehmians. The theory of Boehmians, introduced by J. Mikusins̀ki in
[
138
]
, as a space of generalized functions, was motivated by the concept of Boehme’s regular operators
[
43
]
. The algebraic construction of Boehmians is similar to the construction of the field of quotients. The main difference is that Boehmians can be constructed even if the ring has zero divisors. If the construction is applied to a function space and the multiplication is interpreted as convolution, the construction yields a space of generalized functions. These spaces provide a natural setting for the extension of the transform like FT, Radon transform, Zak transform, etc.
[
139
]
.

For other methods like sequence or limit method and indirect method (where the considered transform is expressed in terms of another integral transform which is defined in some space of generalized functions), we refer the reader to
[
45
]
,
[
220
]
. Zayed
[
103
]
extended the FrFT to different spaces of generalized function. Similar to the method adopted by Zemanian to extend the Laplace transform to generalized functions, Zayed used the embedding method to extend the FrFT to the generalized function with compact support. He also used the algebraic method to construct suitable space of Boehmians. For that, he introduced a new convolution structure
[
103
]
,
[
218
]

\displaystyle\left(f\star g\right)(x)=\frac{c(\alpha)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-ia(\alpha)x^{2}}\left(\tilde{f}\ast\tilde{g}\right)(x),

(23)

where
\ast
is the classical convolution (
21
),
\tilde{h}(x)=h(x)e^{ia(\alpha)x^{2}},

a(\alpha)=\frac{\cot\alpha}{2}
and
c(\alpha)=\sqrt{1-i\cot\alpha}

for the FrFT, as the FrFT does not behave very nicely under the convolution (
21
). The FrFT of the convolution
\star
is given by

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(f\star g)\right)(\xi)=\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f\right)(\xi)\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}g\right)(\xi)e^{-ia(\alpha)\xi^{2}}.

(24)

This equation (
24
) finds its application in filter design. Like, if we are interested only in the frequency spectrum of the FrFT of
f
in the region
[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}],
we choose g (the filter impulse response) in such a way that
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}g
is constant over
[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}],
and zero or rapidly decays outside that reason. Thus we have the spectrum of
f
over
[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}],
passing output of the filter through the chirp multiplier,
e^{ia(\alpha)\xi^{2}}.
This is easier as compared to the one suggested in
[
8
]
. Taking advantage of the newly defined convolution (
23
) and the associated convolution formula (
24
), he extended the definition of the FrFT to that of the Boehmians. Zayed in
[
217
]
obtained the relation between the FrFT and the FT, which is given by

\displaystyle e^{-ia(\alpha)\xi^{2}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f\right)(\xi)=c(\alpha)\left(\mathcal{F}_{\frac{\pi}{2}}\tilde{f}\right)(2a(\alpha)b(\alpha)\xi),\penalty\ \tilde{f}(t)=e^{iat^{2}}f(t).

(25)

Based on relation (
25
), Zayed obtained the sampling theorem that can be used to reconstruct the signal or its FrFT from their samples at a discrete set of points satisfying the Nyquist rate and the inversion formula for the FrFT from the corresponding results in Fourier analysis
[
219
]
by suitable change of variable. The proof obtained is easier as compared to the one obtained by Xia in
[
211
]
. Xia
[
211
]
studied the properties of bandlimited and time-limited signals and generalized the fact that a non-zero function cannot be both time-limited as well as bandlimited by showing that a non-zero function
f
cannot be bandlimited simultaneously with FrFT
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
for a certain value of
\alpha,
and FrFT
\mathcal{F}_{\beta},
with
\beta\neq\pm\alpha+m\pi,\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{Z}.
Mendlovic et al. in
[
135
]
gave a definition of fractional convolution of functions
f
and
g
in terms of functions in fractional Fourier domain as

\displaystyle(f\mbox{{\char 69\relax}}_{\alpha}g)(t)=\mathcal{F}_{-\alpha}\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f(\xi)\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}g(\xi)\right)(t),

(26)

followed by the definition of the fractional correlation defined as

\displaystyle CORR^{\alpha}(f,g)=(f\mbox{{\char 69\relax}}_{\alpha}\bar{g}).

(27)

Equation (
26
) can be expressed in terms of integral as

\displaystyle(f\mbox{{\char 69\relax}}_{\alpha}g)(t)=\frac{|\csc\alpha|}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\frac{t^{\prime}2+u^{2}+\tau^{2}-t^{2}}{2}\cot\alpha}e^{-iu(t-\tau-t^{\prime})\csc\alpha}f(t^{\prime})h(\tau)dt^{\prime}dud\tau,

which is, unfortunately, complicated to reduce into single integral as in the case of ordinary FT. Ozaktas et al.
[
143
]
introduced an alternative fractional convolution as

\displaystyle(f\mbox{{\char 72\relax}}_{\alpha}g)(t)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{F}_{-\alpha}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f\right)(\xi)\ast\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}g\right)(\xi)\right]\right)(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tau)g(t\cos\alpha-\tau)e^{i\pi t^{2}\cos\alpha\sin\alpha-i2\pi t\tau\sin\alpha}d\tau.

(28)

This definition of fractional convolution in equation (
3
) lacks the classical convolution theorem, i.e., the FT of convolution of the functions is the product of their corresponding FT. Shi et al.
[
180
]
defined a simple fractional convolution structure given by

\displaystyle(f\star_{\alpha}h)(t)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{i}{2}t^{2}\cot\alpha}\left[\left(f(\cdot)e^{\frac{i}{2}(\cdot)^{2}\cot\alpha}\right)\ast h(\cdot)\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(\tau)f(t-\tau)\mathrm{e}^{-i\tau(t-\frac{\tau}{2})\cot\alpha}d\tau,

(29)

which satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}[(f\star_{\alpha}h)](\xi)=\sqrt{2\pi}(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\xi)(\mathcal{F}h)(\xi\csc\alpha).

(30)

Based on the fractional time shift operator given by
(T_{\tau}^{\alpha}f)(t)=f(t-\tau)e^{-i\tau\left(t-\frac{\tau}{2}\right)\cot\alpha},
which generalizes the classical translation operator
(T_{\tau}f)(t)=f(t-\tau),
the fractional convolution in (
3
) can be written in the form

\displaystyle(f\star_{\alpha}h)(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(\tau)\left(T_{\tau}^{\alpha}f\right)(t)d\tau.

(31)

Shi et al.
[
183
]
gave a generalized definition of convolution associated with the FrFT ‘
\star_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
’ as

\displaystyle\left(f\star_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}h\right)(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(\tau)\left(T_{\tau}^{\beta}f\right)(t)\phi_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(t,\tau)d\tau

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{i}{2}t^{2}\cot\alpha}\left[g(\cdot)e^{\frac{i}{2}(\cdot)^{2}\cot\beta}\star h(\cdot)e^{\frac{i}{2}(\cdot)^{2}\cot\gamma}\right](t),

(32)

where
\star
denotes the classical convolution operator and

\displaystyle\phi_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(u,v)=\mathrm{e}^{i\frac{v^{2}}{2}\cot\gamma+i\frac{u^{2}}{2}(\cot\beta-\cot\alpha)}.

(33)

The generalized convolution theorem associated to (
3
) is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\left[\left(f\star_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}h\right)(t)\right](u)=\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(u)(\mathcal{F}_{\beta}f)\left(\frac{u\csc\alpha}{\csc\beta}\right)(\mathcal{F}_{\gamma}h)\left(\frac{u\csc\alpha}{\csc\gamma}\right),

(34)

where

\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(u)=\frac{A_{\alpha}}{A_{\beta}A\gamma}\mathrm{e}^{i\frac{(u)^{2}}{2}}\left(\cot\alpha-\left(\frac{\csc\alpha}{\csc\beta}\right)^{2}\cot\beta-\left(\frac{\csc\alpha}{\csc\gamma}\right)^{2}\cot\gamma\right).

The generalized convolution theorem given in (
33
) reduces to the one studied in
[
118
]
,
[
180
]
,
[
199
]
,

[
218
]
for some constraints on
\alpha,\beta
and
\gamma.

The fractional translation operator

\left(\mathrm{T}_{\tau}^{\alpha}f\right)(t)=f(t-\tau)\mathrm{e}^{-i\tau\left(t-\frac{\tau}{2}\right)\cot\alpha}

satisfies
\left(\mathrm{T}_{\tau}^{\alpha}f\right)(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}}}{{\leftrightarrow}}F_{\alpha}(u)\mathrm{e}^{-iu\tau\csc\alpha}.

Similarly, we can formulate a fractional frequency-shift operator, denoted by
\mathrm{U}_{v}^{\alpha}
, as follows:

\left(\mathrm{U}_{v}^{\alpha}F_{\alpha}\right)(u)=F_{\alpha}(u-v)\mathrm{e}^{iv\left(u-\frac{v}{2}\right)\cot\alpha}

where it is easy to verify that
f(t)\mathrm{e}^{itv\csc\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}}}{{\leftrightarrow}}\left(\mathrm{U}_{v}^{\alpha}F_{\alpha}\right)(u).

For
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\alpha+\beta\nin 2\pi\mathbb{Z},
let

\gamma=\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2},\penalty\ \gamma=\frac{\alpha-\beta}{2},\penalty\ \tilde{a}=\frac{\cot\gamma}{2},\penalty\ \tilde{b}=\frac{\cos\delta}{sin\gamma},\penalty\ d=\frac{ie^{-i\gamma}}{2\pi\sin\gamma},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\zeta}=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{b}&\tilde{c}\\
-\tilde{c}&\tilde{b}\end{pmatrix},

then the FrFT
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}
of
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is defined by Zayed
[
216
]
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f)(\boldsymbol{u})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\boldsymbol{x})K_{\alpha,\beta}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{u})d\boldsymbol{x},

(35)

where
K_{\alpha,\beta}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{u})=de^{-i[\tilde{a}(|\boldsymbol{x}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2})-\boldsymbol{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\zeta}\boldsymbol{u}]}.
This definition is not the tensor product of two one-dimensional transforms and is based on using the Hermite functions of complex variables
[
104
]
as eigen functions of the transform. Kamalakkannan et al.
[
106
]
gave the rigorous proof to extend the definition to the functions in
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in the natural way i.e.,
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f
is the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
limit of
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f_{n},
where
\{f_{n}\}
is a sequence in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
f_{n}\rightarrow f
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as
n\rightarrow\infty.
Moreover,
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}
is a unitary operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
It is easy to observe that when
\alpha=\beta
and
\alpha=\beta=\frac{\pi}{2},
then
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}
becomes the tensor product of two one-dimensional FrFT and the classical two-dimensional FT respectively. The coupled FrFT (
35
) can be written in terms of the FT as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f)(\boldsymbol{u})=2\pi dE_{\gamma}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{u})\widehat{fE_{\gamma}^{-1}}(-\boldsymbol{\zeta}\boldsymbol{u}),

(36)

where
E_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{x})=e^{i\tilde{a}|\boldsymbol{x}|^{2}}
and
E^{-1}_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{x})=e^{i\tilde{a}|\boldsymbol{x}|^{2}},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}
satisfies
\mathcal{F}_{-\alpha,-\beta}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f=f,
for all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
In particular, if
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
then
f(\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f)(\boldsymbol{u})K_{-\alpha,-\beta}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{x})d\boldsymbol{u},
a.e. (
[
216
]
). Authors in
[
106
]
also obtained the additive property
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}}(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}f)=\mathcal{F}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}}(f),\penalty\ f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
where the parameters satisfy the conditions
\alpha_{1}+\beta_{1},\alpha_{2}+\beta_{2},\alpha_{1}+\beta_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\beta_{2}\nin 2\pi\mathbb{Z}.

For
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\penalty\ p=1,2
and
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}),
the convolution associated with the coupled FrFT is given in
[
216
]
and
[
106
]
as

\displaystyle\left(f\bigotimes g\right)(\boldsymbol{x})=2\pi dE_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{x})[f(\cdot)E^{-1}_{\gamma}(\cdot)\star g(\cdot)E^{-1}_{\gamma}(\cdot)](\boldsymbol{x}),

(37)

where
\star
is the convolution given by
(f\star g)(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y})g(\boldsymbol{y})d\boldsymbol{y}
and the associated convolution theorem is given by
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}(f\bigotimes g)=E_{\gamma}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}g.
Recently, Topan et al.
[
198
]
obtained the sharp Hausdorff-Young inequality for the coupled FrFT (
36
) which is given for
f\in L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
where
\frac{1}{s}+\frac{1}{r}=1
and
1\leq r\leq 2
as
\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f\right)(\boldsymbol{u})\right|^{s}d\boldsymbol{u}\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}\leq\frac{\sin^{\frac{2}{s}}\gamma}{2\pi\sin\gamma}(c(r))^{2}\|f\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},
where
c(r)=\left(\frac{r^{\frac{1}{r}}}{s^{\frac{1}{s}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
Moving forward to the higher dimensional FrFT, Verma et al.
[
200
]
introduced the FrFT for the function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
of real order
\alpha,
using the
n-
copies of the kernel of one dimensional fractional Fourier kernel and is defined as

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\boldsymbol{t})K_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{\xi})d\boldsymbol{\xi},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(38)

where

K_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{\xi})=\begin{cases}C_{\alpha}e^{\frac{i}{2}(|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2})\cot\alpha-i\langle\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{\xi}\rangle\csc\alpha},&\mbox{if}\penalty\ \alpha\neq m\pi\\
\delta(\boldsymbol{t}-\boldsymbol{\xi}),&\mbox{if}\penalty\ \alpha=2m\pi\\
\delta(\boldsymbol{t}+\boldsymbol{\xi}),&\mbox{if}\penalty\ \alpha=(2m+1)\pi,m\in\mathbb{Z},\end{cases}

(39)

\delta
is a Dirac delta function in
n
-dimensions and
C_{\alpha}=\left(\frac{1-i\cot\alpha}{2\pi}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}.
It is to be noted that the definition (
38
) reduces to the classical FT in
n-
dimension if
\alpha=\frac{\pi}{2}.
Analogous to the classical definition of the FT the definition of the FrFT (
38
) can be interpreted as
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}f=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}f_{n}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
where
\{f_{n}\}_{n}
is a sequence in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
f_{n}\rightarrow f
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
The existence and uniqueness of the
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}f_{n}
follows from the identity
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Authors in
[
200
]
proved the Parseval’s formula
\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}f,\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
followed by the additive property
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}(\mathfrak{F}_{\beta}f)=\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha+\beta}f,\penalty\ f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Based on the convolution
\star_{\alpha}
defined by
(f\star_{\alpha}g)(\boldsymbol{t})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-\frac{i}{2}(|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2}-|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2})\cot\alpha}f(\boldsymbol{y})g(\boldsymbol{t}-\boldsymbol{y})d\boldsymbol{y},
provided the integral is defined, author obtained the convolution theorem for the transform (
38
) given as
\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}(f\star_{\alpha}g)\right)(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\frac{1}{C_{\alpha}}e^{-\frac{i}{2}|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2}\cot\alpha}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\{e^{-\frac{i}{2}|\cdot|^{2}\cot\alpha}g(\cdot)\}\right)(\boldsymbol{\xi}),
for
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\penalty\ p=1,2
and
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Kamalakkannan et al.
[
105
]
studied the properties of the FrFT defined for the function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with respect to the parameter
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\in(-\pi,\pi)^{n}
as

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\boldsymbol{t})K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{\xi})d\boldsymbol{\xi},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(40)

where
K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{\xi})=\Pi_{l=1}^{n}K_{\alpha_{l}}(t_{l},\xi_{l}),
such that

K_{\alpha_{l}}(t_{l},\xi_{l})=\begin{cases}\frac{c_{\alpha_{l}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{i\{a(\alpha_{l})[t_{l}^{2}+\xi_{l}^{2}-2b(\alpha_{l})t_{k}\xi_{k}]\}},&\mbox{if}\penalty\ \alpha\neq m\pi\\
\delta(t_{l}-\xi_{l}),&\mbox{if}\penalty\ \alpha=2m\pi\\
\delta(t_{l}+\xi_{l}),&\mbox{if}\penalty\ \alpha=(2m+1)\pi,m\in\mathbb{Z},\end{cases}

(41)

where
\boldsymbol{t}=(t_{1},t_{2},\cdot,t_{n}),

a(\alpha_{l})=\cot\alpha_{l}/2,

b(\alpha_{l})=\sec\alpha_{l},

c(\alpha_{l})=\sqrt{1-i\cot\alpha_{l}},

l=1,2,\cdots,n.
With the assumption that
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{0},
authors defined
e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}(\boldsymbol{t})=e^{i\lambda\sum_{l=1}^{n}a(\alpha_{l})t_{l}^{2}},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
based on which the definition can be rewritten as

\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\xi})=c(\boldsymbol{\alpha})e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},1}(\boldsymbol{\xi})(e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},1}f\hat{)}(\xi_{1}\csc\alpha_{1},\xi_{2}\csc\alpha_{2},\cdot,\xi_{n}\csc\alpha_{n}),

where
c(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=c(\alpha_{1})c(\alpha_{2})\cdots c(\alpha_{n})
and
(e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},1}f\hat{)}
denotes the FT of
e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},1}f.
Also the kernel
K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{\xi})
can be written in the form
K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{\xi})=\frac{c(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{(\sqrt{2\pi})^{n}}e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},1}(\boldsymbol{t})e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},1}(\boldsymbol{\xi})e^{i\sum_{l=1}^{n}a(\alpha_{l})t_{l}^{2}}.
It is to be noted that the definition (
40
) reduces to the classical FT in
n-
dimension if
\alpha_{l}=\frac{\pi}{2},\penalty\ l=1,2,\cdots,n.
Analogous to the classical definition of the FT the definition of the FrFT (
40
) can be interpreted as
\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f_{n}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
where
\{f_{n}\}_{n}
is a sequence in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
f_{n}\rightarrow f
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
The existence and uniqueness of the
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f_{n}
follows from the identity
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Authors in
[
105
]
proved the Parseval’s formula
\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f,\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
followed by the additive property
\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}f)=\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\boldsymbol{\beta}}f,\penalty\ f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
They also introduced a generalized fractional convolution for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\penalty\ p=1,2,

g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
given by

\displaystyle f\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}g=c(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda})e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},-\lambda^{2}}\left[(e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda^{2}})\star(e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda^{2}}g)\right],

(42)

where
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{0},

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}=\left(\cot^{-1}(\lambda^{2}\cot\alpha_{1}),\cot^{-1}(\lambda^{2}\cot\alpha_{2}),\cdots,\cot^{-1}(\lambda^{2}\cot\alpha_{n})\right)
and
\star
is the convolution given by
(f\star g)(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{(\sqrt{2\pi})^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y})g(\boldsymbol{y})d\boldsymbol{y}
and obtained the associated convolution theorem for the FrFT (
40
) as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}}\left(f\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}g\right)=e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},-\lambda^{2}}\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}}f\right)\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}}g\right).

(43)

It is to be noted that for
\lambda=1,\penalty\ \alpha_{l}=\frac{\pi}{2},\penalty\ l=1,2,\cdots,n
the convolution
\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}
reduces to the classical convolution and when
n=1,\penalty\ \lambda=1,
the convolution (
42
) reduces to the fractional convolution given in (
23
). Moreover, for
f,f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\penalty\ p=1,2

g,g_{1},g_{2}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}),

\gamma\in\mathbb{C}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}_{0},
the convolution
\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}
enjoys the properties
(f_{1}+f_{2})\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}g=(f_{1}\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}g)+(f_{2}\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}g),

f\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}(g_{1}\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}g_{2})=(f\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}g_{1})\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}g_{2},

\gamma(f\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}g)=(\gamma f)\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}g=f\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}(\gamma g),

f\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}g=g\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}f
and also
\tau_{\boldsymbol{t}}(f\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}g)=(\tau_{\boldsymbol{t}}f)\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}(E_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda^{2}\boldsymbol{t}}g)=(E_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda^{2}\boldsymbol{t}}f)\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{t}}g),
where
E_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda^{2}\boldsymbol{t}}(\boldsymbol{x})=e^{2i\lambda^{2}\sum_{l=1}^{n}a(\alpha_{l})t_{l}x_{l}}
and
M_{s}(f\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda s}g)=|s|^{n}\frac{c(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda})}{c(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}s)}(M_{s}f)\star_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda s}(M_{s}g),
where
(M_{s}f)(\boldsymbol{x})=f(s\boldsymbol{x}).
As a consequence of the convolution result (
43
), author also obtained the product theorem
\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}}f\star_{-\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}}g=\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}}(e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda^{2}}fg)
for
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}}g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Uncertainty Principles:
The UP in the time-frequency plane plays a vital role in signal processing. It states that for a given function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=1,
the product of its spread in the time domain and frequency domain is bounded below and is given by

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}(t-t_{0})^{2}|f(t)|^{2}dt\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\xi-\xi_{0})^{2}|(\mathcal{F}f)(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)\geq\frac{1}{4},

(44)

where
t_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t|f(t)|^{2}dt
and
\xi_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi|(\mathcal{F}f)(\xi)|^{2}d\xi.
It has been seen in the
[
11
]
,
[
142
]
that the lower bound of the product of the spread in the time domain and the FrFT domain is given by
\frac{\sin^{2}{\alpha}}{4}.
This can also be obtained using the UP for the FT and the relation between FrFT and the FT given by (
25
). Shinde et al.
[
186
]
gave the lower bound of the product of the spread of real unit energy signal
f
in two FrFT domains as

\displaystyle\Delta\xi_{\alpha,f}^{2}\Delta\xi_{\beta,f}^{2}\geq\left[\Delta t_{f}^{2}\cos\alpha\cos\beta\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{\sin\alpha\sin\beta}{4\Delta t_{f}^{2}}\right]^{2}+\frac{\sin^{2}(\alpha-\beta)}{4}\left(=L(\Delta t_{f},\alpha,\beta)\penalty\ say\right),

(45)

where
\Delta\xi_{\alpha,f}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\xi-\xi_{f,\alpha 0})^{2}|(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)|^{2}d\xi,

\Delta\xi_{\beta,f}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\xi-\xi_{f,\beta 0})^{2}|(\mathcal{F}^{\beta}f)(\xi)|^{2}d\xi
and
\Delta t_{f}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(t-t_{f,0})^{2}|f(t)|^{2}dt
with
\xi_{f,\alpha 0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi|(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(t)|^{2}dt,

\xi_{f,\beta 0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi|(\mathcal{F}^{\beta}f)(t)|^{2}dt
and
t_{f,0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t|f(t)|^{2}dt
respectively. The equality in (
45
) is achieved when

f(t)=\left(\frac{1}{\pi\sigma^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}},

where
\sigma
is an arbitrary real constant. The lower bound given in (
45
) is tighter than the one given in
[
142
]
. As it is known that the shifting and scaling, given by
(\tau_{\tau}f)(t)=f(t-\tau)
and
f_{c}(t)=c^{\frac{1}{2}}f(ct)
respectively does not change the inequality (
44
). Shinde et al.
[
186
]
also studied the effect of the shifting and scaling of the signal in the UP (
45
) and obtained that the shifting has no effect on the inequality, whereas the scaling of the signal has the following form.

\displaystyle\Delta\xi_{\alpha,f_{c}}^{2}\Delta\xi_{\beta,f_{c}}^{2}\geq\frac{c^{4}\sin^{2}\alpha\sin^{2}\beta}{\sin^{2}\theta\sin^{2}\gamma}L(\Delta t_{f_{c}},\theta,\gamma),

(46)

where
\tan\theta=c^{2}\tan\alpha
and
\tan\gamma=c^{2}\tan\beta.

Guanlei et al.
[
78
]
obtained the Shannon UP in two FrFT domains.

\displaystyle E\left\{\left|(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)\right|^{2}\right\}-E\left\{\left|(\mathcal{F}^{\beta}f)(\xi)\right|^{2}\right\}\geq\ln(\pi e|\sin(\alpha-\beta)|),

(47)

where
E(\rho(x))=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\rho(x)\ln\rho(x)dx
is the Shannon Entropy for the probability density function
\rho
. If
\alpha\rightarrow 0
and
\beta\rightarrow\frac{\pi}{2},
(
47
) reduces to the traditional case. They also obtained the Renyi UP in two FrFT domains.

\displaystyle H_{\theta}\{|(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)|^{2}\}+H_{\lambda}\{|(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)|^{2}\}\geq\frac{\ln(\frac{\theta}{\pi})}{2(\theta-1)}+\frac{\ln(\frac{\lambda}{\pi})}{2(\lambda-1)}+\ln|\sin(\alpha-\beta)|,

(48)

where
H_{\mu}(\rho(x))=\frac{1}{1-\mu}\ln\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\rho(x)|^{\mu}dx
is the Renyi Entropy
[
61
]
for the probability density function
\rho.
Letting
\mu\rightarrow 1,

H_{\mu}(\rho(x))
reduces to
E(\rho(x)).
If
\alpha\rightarrow 0
and
\beta\rightarrow\frac{\pi}{2},
(
48
) reduces to the traditional case. If
\sin(\alpha-\beta)=\pm(\frac{\theta}{\pi})^{-\frac{1}{2(\theta-1)}}(\frac{\lambda}{\pi})^{-\frac{1}{2(\lambda-1)}},
then the bound will be zero, i.e., the bound of the Renyi UP in two FrFT domains can be zero, which also provides the possible better selection in FrFT domain for the information processing.

For the function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
2\leq k<3,
Shah et al.
[
172
]
obtained the following Heisenberg-type UP for the coupled FrFT

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{x}|^{k}\left|f(\boldsymbol{x})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{k}\left|(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f)(\boldsymbol{u})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\frac{\sin^{2k}\gamma}{8\pi^{k}}\left[\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{k}{4}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{4-k}{4}\right)}\right]^{2}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

(49)

For
k=2,
equation (
49
) reduces to the classical Heisenberg UP for the coupled FrFT
obtained by Kamalakkannan et al. in
[
107
]
using the relation (
36
) and the Heisenberg UP for the FT. Moreover, for
k=2
and
\alpha=\beta
equation (
49
) reduces to the Heisenberg UP for the FrFT. Using the logarithmic UP for the FT for the function
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and the relation (
36
) authors obtained

\displaystyle 4\pi^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|\boldsymbol{u}|\left|\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f\right)(\boldsymbol{u})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|\boldsymbol{x}|\left|f(\boldsymbol{x})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\geq\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}(\left(\frac{1}{2}\right))}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)}-\ln\pi+8\pi^{2}\ln|\sin\gamma|\right)\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

(50)

called the logarithmic UP for the coupled FrFT, which reduces to the corresponding inequality for the FrFT when
\alpha=\beta.
Authors in
[
172
]
also obtained the local uncertainty inequality for the coupled FrFT as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{x}|^{2k}\left|f(\boldsymbol{x})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\geq\frac{4\pi^{2}}{C_{k}|E|^{2k}\sin^{2}\gamma}\int_{E}\left|\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f\right)(\boldsymbol{u})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u},

(51)

where
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a set of finite measure,
0<k<1
and
C_{k}
is some constant. Using the classical Amrein-Berthier-Benedicks UP
[
9
]
for the classical FT and the relation (
36
), author in
[
172
]
proved that if
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
E_{1},E_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with the finite measure satisfying
supp(f)\subset E_{1}
and
supp\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f\right)\subset E_{2}.
If
|E_{1}||E_{2}|<\infty
then
f=0.
Apart from these UPs authors in
[
172
]
proved the Donoho-Stark’s UP for the transform (
35
), using the result in the classical setting in
[
63
]
, which says that if
E_{1},E_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be any two measurable set such that a non-zero
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is
\epsilon_{E_{1}}-
concentrated on
E_{1}
and the corresponding coupled FrFT
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f
is
\epsilon_{E_{2}}-
concentrated on
E_{2},
then
|E_{1}||E_{2}|\geq\sin^{2}\gamma\left(1-\epsilon_{E_{1}}-\epsilon_{E_{2}}\right)^{2}.
They also obtained the entropy based UP for the coupled FrFT (
35
) for the function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=1
as
E\left(|f|^{2}\right)+4\pi^{2}E\left(|\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f|^{2}\right)\geq\ln(\pi e)+4\pi^{2}\ln\left(4\pi^{2}\sin^{2}\gamma\right).
For a real valued function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
satisfying
P_{l}f,\penalty\ P_{l}\hat{f}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\penalty\ l=1,2,
where
P_{l}(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{l},
Kamalakkannan et al.
[
108
]
obtained the Heisenberg UP for the the coupled FrFT (
35
), in two coupled fractional Fourier domain as

\displaystyle\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{2}_{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{2}_{\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}}\geq\bigg(\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{0}_{f}|\Delta\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{f}\bigg)\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\sin^{2}(\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 184.9429pt+\bigg[(\sin\gamma_{1}\sin\gamma_{2})\bigg(\Delta\boldsymbol{\xi}^{2}_{f}+|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{0}_{f}|^{2}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\bigg)+(\cos\gamma_{1}\cos\gamma_{2})\Delta\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{f}\bigg]^{2},

where

\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{2}_{\alpha,\beta}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,\beta,0}|^{2}\left|\bigg(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f\bigg)(\boldsymbol{u})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u},

with
\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,\beta,0}=(u_{1}^{\alpha,\beta,0},u_{2}^{\alpha,\beta,0}),

u_{l}^{\alpha,\beta,0}=\frac{1}{\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{l}\left|\bigg(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f\bigg)(\boldsymbol{u})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u},

\gamma_{l}=\frac{\alpha_{l}+\beta_{l}}{2}\penalty\ l=1,2
and

\displaystyle\Delta\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{f}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{0}_{f}|^{2}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x},\penalty\ \mbox{with}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{x}^{0}_{f}=(x^{0}_{1},x^{0}_{2}),\penalty\ x^{0}_{l}=\frac{1}{\|f\|^{2}_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x_{k}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}.

(52)

and

\displaystyle\Delta\boldsymbol{\xi}^{2}_{f}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{\xi}-\boldsymbol{\xi}^{0}_{f}|^{2}|\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi},\penalty\ \mbox{with}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\xi}^{0}_{f}=(\xi^{0}_{1},\xi^{0}_{2}),\penalty\ \xi^{0}_{l}=\frac{1}{\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\xi_{k}|\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}.

(53)

In particular,
\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{2}_{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{2}_{\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}}\geq\|f\|^{4}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\sin^{2}(\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}).
Using the relation between the FrFT (
38
) and the classical FT in
n-
dimension and adopting the method used by Guanlei
[
79
]
, authors in
[
200
]
obtained the Heisenberg’s UP in fractional Fourier domain as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2}|(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}f)(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t}\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2}|(\mathfrak{F}_{\beta}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)\geq\frac{n^{2}}{4}|\sin(\alpha-\beta)|^{2}\|f\|^{4}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

(54)

where
\alpha
and
\beta
are such that none of
\alpha,\penalty\ \beta
and
\alpha-\beta
are integral multiple of
\pi.
Moreover, with the similar assumption on
\alpha
and
\beta
they obtained the local uncertainty inequality for the transform (
38
) in two FrFT domain which says that for a given
\theta>0
there exists a constant
A_{\theta}
such that for any measurable subset
E
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

\displaystyle\int_{E}|(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\leq\begin{cases}\frac{A_{\theta}}{|\sin(\alpha-\beta)|^{2\theta}}(\lambda(E))^{\frac{2\theta}{n}}\left\||\cdot|^{\theta}(\mathfrak{F}_{\beta}f)(\cdot)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}&\mbox{if}\penalty\ 0<\theta<\frac{n}{2},\\
\frac{A_{\theta}}{|\sin(\alpha-\beta)|^{n}}\lambda(E)\|f\|^{2-\frac{n}{\theta}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\left\||\cdot|^{\theta}(\mathfrak{F}_{\beta}f)(\cdot)\right\|^{\frac{n}{\theta}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}&\mbox{if}\penalty\ \theta>\frac{n}{2}.\end{cases}

(55)

It is to be noted that for
n=1,
the Heisenberg’s UP (
54
) reduces to the one obtained by Guanlei
[
79
]
for one-dimensional FrFT. Apart from this,
[
79
]
also contains the logarithmic UP in two FrFT domains for FrFT in one dimension

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|t||(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}f)(t)|^{2}dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|\xi||(\mathfrak{F}_{\beta}f)(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\geq\ln|\sin(\alpha-\beta)|+\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)},

(56)

where
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is such that
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=1.
It follows that if
\alpha\rightarrow 0
and
\beta\rightarrow\frac{\pi}{2},
then (
56
) reduces to the logarithmic UP for the classical FT in one dimension. Using the relation between the FrFT
\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}}
given in equation (
43
) and the classical FT in
n-
dimension, Kaur et al.
[
111
]
obtained the Heisenberg’s UP for the FrFT
\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}},
defined in (
40
), as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2}|f(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t}\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2}|(\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)\geq P_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}\frac{n^{2}}{4}\|f\|^{4}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

(57)

for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
where
P_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}=\frac{|c(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda})|^{2}|\sin\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{m}}{M^{2}(\lambda)^{n+2}},

M=\mbox{max}\left\{\frac{1}{|\sin\alpha_{l}|},l=1,2,\cdots,n\right\}
and with
P_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}^{\prime}=\frac{\lambda^{2n}}{|c(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda})|^{2}|\sin\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{m}},

M=\mbox{max}\left\{\frac{1}{|\sin\alpha_{l}|},l=1,2,\cdots,n\right\}
they also obtained the logarithmic UP

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\ln|\boldsymbol{x}||f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}+P_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}^{\prime}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\ln|\boldsymbol{\xi}||(\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\geq\left(D-P_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}^{\prime}\ln(\lambda^{2}M)\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}

(58)

followed by the local uncertainty inequality, which says that for a given
\theta>0
, there exists a constant
A_{\theta}
such that for any measurable subset
E
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

\displaystyle\int_{E}|(\mathcal{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\leq\begin{cases}|c(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda})|^{2}A_{\theta}\frac{|\sin\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{m}}{(\lambda^{2})^{n}}(\lambda(E))^{\frac{2\theta}{n}}\left\||\cdot|^{\theta}f(\cdot)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}&\mbox{if}\penalty\ 0<\theta<\frac{n}{2},\\
|c(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda})|^{2}A_{\theta}\frac{|\sin\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{m}}{(\lambda^{2})^{n}}\lambda(E)\|f\|^{2-\frac{n}{\theta}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\left\||\cdot|^{\theta}f(\cdot)\right\|^{\frac{n}{\theta}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}&\mbox{if}\penalty\ \theta>\frac{n}{2}.\end{cases}

(59)

3.1
Fractional Fourier transform of type II

If
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
is the space of rapidly decreasing test functions, then the Lizorkin space
\Phi(\mathbb{R})
is the Fourier pre-image of the space
V(\mathbb{R})
in the space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}),
i.e.,

\Phi(\mathbb{R})=\left\{\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\hat{\phi}\in V(\mathbb{R})\right\},

where
V(\mathbb{R})
is the set of all
v\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying the conditions
\frac{d^{n}v(x)}{dx^{n}}|_{x=0}=0,\penalty\ n=0,1,2,\cdots.
It is to be noted that the space
\Phi(\mathbb{R})
is invariant with respect to the fractional integration and differentiation operators, which is not the case for the whole space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}).
Luchko et al.
[
132
]
introduced a new definition of the FrFT of real order
\alpha(0<\alpha\leq 1)
for a function
u\in\Phi(\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle(\mathit{F}_{\alpha}u)(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(t)e_{\alpha}(\omega,t)dt,\penalty\ \omega\in\mathbb{R},

(60)

where

\displaystyle e_{\alpha}(\omega,t)=e^{i\sgn(\omega)|\omega|^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}t}=\begin{cases}e^{-i|\omega|t},&\omega\leq 0,\\
e^{i|\omega|t},&\omega\geq 0.\end{cases}

It is to be noted that for
\alpha=1,
the kernel
e_{\alpha}(\omega,t)
coincides with that of the kernel of the conventional FT, i.e., the FrFT (
60
) reduces to the classical FT when
\alpha=1.
Moreover the relation between the FrFT (
60
) and the classical FT is given by the formula

\displaystyle(\mathit{F}_{\alpha}u)(\omega)=\hat{u}(x)

(61)

where
x=\sgn(\omega)|\omega|^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}=\begin{cases}-|\omega|^{\frac{1}{\alpha}},&\omega\leq 0,\\
|\omega|^{\frac{1}{\alpha}},&\omega\geq 0.\end{cases}

Using this formula and the known properties of the FT, one can calculate the FrFT of the concrete functions and determine the inversion of the FrFT. Kilbas et al.
[
113
]
obtained the inversion formula of (
60
) as

\displaystyle(\mathit{F}^{-1}_{\alpha}u)(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i\sgn(\omega)|\omega|^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}x}|\omega|^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-1}u(\omega)d\omega,\penalty\ x\in\mathbb{R},\alpha>0,

(62)

for
u\in\Phi(\mathbb{R})
and also obtain the FrFT of the translation and dilation of
u\in\Phi(\mathbb{R})
respectively as

\displaystyle\left(\mathit{F}_{\alpha}(\tau_{h}u)\right)(\omega)=e^{i\sgn(\omega)|\omega|^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}h}(\mathit{F}_{\alpha}u)(\omega)

(63)

and

\displaystyle\left(\mathit{F}_{\alpha}(u(\lambda t))\right)(\omega)=\frac{1}{\lambda}(\mathit{F}_{\alpha}u)\left(\frac{\omega}{\lambda^{\alpha}}\right),\penalty\ \lambda>0.

(64)

Apart from this authors also obtained the convolution theorem for the FrFT as

\displaystyle\left(\mathit{F}_{\alpha}(k\star u)\right)(\omega)=(\mathit{F}_{\alpha}k)(\omega)(\mathit{F}_{\alpha}u)(\omega)

(65)

followed by the formula

\displaystyle\left(\mathit{F}_{\alpha}u^{m}\right)(\omega)=\left(-i\sgn(\omega)|\omega|^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)^{m}(\mathit{F}_{\alpha}u)(\omega),

(66)

where
\star
denotes the classical convolution and
k,u\in\Phi(\mathbb{R}).
Observe that the formulas given by equations (
63
), (
64
), (
65
) and (
66
) reduces to the corresponding formulas in the case of classical FT when
\alpha=1.
We know that the conditions of their validity can be improved, for example the convolution theorem for the classical FT is valid for either
k\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}),
or
k\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
or
k\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
Thus, using the relation (
61
), authors in
[
113
]
mentioned that the condition of validity of the formulas given in equations (
63
), (
64
), (
65
) and (
66
) can be improved as in the case of FT. Srivastava et al.
[
190
]
proved that the transform (
60
) and its inverse is an isomorphism from
S(\mathbb{R})
onto itself. Srivastava et al.
[
188
]
studied the FrFT given by (
60
) for the functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and obtained the Parseval formula for
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{g(t)}dt=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\omega|^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-1}(\mathit{F}_{\alpha}f)(\omega)\overline{(\mathit{F}_{\alpha}g)(\omega)}d\omega.

4
Linear Canonical Transform (LCT)

Moshinsky and Quesne proposed linear canonical transform (LCT)
[
140
]
in the early 1990s, which is a promising linear integral transform with three free parameters. It is a generalization of several integral transforms like FT, FrFT, Fresnel transform, and the scaling operations. For a real, unimodular matrix
M=(A,B,C,D),
the LCT of a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is defined by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}^{M}f)(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\mathcal{K}_{M}(t,\xi)dt,

(67)

where the kernel
\mathcal{K}_{M}(t,\xi)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{M}(t,\xi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi iB}}e^{\frac{i(At^{2}-2t\xi+D\xi^{2})}{2B}},\penalty\ B\neq 0.

(68)

It can be seen from (
67
) and (
68
), that for special choices of
M
, (
67
) reduces to several known integral transforms like

1.

for
M=(0,1,-1,0),
definition (
67
) reduces to classical FT

2.

for
M=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta,-\sin\theta,\cos\theta),\theta\neq n\pi,\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{Z}
definition (
67
) reduces to FrFT

3.

for
M=(1,B,0,1),\penalty\ B\neq 0
definition (
67
) reduces to classical Fresnel transform.

Determining a convolution theorem by defining a suitable convolution associated with a given transform has become an active area of research. Different transform requires different definitions of convolutions to obtain the associated convolution theorem. Like in the case of FT, it gives the powerful result that the convolution
``\star"
, in (
21
), of two signals in the time domain results in simple multiplication of their FTs in the FT domain. However, the LCT of
(f\star g)(t)
is given by
[
62
]

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}(f*g)\right)(u)=\left|\frac{1}{A}\right|e^{-i\frac{C}{2A}u^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}f\right)(v)e^{-\char 16\relax\frac{C}{2A}v^{2}}g\left(\frac{u-v}{A}\right)dv.

(69)

This formula lacks the requirement that the convolution in the time domain is the product of LCTs in the LCT domain. Moreover, it is inconvenient for the analysis of multiplicative filters in the LCT domain. Deng et al.
[
62
]
also gave another convolution theorem for the LCT

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}(f\star_{M}g)\right)(u)=\sqrt{i2\pi B}\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}f\right)(u)\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}g\right)(u)e^{-i\frac{D}{2B}u^{2}}

(70)

based on the modified convolution operation given by

\displaystyle\left(f\star_{M}g\right)(t)

\displaystyle=e^{-i\frac{A}{2B}t^{2}}\left(\left(f(\cdot)e^{i\frac{A}{2B}(\cdot)^{2}}\right)*\left(g(\cdot)e^{i\frac{A}{2B}(\cdot)^{2}}\right)\right)(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)g(t-y)e^{iy(y-t)\frac{A}{B}}dy

(71)

Even though the convolution theorem for the LCT given in (
70
) satisfies our need for getting the product of LCTs in the LCT domain, during the process of evaluation of the convolution (
4
), the signal are multiplied three times by the different chirp signals. From realization point of view, this may create difficulty as in communication system, it is nearly impossible to generate a chirp signal accurately. Wei et al.
[
206
]
generalized the convolution theorem for the LCT as

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}\left(f\stackrel{{\scriptstyle M}}{{\Theta}}g\right)\right)(u)=\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}f\right)(u)\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}g\right)(u)

(72)

where
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle M}}{{\Theta}}
denotes generalized convolution
\left(f\stackrel{{\scriptstyle M}}{{\Theta}}g\right)(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tau)g(t\theta\tau)d\tau
in the LCT domain, where
g(t\theta\tau)
is the
\tau-
generalized translation of signal
g(t)
by
\tau
. This convolution theorem has the needed form that the convolution in the time domain is equal to the product of the LCTs in the LCT domain, as in the case of the classical result for the FT. However, it is complicated to reduce the expression of the generalized convolution to a single integral form as in ordinary convolution (
21
). So, it is difficult to implement it compared to the FT. For the function
f
with
\tilde{f}(t)=e^{i\frac{At^{2}}{2B}},
Wei et al.
[
205
]
proposed a new convolution for the LCT of
f
and
g
is defined as

\displaystyle(f\star^{\prime}_{M}g)(t)=e^{-i\frac{At^{2}}{2B}}\left(\tilde{f}\star g\right)(t)

(73)

and obtain the following convolution theorem

\displaystyle\left(L_{M}(f\star^{\prime}_{M}g)\right)(u)=\left(L_{M}f\right)(u)(\mathcal{F}g)\left(\frac{u}{B}\right),

(74)

where
(\mathcal{F}g)(u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t)e^{-itu}dt.
Thus, the LCT of the convolution
\star^{\prime}
given by (
73
) is the multiplication of the LCT of the one with the FT of the other. Moreover, the convolution can be expressed by one-dimensional integrals, as in the ordinary convolution expression, which is useful and easy to implement, particularly in filter designing.

The classical convolution has translational invariance property, i.e.,

\tau_{z}(f\star g)=(\tau_{z}f)\star g=g\star(\tau_{z}g),

where
\star
denotes the convolution given by (
21
) and
(\tau_{z}f)(t)=f(t-z).
So many convolution structures are defined for LCT in the literature, as discussed. Wei et al.
[
204
]
pointed out that the convolution structure
\star_{M}
given by (
4
) is not translational invariance with respect to the translation operator
\tau_{z},
so they generalized the notion of translation and called it as linear canonical translation which is as follows:

For the LCT parameter
M=(A,B,C,D)
and value
z
,

T_{M;z}f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(f\star_{M}\delta)e^{-i\frac{Az^{2}}{2B}},
which can be equivalently written as

(T_{M;z}f)(t)=f(t-z)e^{-i\frac{z(t-\frac{z}{2})A}{B}}.

For
M=(0,1,-1,0)

T_{M,z}=\tau_{z}
(which is the usual translation by
z
).
With this linear canonical translation operator, they obtained the translation invariant property for LCT i.e.,

T_{M;z}(f\star_{M}g)=(T_{M;z}f)\star_{M}g=f\star_{M}(T_{M;z}g)

Motivated by the definition (
22
) of classical convolution, Shi et al.
[
181
]
introduced a generalized canonical convolution operator,
\star_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}},
for
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
in the time domain as

\displaystyle\left(f\star_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}h\right)(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}h(\tau)\left(\mathrm{T}_{\tau}^{M_{1}}f\right)(t)\phi_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}(t,\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=e^{-i\frac{A_{3}}{2B_{3}}t^{2}}\left[f(\cdot)e^{i\frac{A_{1}}{2B_{1}}(\cdot)^{2}}\star g(\cdot)e^{i\frac{A_{2}}{2B_{2}}(\cdot)^{2}}\right](t)

(75)

where
\left(\mathrm{T}_{\tau}^{M_{1}}f\right)(t)=f(t-\tau)e^{-\frac{iA_{1}}{B_{1}}\tau(t-\frac{\tau}{2})}
and

\phi_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}(u,v)=\mathrm{e}^{i\frac{A_{2}}{2B_{2}}v^{2}+i\left(\frac{A_{1}}{2B_{1}}-\frac{A_{3}}{2B_{3}}\right)u^{2}}

is a domain-independent weighted function. It is to be noted that the convolution
\star_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}
given by equation (
4
) reduces to the convolution operator
\star_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
(
33
), for the choice of the matrices
M_{1}=(\cos\beta,\sin\beta;-\sin\beta,\cos\beta),

M_{2}=(\cos\gamma,\sin\gamma;-\sin\gamma,\cos\gamma)
and
M_{3}=(\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha;-\sin\alpha,\cos\alpha)
. They also obtained the convolution theorem associated with the generalized convolution (
4
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{M_{3}}\left[f\star_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}h\right](u)=\epsilon_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}(u)\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{1}}f\right)\left(\frac{uB_{1}}{B_{3}}\right)\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{1}}h\right)\left(\frac{uB_{2}}{B_{3}}\right)

(76)

where
\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{1}}f\right)(u)
and
\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{2}}h\right)(u)
respectively denote the LCT with parameter
M_{1}
of
f(t)
and the LCT with parameter
M_{2}
of
h(t)
and

\epsilon_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}(u)=\frac{M_{B_{3}}}{M_{B_{1}}M_{B_{2}}}\mathrm{e}^{iu^{2}\left(\frac{D_{3}}{2B_{3}}-\frac{D_{1}}{2B_{1}}\left(\frac{B_{1}}{B_{3}}\right)^{2}-\frac{D_{2}}{2B_{2}}\left(\frac{B_{2}}{B_{3}}\right)^{2}\right)},\penalty\ M_{B_{l}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{i2\pi B_{l}}},\penalty\ l=1,2,3,

is a domain-independent weighted function. The convolution result for the LCT given in (
76
) generalized several convolution theorems for particular choices of the matrix parameter
M_{1},\penalty\ M_{2}
and
M_{3}

[
62
]
,
[
118
]
,
[
180
]
,
[
199
]
,
[
204
]
,
[
218
]
.

The sampling process is central in almost all domains of mathematics. One of the most fundamental concepts in digital signal processing is the sampling process, which serves as a link between a continuous and discrete signal. Before we proceed with our discussion on the sampling theorem, we need to know that a signal is said to be time-limited if it is compactly supported in the time domain, whereas it is band-limited if it is compactly supported in the transformed domain. We have interesting results concerning the support of the signal in the LCT domain. It says that if a nonzero signal
f(t)
is band-limited in the LCT domain with parameter

M_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{1}&B_{1}\\
C_{1}&D_{1}\end{array}\right)
,
then
f(t)
cannot be band-limited with another parameter

M_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{2}&B_{2}\\
C_{2}&D_{2}\end{array}\right)
,
satisfying the relationship
A_{1}/B_{1}\neq A_{2}/B_{2}
. By virtue of this, it follows that if a signal is non-bandlimited in the Fourier domain, then it can be band-limited in the LCT domain. Healy et al.
[
94
]
presented non-band-limited signals in the Fourier domain that are band-limited in the LCT domain. So, it is important to establish the sampling theorem for the band-limited function in the LCT domain. Stern
[
192
]
obtained the sampling theorem for the LCT of the compactly supported functions in the time domain or the LCT domain. Even though the formulae given in
[
192
]
can be used to reconstruct the original signal or its LCT from their samples at a discrete set of points that satisfies the Nyquist rate of that particular domain, it is not strictly in an interpolation form. Keeping this in mind Li et al.
[
123
]
developed a sampling theorem for
f(t)
band-limited to
\Omega_{M}
in the LCT domain with parameter

M=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}A&B\\
C&D\end{array}\right)
and
B>0
as

\displaystyle f(t)=e^{-i\frac{A}{2B}t^{2}}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{+\infty}f(nT)e^{i\frac{A}{2B}(nT)^{2}}\frac{\sin\left[\frac{\Omega_{M}(t-nT)}{B\pi}\right]}{\frac{\Omega_{M}(t-nT)}{B\pi}}

(77)

where
T
is the sampling period and satisfies
T=\pi B/\Omega_{M}
; and the Nyquist rate of sampling theorem associated with the LCT is
f_{s}=\Omega_{M}/\pi B
. The result (
77
), which is derived using the relation between the LCT and the FT, is more explicit than that given in
[
192
]
, and its derivation is easier than that in
[
62
]
. It also generalizes the sampling theorem for the FrFT studied in
[
211
]
,
[
217
]
. From an application point of view, when sampling non-stationary signals or broadband signals, the sampling condition in (
77
) may not always hold because of the requirement of the low sampling rate. So, Li et al.
[
123
]
also developed the following sampling formulae that are used to reconstruct the signal from the samples of the signal and its first derivatives or its generalized Hilbert transform
[
122
]
associated with the LCT each at half of the Nyquist rate.

1.

If
f(t)
is band-limited to
\Omega_{M}
in LCT sense and has a continuous first derivative. Then the following sampling formula for
f(t)
holds

\displaystyle f(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-i\left(\frac{A}{2B}\right)t^{2}}\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{i\left(\frac{A}{2B}\right)\left(kT_{1}\right)^{2}}\left\{\left[1+i\left(kT_{1}\right)\left(t-kT_{1}\right)\frac{A}{B}\right]f\left(kT_{1}\right)+\left(t-kT_{1}\right)f^{\prime}\left(kT_{1}\right)\right\}\left[\operatorname{sinc}\left(\frac{t}{T_{1}}-k\right)\right]^{2}

(78)

where
T_{1}=2T=2\pi B/\Omega_{M},f^{\prime}\left(kT_{1}\right)=\left.f^{\prime}(t)\right|_{t=kT_{1}}.

2.

If a signal
f(t)
is band-limited to
\Omega_{M}
in the LCT sense. Then
f(t)
can be represented by the samples of
f(t)
and its generalized Hilbert transform
H_{M}[f(t)]
at half Nyquist rate as follows:

\displaystyle f(t)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-i\left(\frac{A}{2B}\right)t^{2}}\sum_{k=-\infty}^{+\infty}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{i\left(\frac{A}{2B}\right)\left(kT_{2}\right)^{2}}f\left(kT_{2}\right)\cos\left[\frac{\pi}{T_{2}}\left(t-kT_{2}\right)\right]-\mathrm{e}^{i\left(\frac{D}{2B}\right)\left(kT_{2}\right)^{2}}H_{\mathrm{A}}[f]\left(kT_{2}\right)\sin\left[\frac{\pi}{T_{2}}\left(t-kT_{2}\right)\right]\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 284.52756pt\times\operatorname{sinc}\left[\frac{\pi}{T_{2}}\left(t-kT_{2}\right)\right],

(79)

where
T_{2}=2T=2\pi B/\Omega_{M}
.
Equation (
2
) is an extension of the result in
[
221
]
associated with the FrFT.

Equation (
77
) establishes the relationship between the signal samples and the original signal; that is to say, we can reconstruct the original signal or the LCT of a band-limited signal in the LCT domain from the uniform sampling points of the signal, provided the sampling interval satisfies the uniform sampling conditions. However, when applying this formula in practical situations, two problems are encountered. The first is concerned with nonuniform sampling signal processing, and the other is related to the fact that only a finite number of samples are practically available. This problem is addressed in
[
195
]
.

Uncertainty Principles:
Guanlei et al.
[
80
]
extended the traditional Pitt’s inequality in two LCT domain as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u|^{-\lambda}|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{1}}f\right)(u)|^{2}du\leq\frac{R_{\lambda}}{|A_{1}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{1}|^{\lambda}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|v|^{\lambda}|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{2}}f\right)(v)|^{2}dv,

(80)

where
R_{\lambda}=\left[\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1-\lambda}{4})}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{4}\right)}\right]^{2},

0\leq\lambda<1.
Setting
S(\lambda)=|A_{1}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{1}|^{\lambda}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u|^{-\lambda}|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{1}}f\right)(u)|^{2}du-R_{\lambda}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|v|^{\lambda}|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{2}}f\right)(v)|^{2}dv
one can obtain
S^{\prime}(0+)\leq 0,
which on simplification gives
