\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|u|\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{1}}f\right)(u)\right|^{2}du+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|v|\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{2}}f\right)(v)\right|^{2}dv\geq\ln\left(|A_{1}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{1}|\right)+\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}

(81)

which is called the logarithmic UP in LCT domain. If
M_{1}=(0,-1,1,1)
and
M_{2}=(\nu,\nu-1,1,1),
where
\nu=\sqrt{-\frac{2\Gamma^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}},
then
\ln\left(|A_{1}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{1}|^{2}\right)+\frac{2\Gamma^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}=0,
i.e., the bound of (
81
) may be zero. Authors in
[
80
]
also obtained the entropy UP for the LCT as

\displaystyle E(|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{1}}f\right)(u)|^{2})+E(|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{2}}f\right)(v)|^{2})\geq\ln(\pi e|A_{1}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{1}|),

(82)

where
E(\rho(x))=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\rho(x)\ln\rho(x)dx,

\rho(x)
is the probability density function and the Heisenberg’s UP in two LCT domain as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u|^{2}|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{1}}f\right)(u)|^{2}du\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|v|^{2}|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{2}}f\right)(v)|^{2}dv\right)\geq\frac{|A_{1}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{1}|^{2}}{4},

(83)

for function
f
satisfying
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=1.
Equation (
83
), for more general centres can be found in
[
81
]
. It is to be noted that (
80
), (
82
) and (
83
) depends only on the parameters
A
and
B
but not on
C
and
D,
because from the definition of the LCT it is seen that the parameters
C
and
D
only play the role of scaling and modulation and that they have no effect on the inequality (
80
), (
82
) and (
83
). If
M_{1}=(0,-1,1,1)
and
M_{2}=(\frac{1}{\pi e},\frac{1}{\pi e}-1,1,1)
then we have
\ln(\pi e|A_{1}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{1}|)=0,
which means that the bound of the entropic UP may be zero. Moreover, if
M_{1}=(A_{1},B_{1},C_{1},D_{1})=(\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha,-\sin\alpha,\cos\alpha)
and
M_{2}=(A_{2},B_{2},C_{2},D_{2})=(\cos\beta,\sin\beta,-\sin\beta,\cos\beta)
then (
81
),(
82
) and (
83
) reduces respectively to the logarithmic UP, entropy UP and Heisenberg’s UP
[
78
]
,
[
79
]
for the FrFT and to the traditional case
[
71
]
when
M_{1}=(1,0,0,1)
and
M_{2}=(0,1,-1,0),
i.e., in the later case (
80
), (
81
), (
82
) and (
83
) reduces, respectively, to the traditional Pitt’s inequality
[
36
]
, logarithmic UP by Beckner
[
38
]
, Shannon entropy
[
96
]
and Heisenberg UP. Guanlei et al.
[
81
]
obtained the Rényi entropy-based UP in two LCT domains as

\displaystyle H_{\theta}\left\{\left|(\mathcal{L}^{M_{2}}f)(u)\right|^{2}\right\}+H_{\gamma}\left\{\left|(\mathcal{L}^{M_{1}}f)(u)\right|^{2}\right\}\geq\frac{\ln\left(\frac{\theta}{\pi}\right)}{2(\theta-1)}+\frac{\ln\left(\frac{\gamma}{\pi}\right)}{2(\gamma-1)}+\ln|A_{1}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{1}|,

(84)

using the Hausdorff-Young inequality in two LCT domain, for
1<q\leq 2
and
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1,

\displaystyle\|(\mathcal{L}^{M_{2}}f)(u)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\left(\frac{\left(\frac{q}{2\pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}}{\left(\frac{p}{2\pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{|A_{1}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{1}|}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}|A_{1}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|(\mathcal{L}^{M_{2}}f)(u)\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R})}.

(85)

For
\theta\rightarrow 1
and
\gamma\rightarrow 1
equation (
84
) reduces to (
82
). Guanlei et al.
[
82
]
obtained the UPs for the LCT with a bound sharper than that appearing in (
83
) for a complex-valued function of the form
f(t)=s(t)e^{i\phi(t)},
where
s(t)
and
\phi(t)
are real-valued and differentiable functions and
s(t)\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R})
and with the assumption that
\frac{A_{1}}{B_{1}}\neq\frac{A_{2}}{B_{2}}
as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u|^{2}\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{1}}f\right)(u)\right|^{2}du\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|v|^{2}\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{2}}f\right)(v)\right|^{2}dv\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\geq\frac{|A_{1}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{1}|^{2}}{4}+\left(A_{1}A_{2}\Delta t^{2}+B_{1}B_{2}\Delta\omega_{s}^{2}+B_{1}B_{2}K_{1}+(A_{1}B_{2}+A_{2}B_{1})K_{2}\right)^{2}

(86)

where
K_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\phi^{\prime}(t)s(t)\right]^{2}dt,

K_{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t(s(t))^{2}\phi^{\prime}(t)dt
and with
t_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t|s(t)|^{2}dt
and
\omega_{s,0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega|\hat{s}(\omega)|^{2}d\omega,

\Delta t^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(t-t_{0})^{2}|s(t)|^{2}dt
and
\Delta\omega_{s}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\omega-\omega_{0}^{2})^{2}|\hat{s}(\omega)|^{2}d\omega.
For the function
f(t)=\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}te^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}}e^{i\frac{t^{2}}{2}},
it can be shown that
\Delta t^{2}=\Delta_{s}^{2}=K_{1}=K_{2}=\frac{3}{2}.

The equality in (
4
) is obtained for the function
f(t)=k_{2}e^{-i\left[\frac{k_{1}a_{2}-a_{1}}{k_{1}b_{2}-b_{1}}\right]\frac{t^{2}}{2}},
where
k_{1},k_{2}
are arbitrary complex constants.

5
Quadratic Phase Fourier Transform (QPFT)

Recently, Castro et al.
[
50
]
developed a new integral transform called the QPFT (QPFT), which generalizes several transforms like FT, FrFT, and LCT. For the function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
or
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and given parameter
\Lambda=(A,B,C,D,E),\penalty\ B\neq 0,
the QPFT is defined by

\displaystyle(Q_{\Lambda}f)(\omega)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{K}^{i}_{\Lambda}(x,\omega)f(x)dx,

(87)

where the kernel is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{i}_{\Lambda}(x,\omega)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-i(Ax^{2}+Bx\omega+C\omega^{2}+Dx+E\omega)},

(88)

they proved the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma, inversion formula,
Plancherel’s extension theorem and also the Parseval identity given by

\langle Q_{\Lambda}f,Q_{\Lambda}g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\frac{1}{|B|}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},\penalty\ f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

denotes the usual inner product in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
given by
\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=

\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\overline{g(x)}dx
. In the special case of
f=g
, we then have

\|Q_{\Lambda}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}=\frac{1}{|B|}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

They also gave several new convolutions followed by the corresponding convolution theorem, which, in particular cases, was the convolution theorem for the FT, FrFT, and LCT. They also obtained the Young’s convolution inequalities associated with these convolutions. Shah et al.
[
174
]
proved that the transform (
87
) is linear and has the translation formula
Q_{\Lambda}\left[f(t-k)\right](\omega)=e^{-i(Ak^{2}+Bk\omega+Dk)}Q_{\Lambda}\left[e^{-2iAkt}f(t)\right](\omega),
modulation formula
Q_{\Lambda}\left[e^{i\mu t}f(t)\right](\omega)=e^{\frac{i}{B}(\mu^{2}+2\mu B\omega+E\mu B)}\left(Q_{\Lambda}f\right)\left(\omega+\frac{\mu}{B}\right)
and scaling formula

Q_{\Lambda}\left[f(\mu t)\right](\omega)=\frac{1}{\mu}e^{-iC\omega^{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\right)-iE\omega\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)}\left(Q_{\Lambda}F\right)\left(\frac{\omega}{\mu}\right),

where
F(t)=e^{i\left(At^{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\right)+Dt\left(1-\frac{1}{\mu}\right)\right)}f(t).
They also gave the following convolution theorem

\displaystyle\left(Q_{\Lambda}\left[f\otimes_{\Lambda}g\right]\right)(\omega)=e^{i\left(C\omega^{2}+E\omega\right)}\left(Q_{\Lambda}f\right)(\omega)\left(Q_{\Lambda}g\right)(\omega),

(89)

with the convolution
\otimes_{\Lambda}
given by

\displaystyle\left(f\otimes_{\Lambda}g\right)(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2x}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)g(t-x)e^{2iAx(t-x)}dx.

(90)

Kumar et al.
[
120
]
proved the continuity of the linear map
Q_{D,E}^{A,B,C}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})\longmapsto\mathcal{S}_{\Omega}(\mathbb{R}),

where
Q_{D,E}^{A,B,C}
is defined as

\displaystyle\left(Q_{D,E}^{A,B,C}f\right)(y)=\sqrt{\frac{B}{2\pi i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i(Ax^{2}+Bxy+Cy^{2}+Dx+Ey)}f(x)dx

(91)

where
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
is a Schwartz space and
\mathcal{S}_{\Omega}(\mathbb{R})
is the Schwartz-type space which contains a complex-valued
C^{\infty}
-function
\varphi
on
\mathbb{R}
, such that for every pair
(\alpha,\beta)
of non-negative integers, it satisfies

\Gamma_{\Omega}^{\alpha,\beta}(\varphi)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|x^{\alpha}\Delta_{x,a}^{\beta}\varphi(x)\right|<\infty,

where

\Delta_{x,A}=\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}-2iAx\right)

and
\Delta_{x,A}^{\beta}=\sum_{j=0}^{\beta}p_{j}^{\beta}(x)D_{x}^{\beta-j};p_{j}^{\beta}
denotes the polynomial of order
j
with
p_{0}^{\beta}(x)=1
. They also extended the definition of the QPFT to the space of all tempered distribution
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}),
given by

\left\langle Q_{D,E}^{A,B,C}f,\varphi\right\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\left\langle f,Q_{D,E}^{A,B,C}\varphi\right\rangle

for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}),
where
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
consists of all continuous linear functions
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and proved that QPFT is a continuous linear map from
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
onto itself. Prasad et al.
[
161
]
proved the convolution theorem for the QPFT as

\displaystyle\left(Q_{D,E}^{A,B,C}\left(f\star_{\Omega_{D}^{A}}\psi\right)\right)(\omega)=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi i}{B}}e^{-iC\omega^{2}-iE\omega}\left(Q_{D,E}^{A,B,C}f\right)(\omega)\left(Q_{D,E}^{A,B,C}\left(e^{-iA(\cdot)^{2}-iD(\cdot)}\psi(\cdot)\right)\right)(\omega),

(92)

for
f,\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\Omega}(\mathbb{R}),
where the convolution
\star_{\Omega_{D}^{A}}
is defined as

\displaystyle(f\star_{\Omega_{D}^{A}}\psi)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)\psi(x-y)e^{-iA(x^{2}-y^{2})-iD(x-y)}dy.

(93)

Shah et al.
[
177
]
defined a quadratic-phase convolution of
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle\left(f\star^{\prime}_{\Lambda}g\right)(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)g(z-t)e^{-2iAt(t-z)}dt

(94)

and proved that the QPFT (
87
) satisfies the formula
\left(Q_{\Lambda}(f\star^{\prime}_{\Lambda}g)\right)(\omega)=e^{i\left(C\omega^{2}+E\omega\right)}\left(Q_{\Lambda}f\right)(\omega)\left(Q_{\Lambda}g\right)(\omega).
They also defined the correlation of
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as
\left(f\circ^{\prime}_{\Lambda}g\right)(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{g(t-z)}e^{2iAz(z-t)}dt
and the corresponding correlation formula as
\left(Q_{\Lambda}(f\circ^{\prime}_{\Lambda}g)\right)(\omega)=e^{i\left(C\omega^{2}+E\omega\right)}\left(Q_{\Lambda}f\right)(\omega)\overline{\left(Q_{\Lambda}g\right)(\omega)}.
Lone et al.
[
130
]
proposed the convolutions associated with the QPFT followed by the corresponding convolution theorem. These convolution operations enjoy both the commutative and the associative properties. We mention below such convolutions along with the associated convolution theorems.

1.

For any pair of square integrable functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, the quadratic-phase convolution is denoted by
\oplus_{\Lambda}
and is defined as

\displaystyle\left(f_{1}\oplus_{\Lambda}f_{2}\right)(z)=|B|e^{iAz^{2}}\left(\check{f}_{1}(B\penalty\ \cdot)\star\check{f}_{2}(\cdot)\right)(z)e^{iDz},

(95)

where
\breve{f}(t)=f(t)e^{-iAt^{2}}
and
\star
represents the classical convolution operator.
The corresponding convolution theorem is given by

\displaystyle\left(Q_{\Lambda}[f_{1}\oplus_{\Lambda}f_{2}]\right)(\omega)=e^{i\left\{C\left(\frac{\omega}{B}\right\}^{2}+E\left(\frac{\omega}{B}\right)\right)}\left(Q_{\Lambda}F_{1}\right)\left(\frac{\omega}{B}\right)\left(Q_{\Lambda}F_{2}\right)\left(\frac{\omega}{B}\right),

(96)

where
F_{1}(t)=e^{iDt}f_{1}(t)
and
F_{2}(t)=e^{iDt}f_{2}(t).

2.

For a set of parameters
\Lambda=(A,B,C,D,E),\penalty\ B\neq 0
and any pair of functions
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, the weighted quadratic-phase convolution is denoted by
\ast_{\Lambda}
and is defined as

\displaystyle\left(f_{1}\ast_{\Lambda}f_{2}\right)(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{1}(t)f_{2}(z-t)e^{iA\left(z^{2}-t^{2}\right)}\mathrm{dt},

(97)

with the corresponding convolution theorem as

\displaystyle\left(Q_{\Lambda}\left[f_{1}\ast_{\Lambda}f_{2}\right]\right)(\omega)=e^{i\left(C\omega^{2}+E\omega\right)}\left(Q_{\Lambda}f_{1}\right)(\omega)\left(Q_{\Lambda}f_{2}\right)(\omega).

(98)

The convolution theorem in equations (
96
) and (
98
) are such that they contain an extra chirp. So, Lone et al.
[
130
]
,
[
131
]
also gave some convolution in the context of QPFT such that the corresponding convolution theorem has no chirp multiplier. In what follows, we mention such convolution followed by the associated convolution theorem

1.

For a set of parameters
\Lambda=(A,B,C,D,E)
and any pair of functions
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, the chirp-free weighted convolution associated with the QPFT is denoted by
\star_{\Lambda}
and is defined as

\displaystyle\left(f_{1}\star_{\Lambda}f_{2}\right)(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{1}(t)f_{2}(z-t)e^{iA\left(z^{2}-t^{2}\right)}\mathrm{dt},

(99)

with the corresponding convolution theorem as

\displaystyle\left(Q_{\Lambda}\left[f_{1}\star_{\Lambda}f_{2}\right]\right)(\omega)=\left(Q_{\Lambda}f_{1}\right)(\omega)\left(\mathscr{F}F_{2}\right)(B\omega),

(100)

where
\mathscr{F}\left[F_{2}\right]
represents the FT of
F_{2}(t)=e^{-iDt}f_{2}(t)
. The convolution operation given in (
99
) do not satisfy commutative nor associative properties. The convolution given below is a chirp free convolution operation which satisfy commutative as well as associative properties.

2.

For a parametric set
\Lambda=(A,B,C,D,E),B\neq 0
, the chirp-free quadratic-phase convolution of functions
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is denoted by
\odot_{\Lambda}
and is given by

\displaystyle\left(f_{1}\odot_{\Lambda}f_{2}\right)(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{1}(t)f_{2}(\sqrt{2}z-t)e^{-2iA\left(\frac{z}{\sqrt{2}}-t\right)^{2}}\mathrm{dt},

(101)

with the corresponding convolution theorem as

\displaystyle\left(Q_{\Lambda}\left[f_{1}\odot_{\Lambda}f_{2}\right]\right)(\omega)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(Q_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}}f_{1}\right)\left(\frac{\omega}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\left(Q_{\Lambda^{\prime}}f_{2}\right)\left(\frac{\omega}{\sqrt{2}}\right),\quad\Lambda^{\prime}=\left(A,B,C,\frac{D}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{E}{\sqrt{2}}\right)

(102)

Lone et al.
[
129
]
defined the discrete-time QPFT of any sequence
u(n)\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}),
denoted by
D_{\Omega}[u(n)],
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}[u(n)](\omega)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}u(n)e^{-i\left(An^{2}+Bn\omega+C\omega^{2}+Dn+E\omega\right)},

(103)

which generalized the discrete-time FrFT
[
40
]
, discrete-time FrFT
[
40
]
and discrete-time FT as
[
59
]
. The inversion formula corresponding to (
103
) is given by

\displaystyle u(n)=\int_{0}^{2\pi/B}D_{\Omega}[u(n)](\omega)\mathcal{K}_{\Omega}(n,\omega)d\omega,

(104)

where
\mathcal{K}_{\Omega}(n,\omega)=e^{-i(An^{2}+Bn\omega+C\omega^{2}+Dn+E\omega)}.

They also introduced the notion of discrete convolution and semi-discrete convolution in the context of QPFTs for
u(n),v(n)\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\phi\in L^{2}{(\mathbb{R})}
, denoted by
\oplus_{D}
and
\circledast_{S}
respectively, as

\displaystyle u(n)\oplus_{D}v(n)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}u(n)v(k-n)e^{-2\pi iAn(n-k)}

(105)

and

\displaystyle u(n)\circledast_{S}\phi(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}s(n)\phi(t-n)e^{-2\pi iAn(n-t)}.

(106)

The operation of convolution
\oplus_{D}
defined in (
105
) is commutative and associative. Moreover, the convolution theorem for (
103
) associated with (
105
) and (
106
) are given respectively by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}\left[u(n)\oplus_{D}v(n)\right](\omega)=e^{i\left(C\omega^{2}+E\omega\right)}\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}[u(n)](\omega)\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}[v(n)](\omega)

(107)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}[u(n)\circledast_{S}\phi](\omega)=e^{i\left(C\omega^{2}+E\omega\right)}\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}[u(n)](\omega)\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}[\phi](\omega).

(108)

Moreover,
\left|\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}\left[u(n)\right](\omega)\right|
is periodic with periodicity
\frac{2\pi}{B}
. Authors in
[
131
]
have introduced a pair of novel discrete and semi-discrete convolution structures in the framework of QPFT for
x(n),y(n)\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
denoted respectively by
\smallstar_{D}
and
\smallstar_{S}
and are defined by

\displaystyle x(n)\smallstar_{D}y(n)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathrm{Z}}x(n)y(k-n)e^{iA\left(k^{2}-n^{2}\right)}

(109)

and

\displaystyle x(n)\smallstar_{S}\phi(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}x(n)\phi(t-n)e^{iA(t^{2}-n^{2})}.

(110)

The operation of convolution
\smallstar_{D}
defined in (
109
) is neither commutative nor associative. Moreover, the convolution theorem associated with (
109
) and (
110
) are given respectively by

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda}\left[x(n)\smallstar_{D}y(n)\right](\omega)=\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda}[x(n)](\omega)\mathscr{F}\left[y_{\Lambda}(n)\right](B\omega),

(111)

and

\displaystyle\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{N}}\left[x(n)\smallstar_{S}\phi\right](\omega)=\mathbf{D}_{\Lambda}[x(n)](\omega)\mathscr{F}[\Phi](B\omega),

(112)

where
\mathscr{F}[y_{\Lambda}(n)]
and
\mathscr{F}[\Phi]
represent respectively the discrete-time FT of
y_{\Lambda}(n)=e^{-iDn}y(n)
and FT of
\Phi(t)=e^{-iDt}\phi(t).
Observe that the convolution results given in (
111
) and (
112
) does not contain any chirp multipliers as was not the case for the convolution result given in (
107
) and (
108
).

Shanon’s sampling theorem is one of the remarkable, profound, and elegant concepts of digital signal processing, which serves as a bridge between analog and digital signals. The theorem asserts that if a bandlimited signal has a range of
n
frequencies, it can be perfectly reconstructed by taking
2n
evenly spaced samples. If we take any additional samples, it will turn out to be redundant, while as if we take fewer samples, we will lose some quality. This is a fundamental result in the field of information theory, in particular, telecommunications and signal processing
[
59
]
. The Shanon’s sampling theorem for the QPFT serves as a unified sampling theorem for the wide class of integral transforms. Authors in
[
129
]
,
[
87
]
and
[
177
]
proved that a bandlimited function
f(t)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with bandwidth
\sigma
in the quadratic-phase Fourier domains can be reconstructed from its equally spaced samples
f(n\tau),n\in\mathbb{Z}
by the formula

\displaystyle f(t)=e^{i\left(At^{2}+Dt\right)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-i\left(A(n\tau)^{2}+D(n\tau)\right)}\frac{\sin(B\sigma(t-n\tau))}{B\sigma(t-n\tau)}f(n\tau),

(113)

where
\tau=\pi/B\sigma
is the sampling period.

A closed subspace
\mathcal{V}
of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is said to be shift-invariant if
f(t-n)\in\mathcal{V}
, whenever
f\in\mathcal{V}
. A closed shift-invariant subspace
\mathcal{V}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is said to be generated by
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
if
\mathcal{V}=\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{\phi(t-n)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. Lone et al.
[
129
]
introduced the chirp-modulated shift-invariant subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
in the quadratic-phase Fourier domains
\mathcal{V}_{\Omega}(\phi)
for any sequence
u(n)\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\phi(t)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\Omega}(\phi)=\text{ closure }\left\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}f(z)=\left(u(n)\circledast_{S}\phi(t)\right)(z)\right\},

(114)

where
\circledast_{S}
is the semi-discrete convolution given by (
106
) and established that the necessary and sufficient condition for
\left\{e^{-2iAn(n-t)}\phi(t-n)\right\}
to be a Riesz basis for
\mathcal{V}_{\Omega}(\phi)
is that there exists two positive constants
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}[\phi](\omega+n)\right|^{2}\leq\Gamma_{2},\quad\forall\omega\in[0,2\pi/B]

(115)

For any sequence
u(n)\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and a collection of functions
\phi_{\ell}(t)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),1\leqslant\ell\leqslant M
, the generalized SISs of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to a parametric set
\Lambda=(A,B,C,D,E),B>0
is denoted as
\gamma_{\Lambda}\left(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{M}\right)
and is defined by

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Lambda}\left(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{M}\right)=\text{closure}\left\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}f(t)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{M}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{l}(n)\smallstar_{S}\phi_{l}(t)\right\},

(116)

where
\smallstar_{S}
denoted the semi-discrete convolution as defined by (
110
). Let
\gamma_{\Lambda}\left(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{M}\right)\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
be the generalized SISs associated with the QPFT. Then, the family
\quad\left\{e^{An\left(t^{2}-n^{2}\right)}\phi_{\ell}(t-n)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}1\leqslant\ell\leqslant M\right\}
forms a Riesz basis for
\gamma_{\Lambda}\left(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{M}\right)
if and only if there exists a pair of constants
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}\leqslant\sum_{\ell=1}^{M}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|\mathscr{F}\left[\Phi_{\ell}\right](B\omega+n)\right|^{2}\leqslant\Gamma_{2},\quad 1\leqslant\ell\leqslant M,\forall\omega\in[0,2\pi/B]\text{, }

(117)

where
\mathscr{F}[\Phi_{\ell}]
represents the FT of
\Phi(t)=e^{-iDt}\phi_{\ell}(t).

Uncertainty Principles:
Using the relation between the QPFT and the classical FT and the Heisenberg’s and logarithmic UP for the classical FT, Shah et al.
[
174
]
obtained the Heisenberg’s UP for the QPFT as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|t-t_{0}|^{2}|f(t)|^{2}dt\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\omega-\omega_{0}|^{2}|(Q_{\Lambda}f)(\omega)|^{2}dt\right)\geq\frac{1}{4B^{2}}\|f\|^{4}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

(118)

for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and logarithmic UP as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|t||f(t)|^{2}dt+|B|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|\omega|\left|(Q_{\Lambda}f)(\omega)\right|^{2}d\omega\geq\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}-\ln\pi-\ln|B|\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(t)|^{2}dt

(119)

for
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}).
Adopting the same method, authors obtained the local UP for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
0<\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|t|^{2\alpha}|f(t)|^{2}dt\geq\frac{|B|}{C_{\alpha}|E_{0}|^{2\alpha}}\int_{E_{0}}\left|(Q_{\Lambda}f)(\omega)\right|^{2}d\omega.

(120)

Furthermore, they also obtained the Donoho-Stark’s UP for the QPFT, which says that if
E_{1},E_{2}
be any two measurable subsets of
\mathbb{R}
such that
f
is
\epsilon_{E_{1}}-
concentrated on
E_{1}
and
Q_{\Lambda}f
is
\epsilon_{E_{2}}-
concentrated on
E_{2},
then
|E_{1}||E_{2}|\geq\frac{1}{|B|}(1-\epsilon_{E_{1}}-\epsilon_{E_{2}})^{2},
where
|E_{l}|,
denote the Lebesgue measure of
E_{l},\penalty\ l=1,2.
Several other concentration-based UPs like Nazarov’s and Amrein-Berthier-Benedick’s UPs and Hardy’s and Beurling’s UPs are also established in
[
174
]
.

6
Quaternion Fourier Transform (QFT)

In classical theory of Fourier analysis, it is known that if
f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}),
then the FT
\hat{f}
given by
\hat{f}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)e^{-it\xi}dt
is well defined. Moreover, if
\hat{f}
is integrable then
f
is equivalent to a continuous function and is such that
f(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{f}(\xi)e^{it\xi}d\xi.
There is a natural way of defining the FT of function in
L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R})
using the density of
L^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}).
In fact, the FT is not just isometric but also onto from
L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R})
to
L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}).
So, there is a natural question: whether there is an analogous theory of FT for the quaternion-valued function. Because of the non-commutativity of quaternion multiplication, there are at least three different types of quaternion FT for the quaternion-valued function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{2},
namely right-sided, two-sided, and left-sided QFT, which are respectively given as

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{F}_{r}f\right)(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(x_{1},x_{2})e^{-i\omega_{1}x_{1}}e^{-j\omega_{2}x_{2}}dx_{1}dx_{2},

(121)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{F}_{s}f\right)(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-i\omega_{1}x_{1}}f(x_{1},x_{2})e^{-j\omega_{2}x_{2}}dx_{1}dx_{2}

(122)

and

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{F}_{l}f\right)(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-i\omega_{1}x_{1}}e^{-j\omega_{2}x_{2}}f(x_{1},x_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2},

(123)

where
f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
Ernst
[
69
]
and Delucs
[
60
]
first applied quaternions to FT in the late 1980s. In 1992, Ell
[
66
]
analyzed two-sided QFT and applied it to color image processing. In
[
67
]
, Ell et al. gave a detailed interpretation of the Fourier coefficient obtained from a QFT. Following this work, Hitzer
[
97
]
studied the right-sided and two-sided QFTs. Chen et al.
[
52
]
systematically developed the QFT theory for the square-integrable quaternion-valued function. One of the important results in Fourier analysis is the multiplication formula
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{f}(x)g(x)dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\omega)\hat{g}(\omega)d\omega,\penalty\ f,g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}),
which is not true for right-sided QFT. So, they introduced an auxiliary transform
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
given by
\boldsymbol{\alpha}f(x_{1},x_{2})=f_{0}(x_{1},x_{2})+if_{1}(x_{1},-x_{2})+jf_{2}(-x_{1},x_{2})+kf_{3}(-x_{1},-x_{2}),
for
f(x_{1},x_{2})=f_{0}(x_{1},x_{2})+if_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})+jf_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})+kf_{k}(x_{1},x_{2}),
and obtained the multiplication formula as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F_{r}(x_{1},x_{2})g(x_{1},x_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(x_{1},x_{2})H_{r}(x_{1},x_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2},

(124)

where
f,g\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

h=\boldsymbol{\alpha}g,\penalty\ H_{r}=\mathcal{F}_{r}h
and
F_{r}=\mathcal{F}_{r}f.
Moreover, if
g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
then
\|g\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\|h\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.
This multiplication formula has been applied to prove that the right-sided QFT is unitary on
L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
To study the theory of the two-sided QFT, authors in
[
52
]
have obtained the relation of
\mathcal{F}_{s}
with that of
\mathcal{F}_{r}
as
\mathcal{F}_{s}f=\mathcal{F}_{r}(\boldsymbol{\beta}f),
where
\boldsymbol{\beta}f(x_{1},x_{2})=f_{0}(x_{1},x_{2})+if_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})+jf_{2}(-x_{1},x_{2})+kf_{3}(-x_{1},x_{2}).

The two-sided QFT
\mathcal{F}_{Q}(f)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{2d}\mapsto\mathbb{H}
of a quaternion function
f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
is defined by
[
97
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{Q}(f)(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}e^{-2\pi i\boldsymbol{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}}f(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})e^{-2\pi j\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}}d\boldsymbol{x}d\boldsymbol{y}.

(125)

Definition (
125
) has a symmetrical representation

\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=\hat{f}_{1}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})+i\hat{f}_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})+j\hat{f}_{3}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})+i\hat{f}_{4}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})j,

where
\hat{f}_{l}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=\mathcal{F}_{Q}(f_{l})(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}),\penalty\ l=1,2,3,4.
Chen et al.
[
51
]
developed the new module and
L^{p}-
norm of
\hat{f}
given respectively as

\displaystyle|\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})|_{Q}=\left(|\hat{f_{1}}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}+|\hat{f_{2}}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}+|\hat{f_{3}}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}+|\hat{f_{4}}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(126)

and

\displaystyle\|\hat{f}\|_{Q,p}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})|^{p}_{Q}d\boldsymbol{u}d\boldsymbol{v}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

(127)

which are not equivalent to
|\hat{f}|_{Q}
and
\|\hat{f}\|_{L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}
respectively. Apart from several important properties like
\|\hat{f}\|_{Q,\infty}\leq\|f\|_{L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})},
continuity of
\hat{f}
and the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma i.e.,
\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\rightarrow 0
as
|(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})|\rightarrow\infty
for
f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d}),
using the density of
L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\cap L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
in
L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
and the property that
\mathcal{F}_{Q}(h)\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
whenever
h\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\cap L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
they also extended the definition (
125
) of QFT to the functions in
L^{2}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
and then to
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d}),
followed by the equality
\|\hat{f}\|_{Q,2}=\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})},
the inversion formula

\left(\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{Q}\right)(\boldsymbol{x})=\mathcal{F}_{Q}(-\boldsymbol{x}),
for
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d}).

The generalization of two-sided quaternion FT (
125
) by replacing
i,j
with a pair of unit quaternions
\mu,\nu,
i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mu,\nu}(f)(u,v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}e^{-2\pi\mu x\cdot u}f(x,y)e^{-2\pi\nu x\cdot u}dxdy,

(128)

which is called steerable quaternion FT, can be found in
[
99
]
. Using the splitting method of the quaternion signal, (
128
) can be written in the following complex form

\mathcal{F}_{\mu,\nu}(f)(u,v)=\mathcal{F}_{\mu,\nu}(f_{-})(u,v)+\mathcal{F}_{\mu,\nu}(f_{+})(u,v),

where

\mathcal{F}_{\mu,\nu}(f_{\pm})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{2\pi\mu(xu\mp yv)}f_{\pm}dxdy.

Recall that Bebenko
[
12
]
first proved the sharp Hausdorff Youngs inequality for the complex FT in
\mathbb{R}
with even positive number
q
and later for the general case was proved by Beckner
[
35
]
. Using the Minkowski inequality, sharp Hausdorff-Young inequality
[
35
]
for the complex FT and the modulus identities

|f(x,y)|^{2}=|f_{-}(x,y)|^{2}+|f_{+}(x,y)|^{2}

and

|\mathcal{F}_{\mu,\nu}(f)(u,v)|^{2}=|\mathcal{F}_{\mu,\nu}(f_{-})(u,v)|^{2}+|\mathcal{F}_{\mu,\nu}(f_{+})(u,v)|^{2},
which was first proved for
\mu=i,\nu=j
in
[
98
]
, Lian
[
125
]

proved the sharp Hausdorff Young inequality for the quaternion FT as

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{F}_{\mu,\nu}f\|_{L^{q}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq A_{p}^{2}\|f\|_{L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

(129)

where
A_{p}=\left(\frac{p^{\frac{1}{p}}}{q^{\frac{1}{q}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

1\leq p\leq 2,\penalty\ \frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
and
f\in L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
The constant
A_{p}^{2}
appearing in the inequality (
129
) is sharp as can be seen that choosing one of
f_{\pm}
to be zero
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,\nu}
reduces to the complex FT and
A_{p}^{2}
serves as a sharp constant in the Hausdorff-Young inequality for the complex FT
[
35
]
. Using the relation
\mathcal{F}_{l}(f)=\mathcal{F}_{s}\tilde{f}
and using the sharp inequality (
129
), Lian
[
125
]
also obtained the sharp Hausdorff Young inequality, with the same assumption on
p
and
f,
for the left-sided quaternion FT as
\|\mathcal{F}_{l}(f)\|_{L^{q}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq A_{p}^{2}\|\tilde{f}\|_{L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},
where
\tilde{f}(x,y)=f_{0}(x,y)+if_{1}(x,-y)+jf_{2}(x,y)+kf_{3}(x,-y).
The norms
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
and
\|\tilde{\cdot}\|_{L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
are equivalent but they are not in general equal. However
\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\|\tilde{f}\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Uncertainty Principles:
The UP describes a feature of a function and its quaternion FT. Beckner
[
36
]
has shown that sharp Pitt’s inequality yields a short proof of a logarithmic uncertainty estimate, and Heisenberg-Weyl’s inequality follows by using logarithmic inequality. In
[
36
]
, with the aid of rearrangement and symmetrization, Beckner has proved a sharp Pitt’s inequality by applying the sharp
L^{1}
Young’s inequality for convolution on
\mathbb{R}_{+}.
As in
[
37
]
, based on Young’s inequality for the convolution on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times S^{2d-1},
Chen et al.
[
51
]
gave a different proof of the Pitt’s inequality for QFT. They obtained the Pitt’s inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{-\alpha}|\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\leq C_{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\boldsymbol{x}|^{\alpha}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}

(130)

for
f\in S(\mathbb{R}^{2d},\mathbb{R}),\penalty\ 0\leq\alpha\leq 2d,
where
C_{\alpha}=\pi^{\alpha}\left[\Gamma\left(\frac{2d-\alpha}{4}\right)/\Gamma\left(\frac{2d+\alpha}{4}\right)\right]
. Using (
130
), (
126
) they established the Pitt’s inequality for the quaternion valued function
f\in S(\mathbb{R}^{2d},\mathbb{H})
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{-\alpha}|\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{\xi})|_{Q}^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\leq C_{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\boldsymbol{x}|^{\alpha}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}.

(131)

By differentiating the sharp Pitt’s inequality at
\alpha=0,
they also gave the logarithmic UP as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\ln|\boldsymbol{x}||f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\ln|\boldsymbol{\xi}||f(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}_{Q}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\geq D\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x},

(132)

D=\psi(\frac{2d}{4})-\ln(\pi)
and
\psi=\frac{d}{dt}[\ln\Gamma(t)].

The Heisenberg-Weyl’s UP for
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d}),
with
\|f\|^{2}_{2}=1
is

\displaystyle\Delta_{f}\boldsymbol{x}\cdot\Delta_{f}\boldsymbol{\xi}\geq\frac{d}{2\pi},

(133)

where the standard deviations
\Delta_{f}\boldsymbol{x}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0}|^{2}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\Delta_{f}\boldsymbol{\xi}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\boldsymbol{\xi}-\boldsymbol{\xi}_{0}|^{2}|f(\boldsymbol{\xi})|_{Q}^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
are smallest for the expectations
\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}x_{l}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\right)_{l=1}^{2d},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{2d})
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\xi_{l}|f(\boldsymbol{\xi})|_{Q}^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)_{l=1}^{2d},

\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots,\xi_{2d})
respectively is obtained in
[
51
]
. The inequality (
133
) is based on the interpretation of the standard deviation as the size of the essential support of
f.
With the different notion of the support gives different version of the UP like, for a non-zero function
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
which is
\epsilon_{T}-
concentrated on a measurable set
T\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and
\hat{f}
is
\epsilon_{\Omega}-
concentrated on a measurable set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d},

|T||\Omega|\geq(1-\epsilon_{t}-\epsilon_{\Omega})^{2},

which is the Donoho-Stark’s UP, for an alternative proof see
[
117
]
,
[
126
]
. It happens that if
suppf\subset T
and
supp\hat{f}\subset\Omega,
then
|T||\Omega|\geq 1.

In
[
117
]
, authors discussed the recovery of a quaternion-valued signal in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
despite a significant amount of missing information. Like, say, if a signal
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is transmitted to a receiver who knows that it is bandlimited, meaning that
f
was synthesized using only frequencies in a set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}.
Now, suppose that the receiver is unable to observe all the data of
f,
a certain subset
T
of
\boldsymbol{x}-
values is unobserved. Moreover, the observed signal is contaminated by observational noise
n(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
Thus the received signal
r(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfies

r(\boldsymbol{x})=\begin{cases}f(\boldsymbol{x})+n(\boldsymbol{x}),&\boldsymbol{x}\nin T,\\
0,&\boldsymbol{x}\in T.\end{cases}

The receiver’s aim is to reconstruct the transmitted signal
f
from the noisy received signal
r(\boldsymbol{x}).
Although it may seem that information about
f
for
\boldsymbol{x}\in T
is unavailable, the UPs say that the recovery is possible provided that
|T||\Omega|<1.

Donoho and Stark first proved this result
[
63
]
in the classical Fourier domain for the function on
\mathbb{R}.
Some generalizations of the Donoho-Stark UP for the right-sided QFT have been studied in
[
1
]
.

The following result, in
[
51
]
, is a generalization in the quaternion setting of the result due to Benedicks
[
39
]
(also see
[
71
]
) for ordinary FT whose elegant proof was first circulated as a preprint in 1974 but not formally published for another decade.

\displaystyle\mbox{If}\penalty\ f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\penalty\ \mbox{with}\penalty\ suppf\subset T\penalty\ \mbox{and}\penalty\ supp\hat{f}\subset\Omega\penalty\ \mbox{such that}\penalty\ |T||\Omega|<\infty,\penalty\ \mbox{then}\penalty\ f=0.

(134)

Note that if
f\in L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})(p>1)
and
|T|<\infty,
then
f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
by Holder’s inequality and if
f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
and
|\Omega|<\infty
then
f\in L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
for all
p>1
hence the result (
134
) applies equally to
L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
functions. The proof of the result (
134
) relies on the Poisson summation formula for the quaternion valued function, i.e., if
f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d}),
the series
\displaystyle\phi(x_{1},x_{2})=\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2d}}f(x_{1}+k_{1},x_{2}+k_{2})
converges in
L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}([0,1]^{2d}),
then the quaternion Fourier series of
\phi
is
\displaystyle\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2d}}e^{2\pi ik_{1}\cdot x_{1}}\hat{f}(k_{1},k_{2})e^{2\pi jk_{2}\cdot x_{2}}.
The local uncertainty inequalities for the classical FT, which states that if
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C})
is highly localized then its FT can be concentrated in a small neighborhood of two or more separated points, was first obtained by Faris
[
70
]
and sharper by Price
[
163
]
,
[
162
]
. Lian
[
126
]
obtained the local UP for the two-sided QFT as

\displaystyle\int_{E}|(\mathcal{F}f)(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\leq K_{\lambda}|E|^{\lambda}\||\boldsymbol{x}|^{\lambda}f\|^{2}_{2},\penalty\ 0<\lambda<1

(135)

and

\displaystyle\int_{E}|(\mathcal{F}f)(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\leq K_{\lambda}|E|\|f\|^{2-\frac{2}{\lambda}}_{2}\||\boldsymbol{x}|^{\lambda}f\|^{\frac{2}{\lambda}}_{2},\penalty\ \lambda>1

(136)

for all
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and all measurable
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2},
where

\displaystyle K_{\lambda}=\begin{cases}\frac{(1+\lambda)^{2}}{\lambda^{2\lambda}}(2-2\lambda)^{\lambda-2},&0<\lambda<1\\
\frac{\pi}{\lambda\Gamma(\frac{1}{\lambda})}\Gamma(\frac{1}{\lambda})\Gamma(1-\frac{1}{\lambda})(\lambda-1)^{\lambda}(1-\frac{1}{\lambda})^{-1},&\lambda>1.\end{cases}

(137)

Using this local UP and following the lines of
[
75
]
, one can get the global UP, which includes different powers of norm as

\displaystyle\||\boldsymbol{x}|^{s}f\|^{\frac{2\beta}{s+\beta}}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\cdot\||\boldsymbol{y}|^{\beta}\mathcal{F}f\|^{\frac{2s}{s+\beta}}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\geq C_{s,\beta}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

(138)

where
s,\beta>0,

f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
C_{s,\beta}
is some constant.

The Pitt’s inequality and the logarithmic UPs given in (
131
) and (
132
) are expressed in terms of the module given by (
126
). Moreover, it was first proved for real-valued function under two-sided QFT following Beckner’s lines with quite a lot of effort. Thus, a genuine analogues of Pitt’s inequality and the logarithmic UP for quaternion-valued function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is obtained in
[
126
]
, using the two-dimensional plane split of a quaternion and the results for the complex FT, which are same as (
131
) and (
132
) respectively with the norm
|\cdot|_{Q}
replaced as
|\cdot|,
furthermore the constant
D
in equation (
132
) is
\psi(1)-\ln(\pi).
The method adopted by Lian
[
126
]
is easier and also applicable to the one-sided QFT, general two-sided QFT or QFT on higher dimension
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Without using the sharp Hausdorff-Young’s inequality of the two-sided QFT, Lian also obtained the Entropy UP

\displaystyle H(|f|^{2})+H(|\mathcal{F}(f)|^{2})\geq 2(1-\ln 2),

(139)

for
f\in S(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{H})
and
\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=1,
where
H(f)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\boldsymbol{x})\ln f(\boldsymbol{x})d\boldsymbol{x}.

6.1
One dimensional quaternion Fourier transform

So far, we have seen that the quaternion FT was studied for the quaternion-valued function defined on
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{R}^{2d}.
The quaternion FT for the quaternion-valued function defined on
\mathbb{R}
was first defined by He et al.
[
93
]
as

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\xi)=\hat{f_{1}}(\xi)+j\hat{f_{2}}(\xi)

(140)

for
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}).
They obtained the Parseval’s formula
\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})}=\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})},
for
f,g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}),
and proved that
\widehat{(f\star g)}(\xi)\neq\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\xi)
in general, for the convolution
\star
defined by

\displaystyle(f\star g)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-x^{\prime})g(x^{\prime})dx^{\prime}.

(141)

Also if we take
f=f_{1}+jf_{2}
and
g=g_{1}+jg_{2},
then we have

\widehat{(f\star g)}(\xi)=\left[\hat{f_{1}}(\xi)\hat{g_{1}}(\xi)-\hat{\bar{f_{2}}}(\xi)\hat{g_{2}}(\xi)\right]+j\left[\hat{f_{2}}(\xi)\hat{g_{1}}(\xi)-\hat{\bar{f_{1}}}(\xi)\hat{g_{2}}(\xi)\right]

\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\xi)=\left[\hat{f_{1}}(\xi)\hat{g_{1}}(\xi)-\bar{\hat{f_{2}}}(\xi)\hat{g_{2}}(\xi)\right]+j\left[\hat{f_{2}}(\xi)\hat{g_{1}}(\xi)-\bar{\hat{f_{1}}}(\xi)\hat{g_{2}}(\xi)\right].

Thus
\widehat{(f\star g)}(\xi)=\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\xi)
if
\bar{\hat{f_{1}}}(\xi)=\hat{\bar{f_{1}}}(\xi)
and
\bar{\hat{f_{2}}}(\xi)=\hat{\bar{f_{2}}}(\xi).
Bahri et al.
[
33
]
also studied one-dimensional quaternion FT with some convolution
\star
for which
f\star g\neq g\star f
in general and also faces the same problem as that in
[
93
]
. Akila et al.
[
5
]
introduced a novel natural convolution given as

\displaystyle(f\ostar g)(x)=(f_{1}\star g_{1})(x)-(\check{\overline{f_{2}}}\star g_{2})(x)+j\left((\check{\overline{f_{1}}}\star g_{2})(x)+(f_{2}\star g_{1})(x)\right)

(142)

and proved that
f\ostar\delta\in L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}),

\alpha(f\ostar g)=(\alpha f)\ostar g,

(f+g)\ostar\delta=f\ostar\delta+g\ostar\delta
and
(f\ostar g)\ostar\epsilon=f\ostar(\delta\ostar\epsilon)
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{H},

\delta,\epsilon\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}),
and
f\in L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}),\penalty\ p=1,2.
They also proved the convolution theorem
\mathcal{F}(f\ostar g)=\mathcal{F}(f)\mathcal{F}(\delta),\penalty\ \delta\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
and
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}),
and hence
\ostar
defined in (
142
) serves as a better convolution structure for the transform defined in (
140
) than the one given in (
141
) and also in
[
33
]
. In general,
\ostar
is non-commutative, i.e.,
f\ostar g
need not be equal to
g\ostar f.
If both
f
and
g
are complex-valued or if one of them is a real-valued even function, then we get
f\ostar g=g\ostar f.
Finding a suitable non-trivial necessary and sufficient condition on
f
and
g
to get
f\star g=g\star f
is still and interesting open problem. The definition (
140
) together with the convolution structure (
142
) and its properties for the arbitrary dimension
d,
i.e., for the functions defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be found in
[
6
]
.

7
Quaternion Fractional Fourier Transform (QFrFT)

Guanlei et al.
[
77
]
proposed a definition of two-sided QFrFT. It extends the definition of classical FrFT for the complex-valued function to that of the quaternion-valued functions. This transform is a natural extension of the two-sided QFT (
122
). The idea of defining the transform is by replacing the Fourier kernel in (
122
) with that of the fractional Fourier kernel. Thus the definition of the two-sided QFrFT for the function
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
is defined by

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{F}^{ij}_{\alpha,\beta}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K^{i}_{\alpha}(x_{1},u_{1})f(x_{1},x_{2})K^{j}_{\beta}(x_{2},u_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2},

(143)

where
K^{i}_{\alpha}(x_{1},u_{1})=\sqrt{\frac{1-i\cot\alpha}{2\pi}}e^{i\left(\frac{x_{1}^{2}+u_{1}^{2}}{2\tan\alpha}-\frac{x_{1}u_{1}}{\sin\alpha}\right)}
and
K^{j}_{\beta}(x_{2},u_{2})=\sqrt{\frac{1-j\cot\alpha}{2\pi}}e^{j\left(\frac{x_{2}^{2}+u_{2}^{2}}{2\tan\alpha}-\frac{x_{2}u_{2}}{\sin\alpha}\right)}
and
\alpha,\beta\neq n\pi,\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{Z}.
In particular, the QFrFT (
143
), reduces to QFT for
\alpha=\beta=\frac{\pi}{2},
reduces to the classical
1-D
FT of
f(x_{1},x_{2})
for variable
x_{1},
when
\alpha=\frac{\pi}{2}
and
\beta=0,
reduces to the
1-D
FT of
f(x_{1},x_{2})
for variable
x_{2}.
Moreover, the transform (
143
) is such that it has the property that
\left(\mathcal{F}^{ij}_{\alpha,\beta}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})=f(x_{1},x_{2})
for
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,0)\penalty\ \mbox{or}\penalty\ (2\pi,2\pi)
and
\left(\mathcal{F}^{ij}_{\pi,\pi}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})=f(-x_{1},-x_{2}),

\left(\mathcal{F}^{ij}_{3\frac{\pi}{2},3\frac{\pi}{2}}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})=(\mathcal{F}_{s}f)(-u_{1},-u_{2}),
where
\mathcal{F}_{s}
is a two sided QFrFT given by (
122
). Authors in
[
77
]
, obtained the
\mathbb{R}-
linear property, odd-even invariant property, i.e.,
\left(\mathcal{F}^{ij}_{\alpha,\beta}f(\epsilon_{1}x_{1},\epsilon_{2}x_{2})\right)(u_{1},u_{2})=\left(\mathcal{F}^{ij}_{\alpha,\beta}f\right)(\epsilon_{1}u_{1},\epsilon_{2}u_{2}),\penalty\ \epsilon_{l}=\pm 1,\penalty\ l=1,2
and also the additivity property, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}^{ij}_{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}\mathcal{F}^{ij}_{\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}}=\mathcal{F}^{ij}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}}
of the transform (
143
). Wei et al.
[
203
]
, obtained the inversion formula for the transform (
143
) as

f(x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K^{i}_{-\alpha}(x_{1},u_{1})\left(\mathcal{F}^{ij}_{\alpha,\beta}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})K^{j}_{-\beta}(x_{2},u_{2})du_{1}du_{2},

and proved the Parseval’s formula
\langle\mathcal{F}^{ij}_{\alpha,\beta}f,\mathcal{F}^{ij}_{\alpha,\beta}g\rangle=\langle f,g\rangle,\penalty\ f,g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the scalar product (
2
). In particular, when
f=g,
it follows that

\|\mathcal{F}^{ij}_{\alpha,\beta}f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})}=\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})}.
From the signal processing point of view, the Parseval’s formula states that the signal energy is preserved by the transform (
143
). It can be seen that it is not possible to establish a general Parseval’s formula for the inner product given by (
8
) because it lacks the cyclic symmetry (
1
). Thus, to obtain such Parseval’s formula, it is therefore either necessary to modify the symmetric property of the inner product as in (
2
) or to modify the QFrFT itself. Thus, author in
[
203
]
gave the definition of right-sided QFrFT

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{Q}^{ij}_{\alpha,\beta}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(x_{1},x_{2})K^{i}_{\alpha}(x_{1},u_{1})K^{j}_{\beta}(x_{2},u_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2}.

(144)

The transform enjoys the property
\left(\mathcal{Q}^{ij}_{\alpha,\beta}f,\mathcal{Q}^{ij}_{\alpha,\beta}g\right)=\left(f,g\right),\penalty\ f,g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
according to the inner product (
8
), as a consequence of which it follows that
\|\mathcal{Q}^{ij}_{\alpha,\beta}f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})}=\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})}.
In what follows, we see the progress of the FrFT for the quaternion-valued function defined on
\mathbb{R}.

Using the idea of He et al.
[
93
]
, Roopkumar
[
170
]
gave the definition of FrFT of type II of quaternion-valued function
f=f_{1}+jf_{2}\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\xi)=(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f_{1})(\xi)+j(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f_{2})(\xi),

(145)

where
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f_{l},\penalty\ l=1,2
is the classical FrFT. The transform (
145
) is
\mathbb{H}-
linear satisfying the property
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\mathcal{F}_{\beta}f=\mathcal{F}_{\alpha+\beta}f,
for
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}).
Apart from this, the transform also satisfies the following Parseval’s identity

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f,\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}g)=(f,g),\penalty\ f,g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}),

(146)

where
(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the inner product given by (
8
). Motivated by the idea of defining the convolution of one-dimensional quaternion-valued function for the QFT given in (
142
), author in
[
170
]
defined the convolution associated with the QFrFT as

\displaystyle(f\ostar_{\alpha}g)(x)=(f_{1}\star_{\alpha}g_{1})(x)-(\mathcal{F}_{-2\alpha}\overline{f_{2}}\star_{\alpha}g_{2})(x)+j\left((\mathcal{F}_{-2\alpha}\overline{f_{1}}\star_{\alpha}g_{2})(x)+(f_{2}\star_{\alpha}g_{1})(x)\right),

(147)

where
\star_{\alpha}
is the convolution for the complex valued function given by
(f_{1}\star_{\alpha}g_{1})(x)=\frac{c_{\alpha}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-ia_{\alpha}x^{2}}(\tilde{f_{1}}\star\tilde{g_{1}})(x),
where
\tilde{f_{1}}(x)=f_{1}(x)e^{ia_{\alpha}x^{2}},

c_{\alpha}=\sqrt{1-i\cot\alpha},

a_{\alpha}=\frac{\cot\alpha}{2}
and
\star
is the classical convolution given as
(f_{1}\star g_{1})(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{1}(x-y)g(y)dy.
Also, they considered the following operation between two quaternion-valued functions as

\displaystyle(f\otimes_{\alpha}g)(x)=(f_{1}\star^{\prime}_{\alpha}g_{1})(x)-(\overline{\mathcal{F}_{-2\alpha}f_{2}}\star^{\prime}_{\alpha}g_{2})(x)+j\left((\overline{\mathcal{F}_{-2\alpha}f_{1}}\star^{\prime}_{\alpha}g_{2})(x)+(f_{2}\star^{\prime}_{\alpha}g_{1})(x)\right),

(148)

where
\star^{\prime}_{\alpha}
is the operation defined on complex valued functions as
(f_{1}\star^{\prime}_{\alpha}g_{1})(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{ia_{\alpha}x^{2}}(\tilde{\tilde{f_{1}}}\star\tilde{\tilde{g_{1}}})(x),
where
\tilde{\tilde{f_{1}}}(x)=f_{1}(x)e^{-ia_{\alpha}x^{2}}.
For the convolution (
147
) and the operation (
148
), author in
[
170
]
obtained the convolution and product theorem given respectively as
(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(f\ostar_{\alpha}g))(\xi)=(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\xi)(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}g)(\xi)e^{-ia_{\alpha}\xi^{2}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\left(\tilde{\tilde{(fg)}}\right)=c_{-\alpha}[\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f\otimes_{\alpha}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}g]
for
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}),\penalty\ g\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}).
For the convolution (
147
), with
\star_{\alpha}
for the complex valued functions given by
(f_{1}\star_{\alpha}g_{1})(x)=e^{-ia_{\alpha}x^{2}}(\tilde{f_{1}}\star g_{1})(x),
author in
[
169
]
proved that for
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
and
g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})

f\ostar_{\alpha}g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
and obtained the convolution theorem for the transform (
145
) as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(f\ostar_{\alpha}g))(\xi)=\sqrt{2\pi}(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\xi)(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}g)(\xi\csc\alpha).

(149)

They proved that the convolution
\ostar_{\alpha}
satisfies the properties analogue to those for the convolution
\ostar
given in (
142
), like
(f+h)\ostar_{\alpha}g=f\ostar_{\alpha}g+h\ostar_{\alpha}g
and
(\gamma f)\ostar_{\alpha}g=\gamma(f\ostar_{\alpha}g),
for
f,h\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}),

g\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{H}.
Motivated by He et al.
[
93
]
and Roopkumar
[
170
]
, Gupta et al.
[
89
]
extended the FrFT
[
188
]
for the complex-valued functions to the quaternion-valued function. They called the transform as the QFrFT and is defined for
f=f_{1}+jf_{2}\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
and
0<\theta\leq 1
as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}f=\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}f_{1}+j\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}f_{2},

(150)

where
(\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}g)(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t)e^{-i(\sgn\xi)|\xi|^{\frac{1}{\theta}}t}dt
for
g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R})
is the FrFT given in
[
188
]
. They proved that the transform (
150
) is
\mathbb{H}-
linear and using the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma and the Parseval’s formula for the classical case author proved the same for the quaternion case i.e.,
\lim_{|\xi|\rightarrow\infty}|(\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}f)(\xi)|=0,
for
f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)(g(t))^{c}dt=\frac{1}{2\pi\theta}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\xi|^{\frac{1}{\theta}-1}(\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}f)(\xi)((\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}g)(\xi))^{c}d\xi,
for
f,g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}).
With the convolution
\otimes
given by (
142
), the convolution theorem for the QFrFT (
150
) is given by
\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}(f\otimes g)=(\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}f)(\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}g),
for
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}),\penalty\ g\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}).
Authors in
[
89
]
also obtained the Heisenberg type UP for the QFrFT (
150
) as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x|^{2}|f(x)|^{2}dx\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\xi|^{\frac{3}{\theta}-1}|(\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}f)(\xi)|^{2}\right)\geq\frac{\pi\theta}{4}\|f\|^{4}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})},

(151)

for the function
f=f_{1}+jf_{2}
such the
f_{l},\penalty\ l=1,2
are in the Schwartz class.

8
Quaternion Linear Canonical Transform (QLCT)

The LCT has a degree of freedom and is more flexible than the FT and the FrFT, with similar computational costs. Thus, because of these advantages, the LCT has been extended to the quaternion algebra framework. This leads to the notion of the QLCT; furthermore, because of the non-commutativity of the quaternion multiplication, there are three different types of QLCTs, namely right-sided, two-sided, and left-sided QLCT. The following are their definitions of right-sided, left-sided, and two-sided QLCT, respectively, which are obtained by replacing the kernels of the FT with that of the kernels of LCT

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K^{i}_{M_{1}}(x_{1},u_{1})K^{j}_{M_{2}}(x_{2},u_{2})f(x_{1},x_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2},

(152)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{Q}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(x_{1},x_{2})K^{i}_{M_{1}}(x_{1},u_{1})K^{j}_{M_{2}}(x_{2},u_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2}

(153)

and

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K^{i}_{M_{1}}(x_{1},u_{1})f(x_{1},x_{2})K^{j}_{M_{2}}(x_{2},u_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2},

(154)

where

\displaystyle K^{i}_{M_{1}}(x_{1},u_{1})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\sqrt{i2\pi B_{1}}}e^{i\left(\frac{A_{1}}{2B_{1}}x_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{B_{1}}x_{1}u_{1}+\frac{d_{1}}{2B_{1}}u_{1}^{2}\right)},&\penalty\ \mbox{for}\penalty\ B_{1}\neq 0\\
\sqrt{D_{1}}e^{i\left(\frac{C_{1}D_{1}}{2}\right)u_{1}^{2}}\delta(x_{1}-D_{1}u_{1}),&\penalty\ \mbox{for}\penalty\ B_{1}=0,\end{cases}

(155)

and

\displaystyle K^{j}_{M_{2}}(x_{2},u_{2})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\sqrt{i2\pi B_{2}}}e^{j\left(\frac{A_{2}}{2B_{2}}x_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{B_{2}}x_{2}u_{2}+\frac{D_{2}}{2B_{2}}u_{2}^{2}\right)},&\penalty\ \mbox{for}\penalty\ B_{2}\neq 0\\
\sqrt{D_{2}}e^{j\left(\frac{C_{2}D_{2}}{2}\right)u_{2}^{2}}\delta(x_{2}-D_{2}u_{2}),&\penalty\ \mbox{for}\penalty\ B_{2}=0,\end{cases}

(156)

M_{l}=(A_{l},B_{l},C_{l},D_{l})\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
are matrix parameters such that
det(M_{l})=1,
for
l=1,2
and
f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
To obtain the Parseval theorem for the QFT, Hitzer
[
97
]
modified the inner product to be the scalar inner product, while Chen
[
51
]
developed a strategy applying the
Q-
modules, i.e., (
126
), of QFT to define the
L^{p},
i.e., (
127
) to prove the Parseval formula. Bahri
[
15
]
obtained the relation between any two transforms given by (
152
),(
153
) and (
154
). Adopting the method of Chen
[
51
]
, Kou et al.
[
114
]
considered the following symmetrical representation

\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f=\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f_{0}+\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f_{1}i+\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f_{2}j+i\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f_{3}j

and introduced the
Q-
modules and
L^{2}-
norm of QLCT
\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{A_{1},A_{2}}f
respectively as

\displaystyle|\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f|_{Q}=\left(|\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f_{0}|^{2}+|\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f_{1}|^{2}+|\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f_{2}|^{2}+|\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f_{3}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(157)

and

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\|_{Q,p}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})\right|^{p}_{Q}du_{1}du_{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

(158)

to prove the Parseval formula

\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\|\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\|^{2}_{Q,2},\penalty\ f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Bahri et al.
[
20
]
proved some important properties like linearity, shifting, and modulation followed by the continuity of the QLCT of the function in
L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
Achak et al.
[
2
]
proves that Riemann-Lebesgue lemma i.e.,

\lim_{|u_{1}|\rightarrow\infty}|\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})|=0\penalty\ \mbox{and}\penalty\ \lim_{|u_{2}|\rightarrow\infty}|\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})|=0,\penalty\ f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

and the Hausdorff Young inequality

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\|_{Q,r^{\prime}}\leq\frac{|B_{1}B_{2}|^{\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}}}{2\pi}\|f\|_{L^{r}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

(159)

where
1\leq r\leq 2,

\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}=1
and
f\in L^{r}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
In particular,
\|\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\|_{Q,\infty}\leq\frac{|B_{1}B_{2}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{2\pi}\|f\|_{L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.
Using the orthogonal plane split method Bahri
[
16
]
obtained the simplex complex form

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})=\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f_{+}\right)(u_{1},u_{2})+\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f_{-}\right)(u_{1},u_{2})

(160)

and, using (
5
), the corresponding modular form

\displaystyle\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\right)(u_{1},u_{2})\right|^{2}=\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f_{+}\right)(u_{1},u_{2})\right|^{2}+\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f_{-}\right)(u_{1},u_{2})\right|^{2},

(161)

where

\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f_{\pm}\right)(u_{1},u_{2})=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-B_{1}(\mp B_{2})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{\frac{i}{2B_{1}}(A_{1}x_{1}^{2}+D_{1}u_{1}^{2}-2x_{1}u_{1})\mp\frac{i}{2B_{2}}(A_{2}x_{2}^{2}+D_{2}u_{2}^{2}-2x_{2}u_{2})}f_{\pm}(x_{1},x_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2}.

Kundu et al.
[
121
]
obtained the Plancherel theorem with respect the scalar product (
2
) as
\langle\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f,\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}g\rangle=\langle f,g\rangle,\penalty\ f,g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
Also taking the advantage of (
161
) and the sharp Hausdorff-Young inequality for 2D LCT, they obtained the following sharp Hausdorff Young inequality

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\|_{L^{q}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq|B_{1}B_{2}|^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{p^{\frac{1}{p}}}{q^{\frac{1}{q}}}\right)(2\pi)^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p}}\|f\|_{L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

(162)

where
1\leq p\leq 2,

\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
and
f\in L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
Extending the idea of Guanlie et al.
[
80
]
to prove (
80
), authors in
[
121
]
obtained the Pitt’s inequality for LCT of 2D function using which they obtained Pitt’s inequality for the QLCT for
f\in S(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{H})
and
0\leq\lambda<2
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{-\lambda}\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\right)(\boldsymbol{u})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}\leq\frac{R_{\lambda}}{|B_{1}B_{2}|^{\lambda}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{t}|^{\lambda}|f(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t}

(163)

where
R_{\lambda}=\left(\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1-\lambda}{4}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{4}\right)}\right)^{2}.

Uncertainty Principles:
By differentiating the Pitt’s inequality given by (
163
) for LCT of 2D function, Kundu et al.
[
121
]
obtained logarithmic UP for the LCT of two-dimensional complex valued function and using the orthogonal plane split method they obtained the following logarithmic UP for
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|\boldsymbol{u}|\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\right)(\boldsymbol{u})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|\boldsymbol{t}||f(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t}\geq K_{0}^{\prime}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|f(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t},

(164)

where
K_{0}^{\prime}=\frac{d}{d\lambda}(K_{\lambda})
at
\lambda=0
and
K_{\lambda}=\frac{R_{\lambda}}{|b_{1}b_{2}|^{\lambda}}.
Furthermore, they also obtained the local UP for the QLCT,

\displaystyle\int_{E}\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\right)(\boldsymbol{u})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}\leq\frac{K_{\lambda}}{|B_{1}B_{2}|}(2\pi)^{1-2\lambda}|E|^{\lambda}\||\boldsymbol{t}|^{\lambda}f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2},\penalty\ 0<\lambda<1

(165)

and

\displaystyle\int_{E}\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\right)(\boldsymbol{u})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}\leq\frac{K_{\lambda}}{|B_{1}B_{2}|}|E|\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2-\frac{2}{\lambda}}\||\boldsymbol{t}|^{\lambda}f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{\frac{2}{\lambda}},\penalty\ \lambda>1,

(166)

where
K_{\lambda}
is given equation (
137
),

B_{1}B_{2}\neq 0,

E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is measurable with measure
|E|
and
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
Without using the Hausdorff-Young inequality of the QLCT, authors in
[
121
]
obtained the entropy UP for
f\in S(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{H})
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(|f(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2})+\mathcal{H}\left(\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{ij}_{M_{1},M_{2}}f\right)(\boldsymbol{u})\right|^{2}\right)\geq 1+\ln\left(\left|\frac{B_{1}B_{2}}{2}\right|\right),

(167)

where
\mathcal{H}(P(\boldsymbol{t}))=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P(\boldsymbol{t})\ln\left(P(\boldsymbol{t})\right)d\boldsymbol{t},
where
P
is a probability density function. Based on the relation between quaternion LCT and the quaternion FT
[
20
]
, Zhang et al.
[
225
]
obtained the directional UP for
f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{x})^{2}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})^{2}|\mathcal{L}_{M_{1},M_{2}}^{\mathbb{H}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{u})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}\geq\frac{1}{4}[(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{b}\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{2}F_{-}^{2}+(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{b}\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\prime})^{2}F_{+}^{2}],

(168)

where
\boldsymbol{b}\boldsymbol{\gamma}=(B_{1}\gamma_{1},B_{2}\gamma_{2}),
constant vectors
\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\prime}=-\gamma_{1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}+\gamma\boldsymbol{e}_{2},

F_{-}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|f_{-}(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}
and
F_{+}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|f_{+}(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}.
Choosing
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\boldsymbol{e}_{k},\penalty\ k=1,2
equation (
168
) reduces to the component-wise UP

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x_{k}^{2}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{k}^{2}|\mathcal{L}_{M_{1},M_{2}}^{\mathbb{H}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{u})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}\geq\frac{B_{k}^{2}}{4}F^{2},\penalty\ F^{2}=F_{-}^{2}+F_{+}^{2}

(169)

which was obtained in
[
114
]
. Moreover, if
M_{l}=(A_{l},B_{l},C_{l},D_{l})=(0,1,-1,0),\penalty\ l=1,2
then (
168
) reduces to the directional UP proposed by Hitzer for two-sided QFT in
[
98
]
. Author in
[
225
]
also obtained the modified UP for
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{H})
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x_{1}^{2}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}x_{2}^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{1}^{2}|\mathcal{L}_{M_{1},M_{2}}^{\mathbb{H}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{u})|^{2}u_{2}^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}\geq(B_{1}B_{2})^{2}\left|\frac{1}{2}F-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x_{1}J^{f}_{M_{1},M_{2}}(\boldsymbol{x})x_{2}|d\boldsymbol{x}\right|^{2},

(170)

where
F=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}
and

J^{f}_{M_{1},M_{2}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{A_{1}x_{1}}{B_{1}}\overline{\frac{\partial f(\boldsymbol{x})}{\partial x_{2}}}if(\boldsymbol{x})+\overline{\frac{\partial f(\boldsymbol{x})}{\partial x_{2}}}\frac{\partial f(\boldsymbol{x})}{\partial x_{1}}-\frac{A_{2}x_{2}}{B_{2}}j\overline{f(\boldsymbol{x})}if(\boldsymbol{x})\frac{A_{1}x_{1}}{B_{1}}-\frac{A_{2}x_{2}}{B_{2}}j\overline{f(\boldsymbol{x})}\frac{\partial f(\boldsymbol{x})}{\partial x_{1}}.

which generalizes the modified UP for the two-sided QFT by Bahri
[
14
]
. Equality in (
170
) holds for

f(\boldsymbol{x})=\sqrt{B_{1}}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}}e^{-i\frac{A_{1}}{2B_{1}}x_{1}^{2}}K_{0}e^{-\left(\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2\alpha_{1}}+\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{2\alpha_{2}}\right)}E^{-j\frac{A_{2}}{2B_{2}}x_{2}^{2}}e^{j\frac{\pi}{4}}\sqrt{B_{2}},

where
\alpha_{1}>0,\alpha_{2}>0
are real constants and
K_{0}
is a quaternion constant.

\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{H})=\left\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{H})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}(1+|\boldsymbol{x}|^{k})|\partial^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f(\boldsymbol{x})|<\infty\right\},

where
\boldsymbol{x}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}},

\partial^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\frac{\partial^{|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|}}{\partial^{\alpha_{1}}x_{1}\partial^{\alpha_{2}}x_{2}},

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{H})
is the set of smooth function from
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to
\mathbb{H}.

If
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{Q}\{g\}(u_{1},u_{2})=e^{-i\frac{d_{5}}{2b_{5}}u_{1}^{2}}\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{1},M_{2}}\{f\}(u_{1},u_{2})e^{-j\frac{d_{6}}{2b_{6}}u_{2}^{2}},

(171)

then

\displaystyle g\left(\frac{t_{1}}{b_{5}},\frac{t_{2}}{b_{6}}\right)=\sqrt{b_{5}b_{6}}e^{i\frac{a_{5}}{2b_{5}}t_{1}^{2}-i\frac{\pi}{4}}\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{3},M_{4}}\{f\}(t_{1},t_{2})e^{j\frac{a_{6}}{2b_{6}}t_{2}^{2}-j\frac{\pi}{4}},

(172)

where
M_{3}=M_{5}^{-1}M_{1},

M_{4}=M_{6}^{-1}M_{2}.
Moreover,
\left|g\left(\frac{t_{1}}{b_{5}},\frac{t_{2}}{b_{6}}\right)\right|=\sqrt{b_{5}b_{6}}\left|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{5}^{-1}M_{1},M_{6}^{-1}M_{2}}\{f\}(t_{1},t_{2})\right|,
where
b_{5}=A_{3}B_{1}-A_{1}B_{3}
and
b_{6}=A_{4}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{4}.
This result was proved by Zhang et al. in
[
224
]
. Here,
\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{3},M_{4}}\{f\}(t_{1},t_{2})
is the inverse two-sided quaternion LCT of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{1},M_{2}}\{f\}(u_{1},u_{2})
with the parameters
M_{5},M_{6}.
The function
g(t_{1},t_{2})
serves as a bridge to obtain the functional relation between
\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{1},M_{2}}\{f\}(u_{1},u_{2})
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{3},M_{4}}\{f\}(t_{1},t_{2}).
Note that this sort of relation is only available for the two-sided quaternion LCT. Relations (
171
) and (
172
) are important, as they help in obtaining several UPs using the same for the quaternion FT. For
f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with unit energy, they obtained the UP, which presents lower bounds for the product of the spread of the quaternion-valued function in two different quaternion linear canonical domains, as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{l}^{2}|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{1},M_{2}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{u})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}v_{l}^{2}|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{3},M_{4}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{v}\geq P_{l},\penalty\ l=1,2

(173)

where
P_{1}=\frac{(A_{4}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{4})^{2}}{4}
and
P_{2}=\frac{(A_{3}B_{1}-A_{1}B_{3})^{2}}{4}.
In particular, if
M_{3}=M_{4}=(1,0,0,1),
(
173
) reduces to (
169
) for
F=1.
Using the relation (
171
) and the Hausdorff-Young inequality for the two-sided quaternion FT, Zhu et al.
[
228
]
introduced the Hausdorff-Young inequality for the quaternion LCT in two linear canonical domain as

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{1},M_{2}}{f}(\boldsymbol{u})\|_{L^{q}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq\left(\frac{p}{2\pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\frac{q}{2\pi}\right)^{-\frac{1}{q}}|b_{5}b_{6}|^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{A_{3},A_{4}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{t})\|_{L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

(174)

b_{5}=A_{3}B_{1}-A_{1}B_{3}
and
b_{6}=A_{4}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{4},
which is sharper than the one obtained in
[
227
]
. Zhu et al. also obtained the logarithmic UP in two LCT domains using the relation (
171
), for
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{H}),
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln\left|\frac{\boldsymbol{t}}{\boldsymbol{b}}\right||\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{3},M_{4}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|\boldsymbol{u}||\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{1},M_{2}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{u})|_{Q}^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}\geq D\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{3},M_{4}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t}

(175)

where
\frac{\boldsymbol{t}}{\boldsymbol{b}}=\left(\frac{t_{1}}{b_{5}},\frac{t_{2}}{b_{6}}\right)
and
D=\psi(\frac{1}{2})+\ln 2,

\psi=\frac{d}{dt}(\ln(\Gamma(t))),
and the module
|\cdot|_{Q}
on the second term of the left-hand side in equation (
175
), given by (
157
) can also be replaced by
|\cdot|
given by (
3
). Using the Heisenberg UP for the two-sided quaternion FT for the function
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and the relation (
171
), authors in
[
228
]
obtained the Heisenberg-type UP

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(t_{l}^{2})^{\alpha}|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{3},M_{4}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+\beta}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(u_{l}^{2})^{\beta}|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{1},M_{2}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+\beta}}\geq P_{l}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{3},M_{4}}|\{f\}(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t}\right),\penalty\ l=1,2

(176)

for
f\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\alpha,\beta\geq 1,
where
P_{1}=\left(\frac{(A_{3}B_{1}-A_{1}B_{3})^{2}}{4}\right)^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+\beta}}
and
P_{2}=\left(\frac{(A_{4}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{4})^{2}}{4}\right)^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+\beta}},
followed by the local UP associated with the two-sided quaternion LCT in two different linear canonical domain for all measurable subset
E
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as

\displaystyle\int_{E}|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{1},M_{2}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{u})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}\leq K_{\lambda}|E|^{\lambda}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\frac{\boldsymbol{t}}{\boldsymbol{b}}\right|^{2\lambda}|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{3},M_{4}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t},\penalty\ 0<\lambda<1,

(177)

and

\displaystyle\int_{E}|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{1},M_{2}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{u})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}\leq K_{\lambda}|E|^{\lambda}\|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{3},M_{4}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{t})\|^{2-2\lambda}_{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\frac{\boldsymbol{t}}{\boldsymbol{b}}\right|^{2\lambda}|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{3},M_{4}}\{f\}(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{\lambda}},\penalty\ \lambda>1

(178)

where
K_{\lambda}
is given by (
137
) and
\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{5},b_{6}).
The inequalities given by equations (
173
),(
173
),(
175
),(
176
),(
177
) and (
178
) reduces to the corresponding UP for the two-sided quaternion LCT in the spatial and frequency domain when the parameters
M_{5}=M_{1},

M_{6}=M_{2}
which yields
M_{3}=M_{4}=(1,0,0,1),
if further
M_{1}=(0,1,0,-1)=M_{2}=(0,1,0,-1)
then these inequalities leads to the corresponding uncertainty inequalities for the two-sided quaternion FT. Even though the standard deviation is widely accepted as a measure of uncertainty, it is not an ideal tool to express UP in some situations; some examples are cited in
[
42
]
to express limitations on the use of standard deviation. Thus, entropy was proposed by Shannon and Rènyi. In particular, Rènyi entropy is one parameter extension of Shannon entropy.

Definition 8.1
.

(Rènyi entropy) The entropy of a probability density function
P
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is defined by

H_{\alpha}(P)=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\ln\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}[P(\boldsymbol{t})]^{\alpha}d\boldsymbol{t}\right),\penalty\ \alpha\geq 1.

When
\alpha\to 1,
Rènyi entropy leads to Shannon entropy, given by
E(P)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln(P(\boldsymbol{t}))P(\boldsymbol{t})d\boldsymbol{t}.

Using the Hausdorff-Young inequality (
174
), the Rènyi UPs for the two-sided quaternion LCT in different linear canonical domains are obtained in
[
228
]
as

\displaystyle H_{\alpha}(|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{3},M_{4}}{f}(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2})+H_{\beta}(|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{1},M_{2}}{f}(\boldsymbol{u})|^{2})\geq\frac{\ln\alpha}{\alpha-1}+\frac{\ln\beta}{\beta-1}+2\ln\pi+\ln|b_{5}b_{6}|,

(179)

where
\alpha
and
\beta
are such that
\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\beta}=2
and
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=1.
When
\alpha\to 1
and
\beta\to 1,
then (
179
) becomes the Shannon UP

\displaystyle E(|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{3},M_{4}}{f}(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2})+E(|\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{H}}_{M_{1},M_{2}}{f}(\boldsymbol{u})|^{2})\geq 2+2\ln\pi+\ln|b_{5}b_{6}|,

(180)

in two different linear canonical domain. Equations (
179
) and (
180
) reduces to the corresponding UPs for the quaternion LCT in the spatial and frequency domain when
M_{5}=M_{1},M_{6}=M_{2}
and to that of the quaternion FT, if further
M_{3}=M_{4}=(1,0,0,1).

9
Quaternion Quadratic Phase Fourier Transform (QQPFT)

Recently, Gupta and Verma
[
88
]
extended QPFT (
87
) to the quaternion-valued functions. They defined the two-sided quaternion quadratic phase FT by replacing the kernels
\mathcal{K}^{i}(t_{1},\xi_{1})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-it_{1},\xi_{1}}
and
\mathcal{K}^{j}(t_{2},\xi_{2})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-jt_{2},\xi_{2}},
in the definition

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{K}^{i}(t_{1},\xi_{1})f(\boldsymbol{t})\mathcal{K}^{j}(t_{2},\xi_{2})d\boldsymbol{t},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

(181)

of two-sided quaternion FT by the kernels in the quadratic phase FT given in (
88
) as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{K}^{i}_{\wedge_{1}}(t_{1},\xi_{1})f(\boldsymbol{t})\mathcal{K}^{j}_{\wedge_{2}}(t_{2},\xi_{2})d\boldsymbol{t},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

(182)

for
f(\boldsymbol{t})\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\penalty\ \boldsymbol{t}=(t_{1},t_{2}),
where
\wedge_{l}=(A_{l},B_{l},C_{l},D_{l},E_{l}),A_{l},B_{l},C_{l},D_{l},E_{l}\in\mathbb{R}\penalty\ \mbox{and}\penalty\ B_{l}\neq 0\penalty\ \mbox{for}\penalty\ l=1,2

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{i}_{\wedge_{1}}(t_{1},\xi_{1})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-i\left(A_{1}t_{1}^{2}+B_{1}t_{1}\xi_{1}+C_{1}\xi_{1}^{2}+D_{1}t_{1}+E_{1}\xi_{1}\right)}

(183)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{j}_{\wedge_{2}}(t_{2},\xi_{2})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-j\left(A_{2}t_{2}^{2}+B_{2}t_{2}\xi_{2}+C_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}+D_{2}t_{2}+E_{2}\xi_{2}\right)}

(184)

The corresponding inversion formula is given by

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{t})=|B_{1}B_{2}|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\overline{\mathcal{K}^{i}_{\wedge_{1}}(t_{1},\xi_{1})}(\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})\overline{\mathcal{K}^{j}_{\wedge_{2}}(t_{2},\xi_{2})}d\boldsymbol{\xi}.

(185)

Based on the relation between quaternion QPFT and the quaternion FT

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})=e^{-i\left(C_{1}\xi_{1}^{2}+E_{1}\xi_{1}\right)}\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}\tilde{f}\right)(B_{1}\xi_{1},B_{2}\xi_{2})e^{-j\left(C_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}+E_{2}\xi_{2}\right)},

(186)

where
\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}\tilde{f}\right)(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-it_{1}\xi_{1}}\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{t})\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-jt_{2}\xi_{2}}d\boldsymbol{t}
with
\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{t})=e^{-i\left(A_{1}t_{1}^{2}+D_{1}t_{1}\right)}f(\boldsymbol{t})e^{-j\left(A_{2}t_{2}^{2}+D_{2}t_{2}\right)}
author in
[
88
]
obtained the Parseval’s formula
\langle f,g\rangle=|B_{1}B_{2}|\langle\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f,\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}g\rangle,
for
f,g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(in particular,
\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=|B_{1}B_{2}|\|\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})}
), and the sharp Hausdorff-Young inequality for the two-sided quaternion QPFT as

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f\|_{L^{q}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq\frac{(2\pi)^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p}}A_{p}^{2}}{|B_{1}B_{2}|^{\frac{1}{q}}}\|f\|_{L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

(187)

where
1\leq p\leq 2,

\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
and
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
A_{p}=\left(\frac{p^{\frac{1}{p}}}{q^{\frac{1}{q}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
Recently, Bahri et al.
[
19
]
also obtained the same inversion formula, Parseval’s formula and the sharp Hausdorff-Young inequality (
187
) for the QQPFT (
182
). They proved the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma i.e.,
\lim_{|\xi_{l}|\rightarrow\infty}\left|(\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})\right|=0,\penalty\ l=1,2
followed by the Pitt’s inequality
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{\alpha}\left|(\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\leq M(\alpha)|B_{1}B_{2}|^{1-\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{t}|^{\alpha}|f(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t},\penalty\ M(\alpha)=\pi^{\alpha}\left[\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{2-\alpha}{4}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{2+\alpha}{4}\right)}\right]^{2},
where
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{H}),
for the QQPFT using the same for the QFT. Bhat et al.
[
214
]
considered the two-sided quaternion QPFT (
182
) where the factor
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
in the kernels
\mathcal{K}^{i}_{\wedge_{1}}(t_{1},\xi_{1})
and
\mathcal{K}^{j}_{\wedge_{2}}(t_{2},\xi_{2})
are replaced respectively with
\sqrt{\frac{B_{1}i}{2\pi}}
and
\sqrt{\frac{B_{2}j}{2\pi}}.
Let us denote the transform in this case by
{}^{(1)}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}.
They also defined the right-sided and left-sided quaternion QPFT for the functions
L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
Apart from the properties studied in
[
88
]
, like the inversion formula, Parseval’s formula, and Hausdorff-Youngs inequality, authors also obtained the translation formula given by

\displaystyle\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{k})\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega})=e^{-i\left[A_{1}k_{1}^{2}+D_{1}k_{1}+B_{1}k_{1}\omega_{1}-4\frac{A_{1}^{2}C_{1}}{B_{1}^{2}}k_{1}^{2}-4\frac{A_{1}}{B_{1}}C_{1}\omega_{1}k_{1}-2\frac{A_{1}}{B_{1}}k_{1}\right]}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\times\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f\right)\left(\omega_{1}+2\frac{A_{1}}{B_{1}}K_{1},\omega_{2}+2\frac{A_{2}}{B_{2}}K_{2}\right)e^{-j\left[A_{2}k_{2}^{2}+D_{2}k_{2}+B_{2}k_{2}\omega_{2}-4\frac{A_{2}^{2}C_{2}}{B_{2}^{2}}k_{2}^{2}-4\frac{A_{2}}{B_{2}}C_{2}\omega_{2}k_{2}-2\frac{A_{2}}{B_{2}}k_{2}\right]}
