\displaystyle\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{M}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}}f)\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega})=e^{i\left[\frac{C_{1}u_{0}^{2}-2B_{1}C_{1}u_{0}\omega_{1}-B_{1}-B_{1}E_{1}u_{0}}{B_{1}^{2}}\right]}\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f\right)\left(\omega_{1}-\frac{u_{0}}{B_{1}},\omega_{2}-\frac{v_{0}}{B_{2}}\right)e^{j\left[\frac{C_{2}v_{0}^{2}-2B_{2}C_{2}v_{0}\omega_{2}-B_{2}-B_{2}E_{2}v_{0}}{B_{2}^{2}}\right]}.

Uncertainty Principles:
Using the sharp Hausdorff Young inequality (
187
), the following Rèyni UP for the quaternion QPFT

\displaystyle H_{\alpha}(|f|^{2})+H_{\beta}\left(\left|\sqrt{|B_{1}B_{2}|}\left(\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\xi})\right|^{2}\right)\geq-\log(|B_{1}B_{2}|)-2\log(2\pi)-\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log(2\alpha)+\frac{1}{1-\beta}\log(2\beta)\right),

(188)

where
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha<1
and
\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\beta}=1
was proved in
[
88
]
. Equation (
188
) results in Shannon UP for the quaternion QPFT
E(|f|^{2})+E\left(\left|\sqrt{|B_{1}B_{2}|}\left(\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\xi})\right|^{2}\right)\geq\log\left(\frac{e^{2}}{16\pi^{2}|B_{1}B_{2}|}\right),
when
\alpha\to 1
and
\beta\to 1.
It is to be noted that the Hausdorff-Youngs inequality (
187
), obtained in the
[
88
]
is more sharper than the one obtained in
[
214
]
. Based on the relationship between QQPFT and the QFT and using the Heisenberg’s and logarithmic UP for the QFT, authors in
[
214
]
(and also
[
19
]
) obtained the Heisenberg’s UP for the transform
{}^{(1)}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}
given as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x_{l}^{2}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{l}^{2}\left|\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)\geq\frac{1}{4B_{l}^{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\right)^{2},\penalty\ l=1,2

(189)

for
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
followed by the logarithmic UP given as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|\boldsymbol{x}||f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|\boldsymbol{\omega}|\left|\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}\geq(D-\ln|\boldsymbol{B}|)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x},

(190)

for the functions
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{H}),
where
D=\ln(2\pi^{2})-2\psi\left(\frac{1}{2}\right),\penalty\ \psi=\frac{d}{dt}(\ln(\Gamma(t))).
In fact, a more general version of the Heisenberg UP for
\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}
can be found in
[
19
]
. Apart from these UPs, authors in
[
214
]
also obtained the Hardy’s UP and Beurling’s UP for the transform
{}^{(1)}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}},
using the same for the QFT. Using the relation between
{}^{(1)}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}
and the QFT and its associated Donoho-Stark’s UP authors also proved that, if a non-zero
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is
\epsilon_{E_{1}}-
concentrated on
E_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
{}^{(1)}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}f
is
\epsilon_{E_{2}}-
concentrated on
E_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2},
then
|E_{1}||E_{2}|\geq\frac{2\pi}{|\boldsymbol{B}|}(1-\epsilon_{E_{1}}-\epsilon_{E_{2}})^{2}.
This is called the Donoho-Stark’s UP for the QQPFT
{}^{(1)}\mathcal{Q}^{\wedge_{1},\wedge_{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}.

10
Windowed Fourier Transform (WFT)

It is well known that the representation of a function
f
of
x
is usually called its time representation, while its FT
\hat{f}(\xi)
is called the frequency representation. From the application point of view, one often needs information regarding the frequency behavior of a signal at a certain time and the time behavior at certain frequency components of a signal. This leads to constructing several joint time-frequency representations like the Gabor transform, WT, ST, Wigner distribution, etc. Other names for the Gabor transform that are frequently used in the literature are the Weyl-Heisenberg transform, short-time FT, and WFT. The motivations for the development of the WT are of a similar nature. However, it is preferably called the time-scale representation since the parameter
a
involved in its definition cannot completely be identified with an inverse frequency, as it is often done in the literature.

Definition 10.1
.

The Gabor transform of
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the window function
\psi
is defined by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega,t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\overline{\psi(x-t)}e^{-i\omega x}dx.

(191)

Equivalently, (
191
) has several important forms. Before we write them, let us first mention some important operators that we will be frequently using throughout the paper.

1.

Translation operator:

(\tau_{y}\psi)(x)=\psi(x-y),\penalty\ y\in\mathbb{R}.

2.

Dilation Operator(s):

(\delta_{a}\psi)(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|a|}}\psi\left(\frac{x}{a}\right)
and
(\mathcal{D}_{a}\psi)(x)=|a|\psi(ax),\penalty\ a\in\mathbb{R}_{0}.

3.

Modulation Operator:

(\mathcal{M}_{a}\psi)(x)=e^{iax}\psi(x),\penalty\ a\in\mathbb{R}.

Based on these operators, equation (
191
) can be written as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega,t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\overline{(\tau_{t}\psi)(x)}e^{-i\omega x}dx

(192)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega,t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\overline{(\mathcal{M}_{a}\tau_{t}\psi)(x)}dx

(193)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega,t)=\langle f,\psi_{\omega,t}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

(194)

where
\psi_{\omega,t}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}(\mathcal{M}_{a}\tau_{t}\psi)(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\psi(x-t)e^{i\omega x}.
It is a matter of simple verification that
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f
is linear in
f
and conjugate linear in
\psi.
The following properties, called the covariance properties of the WFT, give respectively the formula for the WFT of the translation by
x_{0}
and modulation by
\omega_{0}
of function
f
with respect to the window function
\psi

\displaystyle[\mathcal{G}_{\psi}(\tau_{x_{0}}f)](\omega,t)=e^{-i\omega x_{0}}(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega,t-x_{0})

(195)

\displaystyle[\mathcal{G}_{\psi}(\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{0}}f)](\omega,t)=(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega-\omega_{0},t).

(196)

Using (
195
) and (
196
), one can obtain
[\mathcal{G}_{\psi}(\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{0}}\tau_{x_{0}}f)](\omega,t)=e^{-i\omega x_{0}}(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega-\omega_{0},t-x_{0}),\penalty\ (\omega,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega,t)\overline{(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}g)(\omega,t)}=\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

This in particular, for
f=g,
implies that
\|\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},
which further shows that the normalized WFT
\frac{1}{\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}\mathcal{G}_{\psi}
is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
into
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
The WFT converts a function
f(x)
of one variable into a function
(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega,t)
of two variables without changing the total energy. Also,
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f
is in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
i.e., the range
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}\left(L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\right)
of WFT of square integrable functions is a subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
In fact, it is a proper subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
because
|(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega,t)|\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\penalty\ \omega,t\in\mathbb{R}
implies that
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f
is bounded, but
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
contains functions that are unbounded. Thus, square integrability of
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f
is a necessary condition but not sufficient. The range
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R})),
of bounded linear operator
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
which is isometric upto some constant, is a closed subspace. Being closed subspace of the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\mathcal{G}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
is also a Hilbert space. Moreover, it is a RKHS, with the kernel given by

\displaystyle K_{\psi}(\omega^{\prime},t^{\prime};\omega,t)=\frac{1}{\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}\langle\psi_{\omega,t},\psi_{\omega^{\prime},t^{\prime}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

(197)

which is pointwise bounded, i.e.,
|K_{\psi}(\omega^{\prime},t^{\prime};\omega,t)|\leq 1,\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ (\omega^{\prime},t^{\prime}),(\omega,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.
A strong inequality for the WFT, proved by Lieb
[
127
]
, often called Lieb’s inequality in the literature, for
f,\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
2\leq p<\infty
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}f(x,\omega)|^{p}dxd\omega\leq\left(\frac{2}{p}\right)^{d}\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)^{p}.

(198)

For
1\leq p\leq 2,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}f(x,\omega)|^{p}dxd\omega\geq\left(\frac{2}{p}\right)^{d}\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)^{p},

(199)

where
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}f
for the functions
f,\psi
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
are defined similar to equation (
191
).

Uncertainty Principles:
Wilczok
[
209
]
proved that
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f
of a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\setminus\{0\}
cannot have a support of finite Lebesgue measure. The author also proved that the portion of
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f
lying outside some set
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
cannot be arbitrarily small. For sufficiently small
M,
this fact is justified by the following theorem, which can be proved by estimating the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of a suitably defined operator.

Theorem 10.1
.

(Concentration of
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f
on a small set) Let
\psi
be a window function and
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\lambda^{(2)}(M)<1.
Then, for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
arbitrary

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f-\chi_{M}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\geq\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}(1-\lambda^{(2)}(M)^{\frac{1}{2}})\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

(200)

It is to be noted that the lower bound of
\|\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f-\chi_{M}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
in (
201
) is larger the smaller
\lambda^{(2)}(M)
is. However, it can be seen that the above theorem requires the assumption that
\lambda^{(2)}(M)<1.
So a more general result is established with the assumption that
M
is an arbitrary subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with finite Lebesgue measure, i.e.,
for a given window function
\psi
and
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\lambda^{(2)}(M)<\infty,
there exists a constant
C^{\mathcal{G}}_{\psi,M}>0
such that for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
arbitrary

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f-\chi_{M}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\geq C^{\mathcal{G}}_{\psi,M}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

(201)

This result was established by Wilczok
[
209
]
using the abstract result of Havin and Jöricke in
[
92
]
.
However, the author in
[
209
]
did not give an estimate for the constant
C^{\mathcal{G}}_{\psi,M}
involved in (
201
) by the measure of
M.
Motivated by the idea of Singer
[
187
]
in the case of WT, Wilczok obtained a different class of UPs results for the comparing the localization of
f
and
\hat{f}
with the localization of the WFT regarded as function of one variable as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega^{2}|(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega,t)|^{2}d\omega dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(x^{2}|f(x)|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

(202)

and

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}t^{2}|(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega,t)|^{2}d\omega dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\xi^{2}|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

(203)

which are called the UPs of the Heisenberg type for the WFT in
\omega
and
t
, respectively. Using (
202
) and (
203
) one gets
\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega^{2}|(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega,t)|^{2}d\omega dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}t^{2}|(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega,t)|^{2}d\omega dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(x^{2}|f(x)|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\xi^{2}|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\frac{1}{4}\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|f\|^{4}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},
which is called the phase space uncertainty of WFT. It can be interpreted by saying that the better the phase space localization of the pair
(f,\hat{f}),
the worse the phase space localization of the WFT
(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(\omega,t).
The time-frequency resolution of
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f
is limited by the Heisenberg UP for
\psi,
which is based on the interpretation of the standard deviations as the size of the essential supports of
\psi
and its Fourier transform. Donoho and Stark
[
76
]
,
[
63
]
used the concept “
\epsilon
-concentrated” to replace standard deviation and derived the UP that reads as

\displaystyle|\Omega||\Gamma|\geq 2\pi(1-\epsilon_{\Omega}-\epsilon_{\Gamma})^{2},

whenever a non-zero function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is
\epsilon_{\Omega}-
concentrated on
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}
and its Fourier transform is
\epsilon_{\Gamma}-
concentrated on
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}.
An analogous to which is given as

|U|\geq(1-\epsilon),

for
f,\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),
with
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=1,
and
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
such that

\displaystyle\int\int_{U}\left|(\mathcal{V}_{\psi}f)(x,\omega)\right|^{2}dxd\omega\geq 1-\epsilon,

(204)

\epsilon\geq 0.
With the similar assumptions on
f,\psi,U
and
\mathcal{V}_{\psi}f
satisfying the assumption (
204
), Lieb (see
[
76
]
) obtain a stronger lower bound of essential support as
|U|\geq(1-\epsilon)^{\frac{p}{p-2}}\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)^{\frac{2d}{p-2}},
for all
p>2.
In particular,
|U|\geq\sup_{p>0}(1-\epsilon)^{\frac{p}{p-2}}\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)^{\frac{2d}{p-2}}\geq(1-\epsilon)^{2}2^{d}.
This is often called the Lieb’s UP for short time Fourier transform in literature. With the assumption, that
f,\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d}),

U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
such that
\int\int_{U}\left|(\mathcal{V}_{\psi}f)(x,\omega)\right|^{2}dxd\omega\geq(1-\epsilon)\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}
and using the Lieb’s inequality for
p\geq 2,
Poria in
[
154
]
estimated the size of the essential support of the STFT as
|U|\geq(1-\epsilon)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)^{\frac{d}{p-1}},\penalty\ p\geq 2.
Furthermore, he also proved that if
\int\int_{U}\left|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}f(x,\omega)\right|^{p}dxd\xi\geq(1-\epsilon)\|\mathcal{V}_{\psi}f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}^{p},
then
|U|\geq 2^{\frac{2pd}{2-p}}(1-\epsilon)^{\frac{2}{2-p}}
for all
1\leq p<2.

11
Wavelet Transform (WT)

In signal analysis,
(G_{\phi}f)(b,\xi)
gives the time-frequency content of a signal
f
at time
b
and frequency
\xi
by placing the window
\phi
at time
b.
The drawback here is that a window of fixed width is used for all time
b.
It is more accurate and desirable if we have an adaptive window that gives a wide window for low frequency and a narrow one for high frequency. This can be done using the WT.

Definition 11.1
.

A function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\setminus\{0\}
satisfying the admissibility condition

c_{\psi}=2\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{\psi}(\xi)|^{2}\frac{d\xi}{|\xi|}<\infty

is called a mother wavelet.

For a given mother wavelet
\psi
and
(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R},
the family

\psi_{a,b}(x)=\tau_{b}\delta_{a}\psi(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|}a|}\psi\left(\frac{x-b}{a}\right)
are called the daughter wavelets.

Definition 11.2
.

The WT of
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the mother wavelet
\psi
is defined by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\frac{1}{\sqrt{|a|}}\overline{\psi\left(\frac{x-b}{a}\right)}dx.

(205)

Equivalently, using the definition of translation and the dilation operator in
1
and
2
respectively, (
205
) has several important forms. Like

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\overline{\tau_{b}\delta_{a}\psi(x)}dx.

(206)

and

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\langle f,\psi_{a,b}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

(207)

Another important, equivalent form of the definition of the WT (
205
) is in terms of the convolution given by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\left(f\star\delta_{a}\tilde{\psi}\right)(b),

(208)

where
\tilde{\psi}(t)=\overline{\psi(-t)}.
It is a matter of simple verification that
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f
is linear in
f
and conjugate linear in
\psi.
The following properties, called the covariance properties of the WT, give the formula for the WT of the translation by
x_{0}
and dilation by
c\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
of the function
f
with respect to
\psi

[(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\tau_{x_{0}}f)](a,b)=(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,b-x_{0})

and

[(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(\delta_{c}f)](a,b)=(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)\left(\frac{a}{c},\frac{b}{c}\right).

The transform (
205
), satisfies the following inner product relations

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}g)(a,b)}\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}=c_{\psi}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

This in particular, for
f=g,
implies that
\|\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R},\frac{dadb}{a^{2}})}=c_{\psi}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},
which further shows that the operator
\frac{1}{\sqrt{c_{\psi}}}\mathcal{W}_{\psi}
is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
into
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R},d\mu_{aff}),
where
d\mu_{aff}=\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}
denotes the so called affine measure. The WT converts a function
f(x)
of one variable into a function
(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f)(a,b)
of two variables without changing the total energy. Also, the
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f
is in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R},\frac{dadb}{a^{2}})
for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
i.e., the space of all WFT of square-integrable functions
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
is a subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R},\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}).
In fact, it is a proper subspace because
|(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,b)|\leq\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\penalty\ (a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R}
implies that
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f
is bounded, but
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R},\frac{dadb}{a^{2}})
contains functions that are unbounded. Thus, the belongingness of
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R},\frac{dadb}{a^{2}})
is necessary condition but not sufficient. The range
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R})),
of bounded linear operator
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R},\frac{dadb}{a^{2}})
which is isometric upto some constant, is a closed subspace. Being closed subspace of the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R},\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}),

\mathcal{G}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
is also a Hilbert space. Moreover, it is a RKHS, with the kernel given by

K_{\psi}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime};a,b)=\frac{1}{c_{\psi}}\langle\psi_{a,b},\psi_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

The kernel is pointwise bounded, i.e.,
|K_{\psi}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime};a,b)|\leq\frac{\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{c_{\psi}},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ (a^{\prime},b^{\prime}),(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R}.

Definition 11.3
.

A function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a window function if it satisfies the conditions
x_{l_{1}}x_{l_{2}}\cdots x_{l_{r}}f(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
for all possible choices of
l_{1},l_{2},\cdot,l_{r}\in\{1,2,\cdots,n\},
with
r\in\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
where
l_{1},l_{2},\cdot,l_{r}
are all different. In particular, if
n=2,
then we say that
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a window function if
x_{1}f(\boldsymbol{x}),x_{2}f(\boldsymbol{x})
and
x_{1}x_{2}f(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
For example
\psi(\boldsymbol{x})=x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}e^{-(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+\cdots+x_{n}^{2})}
is a window function.

A function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a basic wavelet if it satisfies the following admissibility condition

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|\hat{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}}{|\boldsymbol{\xi}|_{p}}d\boldsymbol{\xi}<\infty.

(209)

If
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a window function then
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
this can be proved using the notion of pseudo-orthants
[
146
]
. Pandey et al.
[
145
]
proved that a window function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies the admissibility condition (
209
) if and only if
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n})dx_{l},\penalty\ l=1,2,\cdots,n.
Some examples of window functions that are wavelets are
\Pi_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}e^{-x_{i}^{2}}
and
\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\frac{x_{i}}{1+x_{i}^{4}}.
Pandey et al.
[
150
]
defined the WT of
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with respect to the basic wavelet
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
by

\displaystyle(W_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{a})=\left\langle f(\boldsymbol{x}),\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\boldsymbol{a}|_{p}}}\psi\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b}}{\boldsymbol{a}}\right)\right\rangle

(210)

and proved that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(W_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{a})\overline{(W_{\psi}g)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{a})}\frac{d\boldsymbol{a}d\boldsymbol{b}}{|\boldsymbol{a}|_{p}^{2}}=C_{\psi}\langle f,g\rangle\penalty\ \mbox{for all}\penalty\ f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

(211)

followed by the inversion formula is

\displaystyle f=\frac{1}{C_{\psi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(W_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})|\boldsymbol{a}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\psi\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b}}{\boldsymbol{a}}\right)\frac{d\boldsymbol{a}d\boldsymbol{b}}{|\boldsymbol{a}|_{p}^{2}},

(212)

where
C_{\psi}=(2\pi)^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|\hat{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}}{|\boldsymbol{\xi}|_{p}}d\boldsymbol{\xi}
and
|\boldsymbol{a}|_{p}=|a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}|.
The convergence in (
212
) is interpreted in the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
sense. Moreover, the convergence of the inversion formula (
212
) is pointwise at all the points of continuity of
f.
It is to be noted that the parameter
\boldsymbol{a}
was taken to be
(a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{n})
where the wavelet
\psi
is not spherically symmetric in
[
146
]
,
[
145
]
which was not the case in Daubechies
[
57
]
, they took
\boldsymbol{a}=(a,a,\cdots,a),
with
a>0
and the wavelet
\psi
as spherically symmetric to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{da}{a^{n+1}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\boldsymbol{b}(T^{wav}f)(a,\boldsymbol{b})\overline{(T^{wav}g)(a,\boldsymbol{b})}=C_{\psi}\langle f,g\rangle

(213)

and

\displaystyle f=C_{\psi}^{-1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\boldsymbol{b}(T^{wav}f)(a,\boldsymbol{b})\psi^{a,\boldsymbol{b}},

(214)

where
\psi^{a,\boldsymbol{b}}(\boldsymbol{x})=a^{-\frac{n}{2}}\psi\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b}}{a}\right)
and
(T^{wav}f)(a,\boldsymbol{b})=\langle f,\psi^{a,\boldsymbol{b}}\rangle
is the WT of
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with respect to the spherically symmetric function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfying the condition
C_{\psi}=(2\pi)^{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{t}|\eta(t)|^{2}<\infty
and
\hat{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\eta(|\boldsymbol{\xi}|)
for some
\eta,
which is possible to choose because
\psi
is spherically symmetric. Recently, Pandey et al.
[
148
]
defined the continuous WT of tempered distribution
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the wavelet kernel
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
It is to be noted that a function in
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is naturally a window function, as per the definition given in
[
145
]
. So it satisfied the admissible condition (
209
) if and only if its integral with respect to each variable
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}
along the real line vanishes. Let
s(\mathbb{R}^{n})
contains all the wavelets in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
For the distribution
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and the wavelet
\psi\in s(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
the WT is defined in
[
148
]
(also see
[
151
]
) with the corresponding inversion formula, where the convergence is interpreted in the weak topology of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
They found that the WT of a constant distribution is zero and that the inversion formula is not true for a constant distribution but is true for a non-constant distribution which is not the sum of a non-constant distribution and a non-zero constant distribution. Pandey
[
147
]
considered the distribution space
\mathcal{D}_{L^{2}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
where
\mathcal{D}_{L^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
consists of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
function whose every derivative is in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and is equipped with a topology generated by a separating family of semi-norms
\{\gamma_{k}\}_{|k|=0}^{\infty}
given by
\gamma_{k}(\phi)=\|\phi^{(k)}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
for
\phi\in\mathcal{D}_{L^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
They defined the WT of generalized functions in
\mathcal{D}_{L^{2}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
which respect to the wavelet
\psi(\boldsymbol{x})
which are functions
\mathcal{D}_{L^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{D}_{L^{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the property
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(\boldsymbol{x})dx_{l}=0,\penalty\ l=1,2,\cdots,n
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(\boldsymbol{x})d\boldsymbol{x}\neq 0,
where
\boldsymbol{x}^{\boldsymbol{m}}=x_{1}^{m_{1}}x_{2}^{m_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{m_{n}},

\boldsymbol{m}=(m_{1},m_{2},\cdots,m_{n}),m_{1},m_{2},\cdots,m_{n}\geq 1
also obtained the corresponding inversion formula with the convergence interpreted in the weak distributional sense. The space
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{L^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
does not contain a non-zero constant, so it does not encounter a problem as seen with the WT of distribution in the space
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with respect to the wavelet
\phi
in
\mathcal{D}
satisfying the property that all its moments along the real lines are non-zero
[
149
]
, i.e., two functions having the same WT may differ by the constant.

Rieder
[
168
]
extended the WT
L_{\psi}f(b,a)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{C_{\psi}}\langle\tau_{b}\delta_{a}\psi,f\rangle_{0}
to the Sobolev spaces
H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}),\alpha\in\mathbb{R},
consisting of the tempered distribution having a regular and with respect to the weight
(1+\omega^{2})^{\alpha}
square integrable FT, and interpreted its image as subset of the fiber space
\mathscr{F}^{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=L_{2}\left(\left(\mathbb{R}_{0},\frac{da}{a^{2}}\right),H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})\right),
which consists of functions
\phi\in H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{0}}\|\phi\|_{\alpha}^{2}\frac{da}{a^{2}}<\infty.
This space is isomorphic to the tensor product
L_{2}\left(\mathbb{R}_{0},\frac{da}{a^{2}}\right)\otimes H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
as well as the Sobolev space of two variables
H^{0,\alpha}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2},\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}\right).
Using the density of the Schwartz function in
H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}),
author proved that the for a fixed
a,
and an integrable and admissible
\psi,

L_{\psi}f(\cdot,a)
is a continuous mapping from
H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
to itself. Moreover, the operator
L_{\psi}
is an isometry from
H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
to the space
\mathscr{F}^{\alpha},
i.e.,

\displaystyle\|L_{\psi}f\|_{\mathscr{F}^{\alpha}}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\|L_{\psi}f(\cdot,a)\|_{H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\frac{da}{a^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\|f\|_{H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})}

(215)

and Range
(L_{\psi})\subset\mathscr{F}^{\alpha}
is a Hilbert space with the reproducing kernel
P(\tilde{b},\tilde{a},b,a)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{C_{\psi}}}(L_{\psi}\psi)\left(\frac{\tilde{b}-b}{a},\frac{\tilde{a}}{a}\right).
The
H^{s}-
distance of two WT with different admissible and integrable
\psi
and
\phi
of different argument function
f
and
g
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
respectively were obtained to study the dependence of the transform on its wavelets and its argument as

\displaystyle\|(L_{\psi}f)(a,\cdot)-(L_{\phi}g)(a,\cdot)\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R})}\leq|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\left\|\frac{\psi}{\sqrt{C_{\psi}}}-\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{C_{\phi}}}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\|f\|_{H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})}+\left\|\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{C_{\phi}}}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\|f-g\|_{H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})}\right).

(216)

Perrier et al.
[
153
]
extended the WT to the
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
spaces to characterize the Besov spaces
B_{p}^{\alpha,q}(\mathbb{R})
by the behavior at the small scale of the wavelet coefficient.

Definition 11.4
.

The Hardy space
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined as the space of all functions
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sup_{t>0}|(f\star\phi_{t})(\boldsymbol{x})|d\boldsymbol{x}<\infty,
where
\phi
is a function in Schwartz space such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi(\boldsymbol{x})d\boldsymbol{x}\neq 0
and
\phi_{t}(\boldsymbol{x})=t^{-n}\phi(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{t}),\penalty\ t>0,\penalty\ \boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Chuong et al.
[
54
]
proved that for any
a\neq 0
and any basis wavelet
\psi,
with the assumption that
\psi
is integrable, the operator
W_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
given by
f\rightarrow(W_{\psi}f)(a,\cdot)
are bounded such that

\|(W_{\psi}f)(a,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq|a|^{\frac{n}{2}}\|\psi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
and
\|(W_{\psi}f)(a,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=O(|a|^{\frac{n}{2}}).
Moreover,

\displaystyle\|(W_{\psi}f)(a,\cdot)-(W_{\phi}g)(a,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq|a|^{\frac{n}{2}}\left(\|\psi-\phi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|\phi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|f-g\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\right),\penalty\ f,g\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(217)

Similar result was also obtained with the same estimates of the bound in the case of the Besov space
B_{p}^{\alpha,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, bounded mean oscillation space
BMO(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and the
VMO(\mathbb{R}^{n})
space with the norm replaced with the corresponding norm and the basic wavelet compactly supported in the case of
BMO(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
VMO(\mathbb{R}^{n})
spaces. We discuss these results for the weighted Besov spaces and the weighted BMO spaces associated with a tempered weight function. Let us recall the definitions of the weighted Besov spaces and the weighted BMO spaces associated with a tempered weight function, which was proposed by Hörmander to study the theory of partial differential equations, i.e., a positive function
k
defined in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for which there exist
M,N>0
such that
k(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})\leq(1+M|\boldsymbol{x}|)^{N}k(\boldsymbol{y}),\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ \boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
For example,
k(\boldsymbol{x})=(1+|\boldsymbol{x}|^{2})^{\frac{s}{2}},
for every
s\in\mathbb{R}.
We start with the following definitions.

Definition 11.5
.

The weighted Lebesgue space
L^{p}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\penalty\ 1\leq p<\infty
is the space of all measurable functions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\|f\|_{p,k}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{p}k(\boldsymbol{x})d\boldsymbol{x}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty.

Definition 11.6
.

Let
0<\alpha<1
and
1\leq p,q<\infty,
then the weighted Besov space
B_{p,k}^{\alpha,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined as

B_{p,k}^{\alpha,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\left\{f\in L^{p}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|w_{p,k}(f,\boldsymbol{h})|^{q}\frac{d\boldsymbol{h}}{|\boldsymbol{h}|^{n+\alpha q}}<\infty\right\},\penalty\ q<\infty

and

B_{p,k}^{\alpha,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\left\{f\in L^{p}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|\boldsymbol{h}|^{-\alpha}w_{p,k}(f,\boldsymbol{h})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\})\right\}

with the respective norm as
\|f\|_{B_{p,k}^{\alpha,q}}=\|f\|_{p,k}+\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|w_{p,k}(f,\boldsymbol{h})|^{q}\frac{d\boldsymbol{h}}{|\boldsymbol{h}|^{n+\alpha q}}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}
and
\|f\|_{B_{p,k}^{\alpha,\infty}}=\|f\|_{p,k}+\||\boldsymbol{h}|^{-\alpha}w_{p,k}(f,\boldsymbol{h})\|_{\infty},
where
w_{p,k}(f,\boldsymbol{h})=\|f(\cdot+\boldsymbol{h})-f(\cdot)\|_{p,k}.

Definition 11.7
.

The weighted bounded mean oscillation space
BMO_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined as the space of all functions
f\in L_{loc}^{1,k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\|f\|_{BMO_{k}}=\sup_{B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}}=\frac{1}{|B|_{k}}\int_{B}|f(\boldsymbol{x})-f_{B}|k(\boldsymbol{x})d\boldsymbol{x}<\infty,
where supremum is taken over all balls in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
|B|_{k}=\int_{B}k(\boldsymbol{x})d\boldsymbol{x}.

Authors in
[
54
]
also proved that for any
a\neq 0,
and any compactly supported basis wavelet
\psi,
with the support belonging to a ball of radius
r
with center at the origin, the operator
W_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B_{p,k}^{\alpha,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\rightarrow B_{p,k}^{\alpha,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
W_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}BMO_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\rightarrow BMO_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
given by
f\rightarrow(W_{\psi}f)(a,\cdot)
are bounded such that
\|(W_{\psi}f)(a,\cdot)\|_{B_{p,k}^{\alpha,q}}\leq(1+Mr|a|)^{\frac{N}{p}}|a|^{\frac{n}{2}}\|\psi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|f\|_{B_{p,k}^{\alpha,q}}
and
\|(W_{\psi}f)(a,\cdot)\|_{BMO_{k}}\leq(1+Mr|a|)^{2N}|a|^{\frac{n}{2}}\|\psi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|f\|_{BMO_{k}}.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\|(W_{\psi}f)(a,\cdot)-(W_{\phi}g)(a,\cdot)\|_{B_{p,k}^{\alpha,q}}\leq(1+Mr|a|)^{\frac{N}{p}}|a|^{\frac{n}{2}}\left(\|\psi-\phi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|f\|_{B_{p,k}^{\alpha,q}}+\|\phi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|f-g\|_{B_{p,k}^{\alpha,q}}\right),\penalty\ f,g\in B_{p,k}^{\alpha,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

and for
f,g\in BMO_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

\displaystyle\|(W_{\psi}f)(a,\cdot)-(W_{\phi}g)(a,\cdot)\|_{BMO_{k}}\leq(1+Mr|a|)^{2N}|a|^{\frac{n}{2}}\left(\|\psi-\phi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|f\|_{BMO_{k}}+\|\phi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|f-g\|_{BMO_{k}}\right).

(218)

Chuong et. al
[
53
]
studied the WT on the weighted Sobolev space
W_{\omega}^{m,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
which is defined as

\displaystyle W_{\omega}^{m,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\left\{f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})|\partial^{k}f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),|k|\leq m\right\},

(219)

which is equipped with the norm
\|f\|_{m,p,\omega}=\sum_{|\mu|\leq m}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\omega_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{x})|\partial^{\mu}f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{p}d\boldsymbol{x}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty,
where
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\cdots,\mu_{n}),

|\mu|=\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\cdots+\mu_{n},\mu_{l}\geq 0
and
\omega_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\omega_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{x})>0,
for almost all
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and for each
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

\omega_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y})\leq C_{1,\mu}\omega_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{x})
for almost all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Authors in
[
53
]
proved that the WT operator
(L_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},a)\left(\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{1}{C_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2n}}}\frac{1}{|a|^{\frac{n}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\bar{\psi}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b}}{a}\right)f(\boldsymbol{x})d\boldsymbol{x}\right),
for fixed
a>0
maps
W_{\omega}^{m,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to itself and
\|(L_{\psi}f)(\cdot,a))\|_{m,p,\omega}\leq C\|f\|_{m,p,\omega},
where
C=\max_{|\mu|\leq m}C_{\mu},

C_{\mu}=\frac{(C_{1,\mu})^{\frac{1}{p}}}{C_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2n}}}|a|^{\frac{n}{2}}\|\psi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
and
C_{\psi}=(2\pi)^{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|\hat{\psi}(a\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}}{a}da
is a non zero constant for every
\boldsymbol{\xi}\neq 0.
They also estimated the
\|\cdot\|_{m,p,\omega}-
distance of two WT with different basic wavelets
\psi
and
\phi
of different argument function
f
and
g
in
W_{\omega}^{m,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
respectively and studied the asymptotic behavior of the WT for small dilation parameter, analogous to the one studied in
[
168
]
.

Uncertainty Principles:
Wilczok
[
209
]
proved that
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f
of a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\setminus\{0\}
cannot have a support of finite affine measure. The author also proved that the portion of
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f
lying outside some set
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
cannot be arbitrarily small. For sufficiently small
M,
this fact is justified by the following theorem, which can be proved by estimating the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of a suitably defined operator.

Theorem 11.1
.

(Concentration of
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f
on a small set) Let
\psi
be a window function and
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R}
with the assumption
\frac{\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{\sqrt{c_{\psi}}}\mu_{aff}(M)^{\frac{1}{2}}<1.
Then, for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
arbitrary

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f-\chi_{M}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R},\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}\right)}\geq\sqrt{c_{\psi}}\left(1-\frac{\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{\sqrt{c_{\psi}}}\mu_{aff}(M)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

(220)

It is to be noted that the lower bound of
\|\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f-\chi_{M}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
in (
221
) is larger the smaller
\mu_{aff}(M)
is. However, it can be seen that the above theorem requires the assumption that
\frac{\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{\sqrt{c_{\psi}}}\mu_{aff}(M)^{\frac{1}{2}}<1.
So a more general result is established with the assumption that
M
is an arbitrary subset of
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R}
with finite affine measure
\mu_{aff}
, i.e.,
for a given
\psi
and
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R}
with
\mu_{aff}(M)<\infty,
there exists a constant
C^{\mathcal{W}}_{\psi,M}>0
such that for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
arbitrary

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f-\chi_{M}\cdot\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\geq C^{\mathcal{W}}_{\psi,M}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

(221)

This result was established by Wilczok
[
209
]
using the abstract result of Havin and Jöricke
[
92
]
. However, the author in
[
209
]
did not give an estimate for the constant
C^{\mathcal{W}}_{\psi,M}
involved in (
221
) by the measure of
M.
Motivated by the idea of Singer
[
187
]
in the case of WT, Wilczok obtained a different class of UPs results for the comparing the localization of
f
and
\hat{f}
with the localization of the WT regarded as function of one variable as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}b^{2}|(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,b)|^{2}\frac{dadb}{|a|^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\xi^{2}|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{c_{\psi}}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

(222)

and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}a^{2}|(\tilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\psi}f)(a,b)|^{2}dadb\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x^{2}|f(x)|^{2}dx\geq\pi\left(\mathcal{M}\left(|\hat{\psi}|^{2}\right)\right)(2)\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

(223)

where
\mathcal{M}
denotes the classical Mellin transform given by
(\mathcal{M}f)(\sigma)=\int_{0}^{\infty}f(x)x^{-\sigma}\frac{dx}{x}.

12
Stockwell Transform (ST)

The S transform, also known as the ST in the literature, was first introduced by Stockwell et al.
[
193
]
in 1996. This transform is basically an extension of the ideas of the continuous WT, which is based on a moving and scalable localization Gaussian window and is defined as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}f)(\tau,\xi)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)\frac{|\xi|}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(\tau-t)^{2}\xi^{2}}{2}}e^{-i2\pi\xi t}dt.

(224)

One can infer from (
224
) that the width of the window function
e^{-\frac{(\tau-t)^{2}\xi^{2}}{2}}
shrinks as the analyzed frequency increases and provides a better time localization for high frequencies. This transform was introduced to provide frequency-dependent resolution while maintaining a direct relationship with the Fourier spectrum, which was absent in the continuous WT. The advantage of these transforms is due to the fact that the modulation sinusoidal is fixed with respect to the time axis, whereas the localizing scalable Gaussian window dilates and translates. If
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
then the ST given in equation (
224
) can be given in the Fourier domain as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}f)(\tau,\xi)=e^{-2\pi i\xi\tau}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{f}(v)e^{-\frac{2\pi^{2}(v-\xi)^{2}}{\xi^{2}}}e^{i2\pi\xi\tau}dv,\penalty\ \tau\in\mathbb{R},\xi\in\mathbb{R}_{0}.

(225)

Thus, the ST can be obtained as the Fourier multiplier
\mathcal{F}^{-1}e^{-\frac{2\pi^{2}(\cdot-\xi)^{2}}{\xi^{2}}}\mathcal{F}
followed by a modulation for non zero frequency
\xi,
here
(\mathcal{F}f)(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)e^{-2\pi it\xi}dt.
Moreover, the ST satisfy the property
A\mathcal{S}f=\hat{f},\penalty\ f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
where
A
is called the time average operator defined by

\displaystyle(AF)(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}F(\tau,\xi)d\tau,\penalty\ \xi\in\mathbb{R}

(226)

for the measurable function
F\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},
whenever the integral exists. As a result of which we have the inversion formula
f=\mathcal{F}^{-1}A\mathcal{S}f,
for all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
i.e.,

\displaystyle f(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\pi it\xi}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{S}f)(\tau,\xi)\right)d\xi,\penalty\ t\in\mathbb{R}

(227)

and also the Plancherel theorem for the ST as
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{S}f)(\tau,\xi)d\tau\right|^{2}d\xi=\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.
Wong et al.
[
210
]
considered the function space
M=\left\{F\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R\rightarrow\mathbb{C}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}F(\tau,\xi)d\tau\right|^{2}d\xi\right\},
which is indefinite Hilbert space with the indefinite inner product
\langle,\rangle_{M}
given by
\langle F,G\rangle_{M}=\langle AF,AG\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},\penalty\ F,G\in M,
where
AF
is the average-time of
F
given by (
226
). It is to be noted that
\langle,\rangle_{M}
is indefinite in the sense that there exist nonzero
F\in M
such that
\langle F,F\rangle_{M}=0.
They proved that the range
R(\mathcal{S})
of the ST
\mathcal{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\rightarrow M
is a Hilbert space with respect to the inner product
\langle,\rangle_{M}.
In fact,
\mathcal{S}
is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
onto
R(\mathcal{S}).
As a consequence of
A\mathcal{S}f=\hat{f}
and the Plancherel theorem for the ST, its range
R(\mathcal{S})
is characterized in
[
210
]
as
R(\mathcal{S})=M/Z,
where
Z=\{F\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow C\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}AF=0\}.

The ST with the general window in the place of the Gaussian function in the definition given in
[
193
]
can be defined below which is a hybrid of the Gabor transform and the WT

Definition 12.1
.

The ST of a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the window function
\varphi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is defined by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f)(b,\xi)=\frac{|\xi|}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ix\xi}f(x)\overline{\varphi(\xi(x-b))}dx,\penalty\ x\in\mathbb{R},\penalty\ \xi\in\mathbb{R}_{0}.

(228)

Equivalently, using the definition of translation, dilation and the modulation operators in
1
,
2
and
3
respectively, (
228
) has several important forms.

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f)(b,\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\overline{\mathcal{M}_{\xi}\tau_{b}\mathcal{D}_{\xi}\varphi(x)}dx

(229)

which can also be written in the form

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f)(b,\xi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\langle f,\varphi^{b,\xi}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

(230)

where
\varphi^{b,\xi}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathcal{M}_{\xi}\tau_{b}\mathcal{D}_{\xi}\varphi(x).
Another important, equivalent form of the definition of the WT (
205
) is in terms of the convolution given by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f)(b,\xi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{-\xi}f\star\mathcal{D}_{\xi}\tilde{\varphi}\right)(b),

(231)

where
\tilde{\psi}(t)=\overline{\psi(-t)}.
It is a matter of simple verification that
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f
is linear in
f
and conjugate linear in
\varphi.
The ST
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
has been extended to a family of modified ST
\mathcal{S}^{s}_{\varphi}
for
0<s\leq\infty
in
[
84
]
(also see
[
83
]
), which are defined as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}^{s}_{\varphi}f)(b,\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}|\xi|^{\frac{1}{s}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ix\xi}f(x)\overline{\varphi(\xi(x-b))}dx

(232)

\displaystyle=\langle f,\varphi_{s}^{b,\xi}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

(233)

where
\varphi_{s}^{b,\xi}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathcal{M}_{\xi}\tau_{b}\mathcal{D}^{s}_{\xi}\varphi(x)
and
{D}^{s}_{\xi}\varphi(x)=|\xi|^{\frac{1}{s}}\varphi(\xi x).
This family include the classical ST (
224
) when
s=1
and a variant of WT when
s=2.
The modified ST can be represented in the classical one as
(\mathcal{S}^{s}_{\varphi}f)(b,\xi)=|\xi|^{-\frac{1}{s^{\prime}}}(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f)(b,\xi),
where
\frac{1}{s}+\frac{1}{s^{\prime}}=1.
The modified ST satisfies the resolution of identity formula
[
84
]

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\frac{1}{c_{\varphi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{S}^{s}_{\varphi}f)(b,\xi)\overline{(\mathcal{S}^{s}_{\varphi}g)(b,\xi)}\frac{dbd\xi}{|\xi|^{1-\frac{1}{s^{\prime}}}},

(234)

where
\varphi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is such that
\int_{R}\varphi(x)dx=1
and

\displaystyle c_{\varphi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\hat{\varphi}(\xi-1)|^{2}}{|\xi|}d\xi<\infty,

(235)

with
\hat{\varphi}(\xi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ix\xi}\varphi(x)dx,\penalty\ \xi\in\mathbb{R}.
The modified ST
\mathcal{S}^{s}_{\varphi},\penalty\ 0<s\leq\infty,
for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
can be reconstructed from the time-frequency representation
\mathcal{S}^{s}_{\varphi}f
using the formula

\displaystyle f=\frac{1}{c_{\varphi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{S}^{s}_{\varphi}f)(b,\xi)\varphi^{b,\xi}_{s}\frac{dbd\xi}{|\xi|^{1-\frac{2}{s^{\prime}}}}.

(236)

For
s=1,
the resolution of identity formula (
234
) and the inversion formula (
236
) reduces to the one obtained by Du et al. in
[
64
]
. Moreover, if
\varphi
is a normalized window function such that
c_{\varphi}=1,
then for all
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\langle\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f,\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}g\rangle_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{0},\frac{dbd\xi}{|\xi|}\right)},
which implied that the transform
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{0},\frac{dbd\xi}{|\xi|}\right),
for all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and also
\|\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{0},\frac{dbd\xi}{|\xi|}\right)}=\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.
Thus, the transform
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
to the Hilbert space
L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{0},\frac{dbd\xi}{|\xi|}\right)
with its range
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
as a closed subspace. Furthermore, the range
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
is a RKHS with the kernel
K(b,\xi;b^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})=\overline{(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}^{b,\xi}\varphi^{b,\xi})(b^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})}=\langle\varphi^{b^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}},\varphi^{b,\xi}\rangle,
for
(b,\xi),(b^{\prime}\xi^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{0}
was proved in
[
102
]
.

So far, we have considered the ST in one dimension. We now see the multidimensional ST and explore the results that have been explored in the literature. For all
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
let
A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\left(a_{ij}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
be an invertible
n\times n
real matrix and let
\varphi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi(x)dx=1.
Then the
n-
dimensional ST
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f
of
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with respect to the window function
\varphi
is given by Riba et al.
[
167
]
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})=(2\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}|\det A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\boldsymbol{x})e^{-i\boldsymbol{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}\overline{\varphi(A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b}))}d\boldsymbol{x},\penalty\ (\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},

(237)

with the inversion formula as
f=\mathcal{F}^{-1}A\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f,
where
\mathcal{F}
is the inverse FT and the operator
A
is given by
(AF)(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})d\boldsymbol{b},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for all measurable function
F
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
provided the integral exists. It right hand side of equation (
237
) can be can be written in the form
\langle f,\varphi^{\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
with
\varphi^{\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}=(2\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\tau_{\boldsymbol{b}}\mathcal{D}_{A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\varphi,
where
(\mathcal{D}_{A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\varphi)(\boldsymbol{x})=|\det A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|\varphi(A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\boldsymbol{x}),

(\tau_{\boldsymbol{b}}\varphi)(\boldsymbol{x})=\varphi(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b})
and
(\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\varphi)(\boldsymbol{x})=e^{i\boldsymbol{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}\varphi.
Author in
[
167
]
, gave a resolution of identity formula for the
n-
dimensional ST associated with some special class of matrices, which are discussed below:

Let
A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in GL(n,\mathbb{R}),\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
satisfying the conditions

(i)

\boldsymbol{\eta}=\left(A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{-1}\right)^{t}\boldsymbol{\zeta}
for some
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
then there exists positive functions
f_{1},f_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\left|\det\left(\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right)\right|=\frac{f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\eta})}{f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\xi})},\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
where
\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\xi}}
is the Jacobian matrix of
\boldsymbol{\eta}
with respect to
\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\xi}}

(ii)

there exists a vector
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\left(A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{-1}\right)^{t}\boldsymbol{\xi}=\boldsymbol{v},

then, for such matrices the following resolution of identity holds

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})\overline{(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}g)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})}d\boldsymbol{b}\frac{d\boldsymbol{\xi}}{f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\xi})}=c_{\varphi}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

(238)

for all
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
where relation
c_{\varphi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\hat{\varphi}\left(\left(A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{-1}\right)^{t}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\boldsymbol{\xi})\right)\right|^{2}\frac{d\boldsymbol{\xi}}{f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\xi})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\hat{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{v})\right|^{2}\frac{d\boldsymbol{\eta}}{f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\eta})}<\infty.
Furthermore, if for all
\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots,\xi_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\penalty\ \xi_{l}\neq 0,l=1,2,\cdots,n,
we consider the matrices
I_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\delta_{ll^{\prime}})_{n\times n},\delta_{ll}=\xi_{l}
and
\delta_{ll^{\prime}}=0,
if
l\neq l^{\prime},
the the corresponding transform is called the non-isotropic
n-
dimensional ST given as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})=(2\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\Pi_{l=1}^{n}|\xi_{l}|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-i\boldsymbol{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}f(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\varphi\left(I_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b})\right)}d\boldsymbol{x}

(239)

and the corresponding resolution of identity is given as equation (
238
) with
f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\Pi_{l=1}^{n}|\xi_{l}|
and
c_{\varphi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\hat{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\xi}-\boldsymbol{1})\right|^{2}\frac{d\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\Pi_{l=1}^{n}|\xi_{l}|},
where
\boldsymbol{1}=(1,1,\cdots,1)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
These results, in
[
167
]
, extend those for the two-dimensional ST studied by Liu et al. in
[
128
]
. Hleili et al.
[
101
]
proved that the transform
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f
given in (
239
) have the property that for window functions
\varphi
and
\psi,

(\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}f)(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}g)\in L^{p}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\frac{d\boldsymbol{b}d\boldsymbol{\xi}}{|\det I_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|}\right)
for all
p\in[1,\infty]
and
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Riba et al.
[
167
]
also introduced a special class of matrices
A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
satisfying

(I)

\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{\xi}|}A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in O(n,\mathbb{R})
for every
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\},

(II)

there exists a matrix
P\in O(n,\mathbb{R})
such that
A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\boldsymbol{\zeta}=PA_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\boldsymbol{\xi},

(III)

there exists a vector
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\left(A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{-1}\right)^{t}\boldsymbol{\xi}=\boldsymbol{v}.

This class of matrices are such that
A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in GL(n,\mathbb{R}),\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}_{0}
and it satisfies both the property
(i)
and
(ii)
. The matrices

A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{2\times 2}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}&\xi_{2}\\
-\xi_{2}&\xi_{1}\end{pmatrix},

A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{4\times 4}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}&\xi_{2}&\xi_{3}&\xi_{4}\\
-\xi_{2}&\xi_{1}&\xi_{4}&-\xi_{3}\\
-\xi_{3}&-\xi_{4}&\xi_{1}&\xi_{2}\\
-\xi_{4}&\xi_{3}&-\xi_{2}&\xi_{1}\end{pmatrix}

and

A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{8\times 8}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}&\xi_{2}&\xi_{3}&\xi_{4}&\xi_{5}&\xi_{6}&\xi_{7}&\xi_{8}\\
-\xi_{2}&\xi_{1}&\xi_{4}&-\xi_{3}&\xi_{6}&-\xi_{5}&-\xi_{8}&\xi_{7}\\
-\xi_{3}&-\xi_{4}&\xi_{1}&\xi_{2}&\xi_{7}&\xi_{8}&-\xi_{5}&-\xi_{6}\\
-\xi_{4}&\xi_{3}&-\xi_{2}&\xi_{1}&\xi_{8}&-\xi_{7}&\xi_{6}&-\xi_{5}\\
-\xi_{5}&-\xi_{6}&-\xi_{7}&-\xi_{8}&\xi_{1}&\xi_{2}&\xi_{3}&\xi_{4}\\
-\xi_{6}&\xi_{5}&-\xi_{8}&\xi_{7}&-\xi_{2}&\xi_{1}&-\xi_{4}&\xi_{3}\\
-\xi_{7}&\xi_{8}&\xi_{5}&-\xi_{6}&-\xi_{3}&\xi_{4}&\xi_{1}&-\xi_{2}\\
-\xi_{8}&-\xi_{7}&\xi_{6}&\xi_{5}&-\xi_{4}&-\xi_{3}&\xi_{2}&\xi_{1}\end{pmatrix}

of order
2,

4
and
8
respectively satisfy properties
(I)
,
(II)
and
(III)
. Authors in
[
166
]
explained that the family
A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in GL(n,\mathbb{R}),\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}_{0}
for which the properties
(i)
and
(ii)
hold cannot be satisfied for the dimensions apart from
1,2,4
and
8.
They also extended the multidimensional ST to the multidimensional modified ST. For that, they considered the multidimensional dilation operator
\mathcal{D}^{s}_{A}\varphi,\penalty\ 1\leq s<\infty,
defined for
A\in GL(n,\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}^{s}_{A}\varphi)(\boldsymbol{x})=|\det A|^{-\frac{1}{s}}\varphi(A^{-1}\boldsymbol{x}),\penalty\ \boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

(240)

for all measurable function
\varphi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
In particular,
\mathcal{D}^{2}_{A}
is a unitary operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Also let,
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow GL(n,\mathbb{R}),
be given by
\boldsymbol{\xi}\mapsto A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}.
Then the multi-dimensional modified ST of
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with respect to
\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
was given by Riba et al.
[
166
]
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}^{s}_{A,\varphi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})=(2\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}|\det A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|^{-\frac{1}{s}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\boldsymbol{x})e^{-i\boldsymbol{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}\overline{\varphi(A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b}))}d\boldsymbol{x},\penalty\ (\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},

(241)

which can be expressed in several equivalent form as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}^{s}_{A,\varphi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})

\displaystyle=|\det A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s}}(\mathcal{S}^{2}_{A,\varphi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})

(242)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}|\det A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s}}\langle f,\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\tau_{\boldsymbol{b}}\mathcal{D}^{s}_{A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

(243)

Equation (
241
) can also be expressed in terms of the FT
(\mathcal{F}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=(2\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\boldsymbol{x})e^{-2\pi i\boldsymbol{x}.\boldsymbol{\xi}}d\boldsymbol{t}
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}^{s}_{A,\varphi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})=|\det A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|^{1-\frac{1}{s}}e^{-i\boldsymbol{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left(\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}\mapsto\boldsymbol{b}}f_{\boldsymbol{\xi},A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\right)(\boldsymbol{b}),\penalty\ \boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(244)

where
f_{\boldsymbol{\xi},A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta})=\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\overline{\hat{\varphi}\left(A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\boldsymbol{\xi})\right)},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\zeta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Moreover, the inversion formula for the modified ST of
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and the window function
\varphi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfying
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})d\boldsymbol{x}=1
can be obtained from the relation
\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\left|\det A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right|^{\frac{1}{s}-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathcal{S}^{s}_{A,\varphi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})d\boldsymbol{b},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Authors in
[
166
]
also obtained the resolution of identity formula for
\mathcal{S}^{s}_{A,\varphi}
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathcal{S}^{s}_{A,\varphi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})\overline{(\mathcal{S}^{s}_{A,\varphi}g)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi})}d\boldsymbol{b}\left|\det A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right|^{\frac{2}{s}-1}d\boldsymbol{\xi}=c_{\varphi}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

(245)

for all
f,g,\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
c_{\varphi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\hat{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}\frac{d\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\left|\det\left(F^{i}_{jk}\xi^{j}+\delta^{i}_{k}\right)\right|}<\infty,
where
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow GL(n,\mathbb{R})
be given by
\boldsymbol{\xi}\mapsto A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
is a piecewise differentiable function for which there exist fixed
(1,2)-
tensor
F
and a fixed
(1,1)-
tensor
G
such that
\left(A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t}\right)^{-1}=\left(F^{i}_{jk}\xi^{j}+\delta^{i}_{k}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
and
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{t}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\boldsymbol{\xi}),\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is such that
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathbb{R}^{n}
for all
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
These assumptions on the map
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow GL(n,\mathbb{R}^{n})
are satisfied by the matrix-valued functions
A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{2\times 2},

A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{4\times 4}
and
A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{8\times 8}
mentioned earlier. Moreover, the equation (
245
) is applicable in all the dimensions to the constant and diagonal matrix-valued functions
I_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\delta_{ll^{\prime}})_{n\times n},\delta_{ll}=\xi_{l}
and
\delta_{ll^{\prime}}=0,
if
l\neq l^{\prime}.

The two-dimensional ST given in
[
128
]
is inadequate for capturing anisotropic features like corners and edges of signals due to the directional insensitivity of the window function. So, Shah et al.
[
176
]
proposed a novel transform called the non-isotropic angular ST with respect to a window function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
defined as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{M}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\langle f,\psi^{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

(246)

for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
with

\displaystyle\psi^{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\boldsymbol{t})=E_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}R_{\theta}\mathcal{D}_{A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\tau_{\boldsymbol{b}}\psi(\boldsymbol{t})=|\det A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|e^{i\boldsymbol{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}\psi(R_{-\theta}A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\boldsymbol{t}-\boldsymbol{b})),

(247)

where
(\mathcal{D}_{A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\psi)(\boldsymbol{t})=|\det A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|\psi(A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\boldsymbol{t}),

(E_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\psi)(\boldsymbol{t})=e^{i\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\boldsymbol{t}}\psi(\boldsymbol{t}),

\tau_{\boldsymbol{b}}\psi)(\boldsymbol{t})=\psi(\boldsymbol{t}-\boldsymbol{b}),

(R_{\theta}\psi)(\boldsymbol{t})=\psi(R_{-\theta}\boldsymbol{t}),

A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}&0\\
0&\xi_{2}\end{pmatrix},\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\neq 0
and
R_{\theta}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix},

\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\theta\in[0,2\pi).
Apart from the several important properties like linearity, anti-linearity, translation, scaling, reflection and conjugation, authors expressed the non-isotropic angular ST in the Fourier domain as
(\mathcal{M}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},\theta)=e^{-i\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\boldsymbol{b}}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\overline{\hat{\psi}\left((\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\xi})A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{-1}R_{\theta}\right)}\right](\boldsymbol{b})
and obtained the following resolution of identity formula

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}(\mathcal{M}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},\theta)\overline{(\mathcal{M}_{\psi}g)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},\theta)}d\theta d\boldsymbol{\xi}d\boldsymbol{b}=C_{\psi}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\penalty\ f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

(248)

C_{\psi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left|(\mathcal{F}\psi)\left((\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\xi})A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{-1}R_{\theta}\right)\right|^{2}d\theta d\boldsymbol{\xi}<\infty.
This in particular, for
f=g,
implies that
\|\mathcal{M}_{\psi}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=C_{\psi}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},
which further shows that for the function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying
C_{\psi}=1,

\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
into
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}\times[0,2\pi))
and hence it is one-one. Moreover,
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is a proper subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}\times[0,2\pi)).
Thus, square integrability of
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}f
is necessary condition but not sufficient. The range
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})),
of bounded linear operator
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}\times[0,2\pi))
which is isometric when
C_{\psi}=1,
is a closed subspace. Being closed subspace of the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}\times[0,2\pi)),

\mathcal{M}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is also a Hilbert space. Furthermore, it is a RKHS, with the kernel given by
K_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},\theta;\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{\eta},\theta^{\prime})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\langle\psi^{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi}},\psi^{\theta^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{\eta}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.
The proposed transform (
246
), has the following inversion formula

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{t})=\frac{1}{2\pi C_{\psi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}(\mathcal{M}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},\theta)\psi^{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\boldsymbol{t})d\theta d\boldsymbol{\xi}d\boldsymbol{b},\penalty\ a.e.

(249)

where
\psi^{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\boldsymbol{t})
is given in (
247
).

Uncertainty Principles:
Motivated by the idea of Singer
[
187
]
and Wilczok
[
209
]
in the case of WFT and WT, authors in
[
176
]
obtained a class of UPs results for comparing the localization of
f
and
\mathcal{F}{f}
with the localization of the non-isotropic angular ST regarded as function of one variable as

R_{\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\omega}}\geq\frac{C_{\psi}}{2}\|f\|^{2}_{2}
and
R_{\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{t}}\geq\frac{C_{\psi}}{2}\|f\|^{2}_{2},
where

R_{\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\omega}}=\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\boldsymbol{b}|^{2}|(\mathcal{M}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},\theta)|^{2}d\theta d\boldsymbol{\xi}d\boldsymbol{b}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{\omega}|^{2}|(\mathcal{F}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}

and

R_{\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{t}}=\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2}|(\mathcal{M}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},\theta)|^{2}d\theta d\boldsymbol{\xi}d\boldsymbol{b}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2}|f(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

As a consequence of which we have
R_{\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\omega}}R_{\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{t}}\geq\frac{C_{\psi}}{2}\|f\|^{2}_{2},
which shows that the phase-space localization of the pair
(f,\mathcal{F}f)
varies inversely with the phase space localization of the transform (
246
), i.e., better the phase-space localization of
(f,\mathcal{F}f)
, the worse is the phase-space localization of
(\mathcal{M}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},\theta)
and vice versa. In fact, a more general version for
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is proved by the authors as

\displaystyle\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\boldsymbol{b}|^{p}|(\mathcal{M}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},\theta)|^{p}d\theta d\boldsymbol{\xi}d\boldsymbol{b}\right\}^{\frac{1}{p}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{\omega}|^{p}|(\mathcal{F}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega})|^{p}d\boldsymbol{\omega}\right\}^{\frac{1}{p}}\geq\frac{C_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2}\|f\|^{2}_{2},\penalty\ 1\leq p\leq 2

(250)

and

\displaystyle\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\boldsymbol{b}|^{p}|(\mathcal{M}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},\theta)|^{2}d\theta d\boldsymbol{\xi}d\boldsymbol{b}\right\}^{\frac{1}{p}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{\omega}|^{p}|(\mathcal{F}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}\right\}^{\frac{1}{p}}\geq\frac{C_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{p}}}{2}\|f\|^{\frac{4}{p}}_{2},\penalty\ p\geq 2

(251)

following the idea of Cowling and Price
[
55
]
. Furthermore, they also obtained the following logarithmic UP for the transform (
246
) using the Pitt’s inequality
[
36
]

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\ln|\boldsymbol{b}||(\mathcal{M}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\xi},\theta)|^{2}d\theta d\boldsymbol{\xi}d\boldsymbol{b}+C_{\psi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|\boldsymbol{\omega}||(\mathcal{F}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}\geq C_{\psi}\|f\|^{2}_{2}\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}-\ln\pi\right).

(252)

13
Short time fractional Fourier transform (STFrFT)

The FrFT only reveals the overall fractional Fourier domain (FrFD) frequency content of the signal. Sometimes, we want to know the FrFD-frequency contents and how they change over time. So, the representation combining the time and FrFD-frequency information should be developed. A localized FrFT was developed in
[
136
]
,
[
215
]
,
[
144
]
based on which time/space-dependent FrFD filtering approach was obtained. But they cannot be regarded as the time-FrFD-frequency representation since it has no explicit
2-D
domain. A kind of STFrFT was proposed in
[
48
]
. It modifies the classical STFrFT by utilizing a dechirp window whose chirp rate is obtained by the FrFT in a searching scheme. An adaptive STFrFT was given in
[
222
]
for the function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the FrFT domain window function
g(\omega^{\prime})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f)(\omega,t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\omega^{\prime})\overline{g(\omega^{\prime}-\omega)}e^{-i\omega^{\prime}t}d\omega^{\prime},

(253)

which rotates the signal in the time-frequency domain by the FrFT before implementing the STFT. They could be classified as a kind of adaptive STFT rather than time-FrFD-frequency representation because they analyze signals in the time-frequency domain but not in the time-FrFD-frequency domain. Such a definition was independently derived by Stankovic̀ et al.
[
191
]
for time-frequency analysis of chirp signals. From equation (
253
), it follows that the STFrFT, which is given in
[
191
]
,
[
222
]
looses all time-varying information and hence cannot be viewed as a joint time-FrFD-frequency analysis transform. As a kind of time-FrFD-frequency representation, the different STFrFT was in Zhang et al.
[
223
]
and Tao et al.
[
196
]
, which is defined for functions
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the window function
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\left({}^{(0)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(\omega,t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)g(x-t)K_{\alpha}(x,\omega)dx.

(254)

The transform (
254
) is linear, which indicates that it satisfies the superposition principle, which is favorable for the analysis of multicomponent signals. It satisfies the time marginal constraints, i.e., integrating the STFrFT with respect to the time variable gives the LCT multiplied by a constant, i.e.,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left({}^{(0)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(\omega,t)dt=C(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\omega),
for some constant
C
and the additivity of rotation, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}\left(\left({}^{(0)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(\omega,t)\right)(\omega^{\prime},t)=\left({}^{(0)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha+\beta}_{g}f\right)(\omega^{\prime},t).
Apart from this, author in
[
196
]
proved the covariance properties

\left({}^{(0)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}[e^{i\omega_{0}x}f(x-t_{0})]\right)(\omega,t)=e^{i\phi(\omega)}\left({}^{(0)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(\omega-(t_{0}\cos\alpha+\omega_{0}\sin\alpha),t-t_{0}),

\phi(\omega)=(\omega_{0}\cos\alpha-t_{0}\sin\alpha)\omega+\frac{t_{0}^{2}-\omega_{0}^{2}}{2}\sin\alpha\cos\alpha+t_{0}\omega_{0}\sin^{2}\alpha.
With the assumption that the window function satisfies the conditions
g(0)\neq 0,
the
1-D
inversion formula is

\displaystyle f(t)=\frac{1}{g(0)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left({}^{(0)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(\omega,t)\overline{K_{\alpha}(t,\omega)}d\omega

(255)

and under the assumption that
\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=1,
the
2-D
inversion formula is
f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left({}^{(0)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(\omega,t)g(x-t)\overline{K_{\alpha}(x,\omega)}dtd\omega
are also obtained in
[
196
]
. Based on the fractional convolution
\star_{\alpha},
Shi et al.
[
185
]
obtained a novel STFrFT which is defined for function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the window function
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(\omega,t)

\displaystyle=e^{-it\omega\csc\alpha}\left[f(x)\star_{\alpha}\left(\overline{g(-x)}e^{ix\omega\csc\alpha}\right)\right](\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\overline{g_{\alpha,t,\omega}(x)}dx,

(256)

where
g_{\alpha,t,\omega}(x)=g(x-t)e^{-i\frac{x^{2}-t^{2}}{2}\cot\alpha+ix\omega\csc\alpha}.
This definition can be equivalently written in fractional Fourier domain as

\displaystyle\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(\omega,t)=e^{-it\omega\csc\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\omega^{\prime})\overline{(\mathcal{F}g)((\omega^{\prime}-\omega)\csc\alpha)}\overline{K_{\alpha}(\omega^{\prime},t)}d\omega^{\prime},

(257)

where
(\mathcal{F}g)(\omega^{\prime}\csc\alpha)
denotes the FT of
g
with its argument scaled by
\csc\alpha.
Transform (
13
) is linear in
f
and anti-linear in
g.
It satisfy the property called the additivity of rotation i.e.,
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}\left\{A_{\alpha}e^{\frac{t^{2}+\omega^{2}}{2}\cot\alpha}\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(\omega,t)\right\}(\omega^{\prime},t)=\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha+\beta}_{g}f\right)(\omega^{\prime},t),

A_{\alpha}=\sqrt{\frac{1-i\cot\alpha}{2\pi}}.
Apart from this, it has the following covariance properties

\displaystyle\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}\left(\tau_{x_{0}}f\right)\right)(\omega,t)=(^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f)(\omega,t-t_{0})e^{i\left(\frac{3}{2}x_{0}^{2}-x_{0}x\right)\cot\alpha-it_{0}\omega\csc\alpha}

and

\displaystyle\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}\left(e^{i\omega_{0}x}f(x)\right)\right)(\omega,t)=\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(\omega-\omega_{0}\sin\alpha,t).

The covariance property obtained above indeed gives the formula for the WQPFT for the time and frequency shift of a function
f.
For
f_{1},f_{2},g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
the WQPFT (
13
) satisfies the following inner product relation

\displaystyle\csc\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f_{1}\right)(\omega,t)\overline{\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f_{2}\right)(\omega,t)}dtd\omega=\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle.

(258)

In particular, if
f_{1}=f_{2}=f
then
(\csc\alpha)\left\|{}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
and thus WQPFT is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
upto some constant, this is called the energy conservation property. With the assumption that the time domain window function
g
satisfying the condition
g(0)\neq 0,
author obtained the
1-D
inversion formula for the transform (
13
) as
f(t)=\frac{\csc\alpha}{2\pi\overline{g(0)}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(\omega,t)e^{it\omega\csc\alpha}d\omega
and also for the time domain window function
\gamma(x),

h(x)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{\csc\alpha}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(\omega,t)\gamma_{\alpha,t,\omega}(x)dtd\omega
is such that if
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma(x)\overline{g(x)}dx=1
then
h(x)=f(x).
There are several choices of the window function
\gamma(x)
that satisfies
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma(x)\overline{g(x)}dx=1.
One such important choice is that
\gamma(x)=g(x).
For which
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(x)|^{2}dx=1
and the
2-D
reconstruction formula for the WLCT is given as

\displaystyle f(x)=\frac{\csc\alpha}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(t,\omega)g_{\alpha,t,\omega}(x)dtd\omega.

(259)

The transform (
13
) represents a
1-D
signal
f(x)
using the
2-D
function
\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(x,u).
Based on the energy conservation property, it follows that
{}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
It is not in general true that, a function
F
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a WQPFT of some function in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
The author in
[
185
]
characterized the range
{}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
of the transform (
13
) showing that
F\in\penalty\ ^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
if and only if it satisfies
F(t^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}F(t,\omega)\langle g_{\alpha,t,\omega},g_{\alpha,t^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}dtd\omega.
Consequently, it follows that the range
{}^{(1)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
is a RKHS with the kernel
\frac{1}{2\pi}\langle g_{\alpha,t,\omega},g_{\alpha,t^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.
In view of equation (
13
) and its representation in the fractional Fourier domain (
257
), the WQPFT gives the local information of
f(x)
in the time window
\left[E_{g}+t-\Delta_{g},E_{g}+t+\Delta_{g}\right]
and the local information of
f(x)
in the fractional Fourier domain
\left[E_{G}\sin\alpha+\omega-\Delta_{G}\sin\alpha,E_{G}\sin\alpha+\omega+\Delta_{G}\sin\alpha\right]
, where
G
is the FT of
g
having the constant window area
4\Delta_{g}\Delta_{G}\sin\alpha
in the time-fractional-frequency plane with the optimal window function as
g_{\sigma}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sqrt{\pi}\sigma}}e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}},
where
\sigma
is a positive number. Kamalakkannan et al.
[
108
]
proposed the short time coupled FrFT for the function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with respect to a non-zero
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and the parameters
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\alpha+\beta\neq 2n\pi,\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{Z}
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}^{\alpha,\beta}_{g}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{t})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\boldsymbol{x})g(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{t})K_{\alpha,\beta}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{\omega})d\boldsymbol{y}.

(260)

The authors proved the linearity, time delay, frequency shift, and addition property of the transform. On applying the equivalent form
(\mathcal{G}^{\alpha,\beta}_{g}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{t})=(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}\left(f\tau_{\boldsymbol{t}}\bar{g}\right))(\boldsymbol{\omega}),

\tau_{\boldsymbol{t}}\bar{g}(\boldsymbol{y})=\overline{g(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{t})}
of (
260
), authors obtained the Parseval’s formula
\left\langle\mathcal{G}^{\alpha,\beta}_{g}f_{1},\mathcal{G}^{\alpha,\beta}_{g}f_{2}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\left\langle f_{1},f_{2}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},
and the inversion formula

f=\frac{1}{\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\mathcal{G}^{\alpha,\beta}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{t})M_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\alpha,\beta}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{t}}g)d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{t},

weakly in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
where
M_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\alpha,\beta}(h)(\boldsymbol{y})=h(\boldsymbol{y})\overline{K_{\alpha,\beta}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{\omega})}.
They also characterized the range
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha,\beta}_{g}\left(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\right)
and proved that it is a reproducing kernel Hilbert space with the kernel
\frac{2\pi}{\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}}\left\langle M_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\alpha,\beta}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}g),M_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime},\alpha,\beta}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}}g)\right\rangle

Uncertainty Principles:
Using relation (
54
) for
n=1,
author in
[
79
]
obtained the Heisenberg UP for the STFrFT (
253
) as

\displaystyle\Delta t^{2}_{\alpha}\Delta\omega^{2}_{\beta}\geq\frac{\left(|\cos(\alpha-\beta)|+1\right)^{2}}{4},

(261)

where
t_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}t\left|(\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f)(\omega,t)\right|^{2}dtd\omega
and
\omega_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega\left|(\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f)(\omega,t)\right|^{2}dtd\omega
are expected values of the variables
t
and
\omega
of the spectrogram
\left|(\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f)(\omega,t)\right|^{2}
respectively.
\Delta t^{2}_{\alpha}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(t-t_{0})^{2}\left|(\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f)(\omega,t)\right|^{2}dtd\omega
and
\Delta\omega^{2}_{\alpha}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\omega-\omega_{0})^{2}\left|(\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f)(\omega,t)\right|^{2}dtd\omega
are the spread for the variables
t
and
\omega
respectively. It can be seen from (
261
) the UP is also connected with the parameters
\alpha
