and
\beta.
In particular for
\alpha=\beta,
the UP reads
\Delta t^{2}_{\alpha}\Delta\omega^{2}_{\beta}\geq 1.
With the assumptions that
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
are real valued and
P_{l}f,\penalty\ P_{l}\hat{f},\penalty\ P_{l}g,\penalty\ P_{l}\hat{g}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

P_{l}(\boldsymbol{x})=x_{l},\penalty\ l=1,2,
authors in
[
108
]
, obtained the Heisenberg UP for the short time coupled FrFT (
260
) in
\boldsymbol{\omega}
as

\displaystyle\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}^{2}_{\alpha,\beta}\Delta\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{f}\geq\cos^{2}\gamma\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\left(\Delta\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{f}\right)^{2}+\sin^{2}(\gamma)\left(\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\left(|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{0}_{f}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{0}_{g}|^{2}\right)\Delta\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{f}+\frac{\Delta\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{f}}{\Delta\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{g}}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\right)\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

where
\gamma=\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2},

\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}^{2}_{\alpha,\beta}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\alpha,\beta,0}|^{2}\left|(\mathcal{G}^{\alpha,\beta}_{g}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{t})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{t}
with
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\alpha,\beta,0}=(\omega_{1}^{\alpha,\beta,0},\omega_{2}^{\alpha,\beta,0}),

\omega_{l}^{\alpha,\beta,0}=\frac{1}{\|\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{l}\left|\left(\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{t})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{t},\penalty\ l=1,2

and
\Delta\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{f},\penalty\ \Delta\boldsymbol{\xi}^{2}_{f}
are given respectively by (
52
) and (
53
).

14
Fractional wavelet transform (FrWT)

The concept of FrWT was proposed by Mendlovic et al.
[
137
]
, where fractional FT is firstly used to derive the fractional spectrum of a signal, and the WT is then performed on the obtained fractional spectrum. Since the fractional spectrum derived by the FrFT only represents the FrFD-frequency over the entire duration of the signal, the FRWT defined in
[
137
]
actually fails in obtaining the information of the local property of the signal. To overcome this drawback, using the relation (
208
), between the WT and the classical convolution, Shi et al.
[
184
]
defined a novel FrWT by replacing the classical convolution by the fractional convolution (
3
) as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=

\displaystyle\left(f\star_{\alpha}\delta_{a}\tilde{\psi}\right)(b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\psi_{\alpha,a,b}(t)}dt,

(262)

where

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha,a,b}(t)=e^{-\frac{i}{2}(t^{2}-b^{2})\cot\alpha}\psi_{a,b}(t)

(263)

and
\psi_{a,b}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\psi\left(\frac{t-b}{a}\right).

Equation (
14
) can be expressed in terms of the FrFT of
f
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sqrt{2\pi a}(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)\overline{\hat{\psi}(a\xi\csc\alpha)}\mathcal{K}_{-\alpha}(\xi,b)d\xi

(264)

It is to be noted that for
\alpha=1,
the FrWT (
14
) reduces to the classical WT. It is a matter of simple verification that
\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f
is linear in
f
and conjugate linear in
\psi.
The following properties, called the covariance properties of the fraction WT, give the formula for the FrWT of the dilation by
c>0
of the function
f
with respect to
\psi

\displaystyle[(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}(f(ct))](a,b)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{c}}(\mathcal{W}^{\beta}_{\psi}f)\left(ca,cb\right),\penalty\ a\in\mathbb{R}^{+},b\in\mathbb{R}

(265)

where
\varphi=\cot^{-1}(c^{2}\cot\alpha)
and
\beta=\varphi/(\frac{\pi}{2}).
Bahri et al.
[
22
]
obtained the fractional convolution and the correlation results associated with the FrWT as

(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi_{1}\star_{\alpha}\psi_{2}}(f\star_{\alpha}g))(a,b)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi_{1}}f)(a,b-u)(\mathcal{W}^{-\alpha}_{\psi_{2}}g)(a,u)du

and

(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi_{1}\circ_{\alpha}\psi_{2}}(f\circ_{\alpha}g))(a,b)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{(\mathcal{W}^{-\alpha}_{\psi_{1}}f)(a,-b+u)}(\mathcal{W}^{-\alpha}_{\psi_{2}}g)(a,u)du,

respectively, where
\circ_{\alpha}
is the fractional correlation given as
(f\circ_{\alpha}g)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\frac{i}{2}(x^{2}+y^{2})\cot\alpha}\overline{f(y)}g(x+y)dy.
Authors in
[
184
]
obtained the resolution of identity formula for the transform (
14
) as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}g)(a,b)}\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}=2\pi c_{\psi}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},\penalty\ f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

(266)

where
c_{\psi}
is a positive constant depending on wavelet given as
c_{\psi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\hat{\psi}(\xi)|^{2}}{\xi}d\xi<\infty,
where
\hat{\psi}(\xi)
denotes the FT of
\psi(t).
This in particular, for
f=g,
implies that
\|\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R},\frac{dadb}{a^{2}})}=2\pi c_{\psi}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},
which further shows that the normalized WT
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi c_{\psi}}}\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}
is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
into
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R},d\mu_{aff}),
where
d\mu_{aff}=\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}
denotes the so called affine measure. Furthermore, the corresponding inversion formula is given by

\displaystyle f(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi c_{\psi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\psi_{\alpha,a,b}(t)\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}

(267)

From equations (
14
) and (
264
), it follows that if the kernel
\psi_{\alpha,a,b}(t)
is supported in the time domain, the
(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)
is supported in the time domain. Thus
(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)
contains information about
f(t)
near
b.
Similarly, if
\overline{\hat{\psi}}(a\xi\csc\alpha)
is bandpass, i.e.,
\hat{\psi}(\xi)
satisfies the condition
c_{\psi}<\infty,

then the multiplication
\hat{f}(\xi)
and
\overline{\hat{\psi}}(a\xi\csc\alpha)
can provide fractional-domain local properties of
f(t)
. This implies that the FrWT is capable of providing the time- and fractional-domain information simultaneously, hence giving a time-fractional-frequency representation of the signal. To be specific, both
\psi(t)
and its FT
\hat{\psi}(\xi)
must have sufficiently fast decay so that they can be used as window functions. Suppose that
\psi(t)
and
\hat{\psi}(\xi)
are functions with finite centers
E_{\psi}
and
E_{\hat{\psi}}
and finite radii
\Delta_{\psi}
and
\Delta_{\hat{\psi}}
. Then, the center and radii of the time-domain window function
\psi_{\alpha,a,b}(t)
of the FrWT are respectively given by
E[\psi_{\alpha,a,b}(t)]=b+aE_{\psi}
and
\Delta[\psi_{\alpha,a,b}(t)]=a\Delta_{\psi},
where
E[\cdot]
and
\Delta[\cdot]
denote the expectation and deviation operator respectively. Similarly, the center and radii of the frequency-domain window function
\hat{\psi}(a\xi\csc\alpha)
of the WT can be easily derived as
E[\hat{\psi}(a\xi\csc\alpha)]=\frac{E_{\hat{\psi}}}{a}
and
\Delta[\hat{\psi}(a\xi\csc\alpha)]=\frac{\Delta_{\hat{\psi}}}{a}.
The
Q-
factor (or the ratio between the width and the center) of the fractional-domain window function of the FrWT is given by
Q=\frac{\Delta_{\hat{\psi}}}{E_{\hat{\psi}}},
which is independent of the order
\alpha
and the scaling parameter
a.
This is the constant
-Q
property of the FrWT. Thus, the FrWT gives the local information of the signal
f(t)
in the time window
[b+aE_{\psi}-a\Delta_{\psi},b+aE_{\psi}+a\Delta_{\psi}]
and the local FrFD spectrum information in the fractional frequency window
\left[\frac{E_{\hat{\psi}}}{a}-\frac{\Delta_{\hat{\psi}}}{a},\frac{E_{\hat{\psi}}}{a}+\frac{\Delta_{\hat{\psi}}}{a}\right]\sin\alpha,
resulting into the time-fractional-frequency window

\displaystyle[b+aE_{\psi}-a\Delta_{\psi},b+aE_{\psi}+a\Delta_{\psi}]\times\left[\frac{E_{\hat{\psi}}}{a}-\frac{\Delta_{\hat{\psi}}}{a},\frac{E_{\hat{\psi}}}{a}+\frac{\Delta_{\hat{\psi}}}{a}\right]\cdot\sin\alpha

(268)

with constant area
4\Delta_{\psi}\Delta_{\hat{\psi}}\sin\alpha
in the time-FrFD-frequency plane. The area depends only on the mother wavelet
\psi(t)
and the parameter
\alpha
but is independent of the parameters
a
and
b.
However, for a given
\alpha,
the time-fractional-frequency window varies with the scaling parameter
a.
More precisely, the window becomes narrower for detecting high FrFD-frequency phenomena, i.e., small values of
a
, and wider for detecting low FrFD-frequency phenomena, i.e., large
a.

In view of (
264
), it follows the FrWT (
14
) analyzes the signal in time-frequency-FrFD domain, its physical meaning requires deeper interpretation. So, Prasad et al.
[
160
]
expressed the FrWT (
14
) in the fractional Fourier domain as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\sqrt{a}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\frac{i}{2}(b^{2}+\xi^{2})\cot\alpha+ib\xi\csc\alpha+\frac{i}{2}a^{2}\xi^{2}\cot\alpha}(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)\overline{\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}\left[e^{-\frac{i}{2}(\cdot)^{2}\cot\alpha}\psi\right](a\xi)}\mathcal{K}_{-\alpha}(\xi,b)d\xi

(269)

as a result, the signal analysis only involves the time-FrFD domain. Moreover, in that case, the wavelet satisfies the following admissibility condition

\displaystyle c_{\psi,\alpha}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{|\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}\left(e^{-\frac{i}{2}(\cdot)\cot\alpha}\psi\right)(\xi)|^{2}}{|\xi|}d\xi<\infty.

(270)

Authors in
[
160
]
, generalized the inner product relation (
266
) in terms of two wavelets
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi_{1}}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi_{2}}g)(a,b)}\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}=2\pi\sin\alpha c_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\alpha}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},\penalty\ f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

(271)

where
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are such that
C_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\alpha}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\overline{\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}\left[e^{-\frac{i}{2}(\cdot)^{2}\cot{\alpha}}\psi_{1}\right](a)}\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}\left[e^{-\frac{i}{2}(\cdot)^{2}\cot{\alpha}}\psi_{2}\right](a)\frac{da}{a}<\infty.
From (
271
) it follows that, if
\psi_{1}=\psi_{2}=\psi
then we have
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}g)(a,b)}\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}=2\pi\sin\alpha c_{\psi,\alpha}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.
Furthermore, if
f=g
then
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\right|^{2}\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}=2\pi\sin\alpha c_{\psi,\alpha}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.
For fixed
a
treating
(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)
as a function of the variable
b,
authors also obtained the following

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}g)(a,b)}\right]db=2\pi a\sin\alpha\langle U_{\alpha},V_{\alpha}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

where
U_{\alpha}(\xi)=(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)e^{\frac{i}{2}a^{2}\xi^{2}\cot\alpha}\overline{\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}\left[e^{-\frac{i}{2}(\cdot)^{2}\cot\alpha}\psi\right](a\xi)}
and

V_{\alpha}(\xi)=(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}g)(\xi)e^{-\frac{i}{2}a^{2}\xi^{2}\cot\alpha}\overline{\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}\left[e^{-\frac{i}{2}(\cdot)^{2}\cot\alpha}\psi\right](a\xi)}.
Motivated by the convolution operator (
23
) given by Zayed, Dai et al.
[
56
]
introduced a new definition of the fractional WT

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}^{\alpha}f\right)(a,b)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{i}{2}b^{2}\cot\alpha}\left\{\left[f(t)e^{\frac{i}{2}t^{2}\cot\alpha}\right]\star\overline{\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\psi\left(-\frac{t}{a}\right)e^{\frac{i}{2}\left(\frac{t}{a}\right)^{2}\cot\alpha}\right]}\right\}(b)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\psi_{\alpha,a,b}(t)}dt

(272)

where
\psi_{\alpha,a,b}(t)=e^{-\frac{i}{2}\left(t^{2}-b^{2}-\left(\frac{t-b}{a}\right)\right)\cot\alpha}\psi_{a,b}(t).
Equation (
14
), can be expressed in the fractional Fourier domain as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi a}{1+j\cot\alpha}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\frac{i}{2}a^{2}\xi^{2}\cot\alpha}(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)\overline{(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}\psi)(a\xi)}\mathcal{K}_{-\alpha}(\xi,b)d\xi.

(273)

As a result of which, the authors in
[
56
]
obtained the following inner product relation

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)}\frac{da}{a}db=2\pi\sin\alpha c_{\psi}\langle f,g\rangle

(274)

followed by the inversion formula

\displaystyle f(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sin\alpha c_{\psi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{a^{2}}(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\psi_{a,b,\alpha}(t)dadb,

(275)

where
c_{\psi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{|\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}\psi(s)|^{2}}{|s|}ds
is a positive constant. From equation (
274
), the FrWT satisfies the Parseval identity
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(t)|^{2}dt=\frac{1}{2\pi\sin\alpha C_{\psi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)|^{2}\frac{da}{a^{2}}db.
As a consequence of which it can be shown that the range
\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
of the FrWT given by (
14
) is a RKHS with the kernel
K_{\phi_{\alpha}}(a_{0},b_{0};a,b)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sin\alpha C_{\psi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{a,b,\alpha}(t)\overline{\psi_{a_{0},b_{0},\alpha}(t)}dt.
The FrWT (
14
) gives the local information of the signal
f(t)
in the time window
[b+aE_{\psi}-a\Delta_{\psi},b+aE_{\psi}+a\Delta_{\psi}]
and the local FrFD spectral information in the FrFD as
[kE_{\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\psi}-k\Delta_{\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\psi},kE_{\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\psi}+k\Delta_{\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\psi}],
where
k=\sqrt{\frac{1-i\cot\alpha}{a^{2}-i\cot\alpha}},\beta=\arctan(a^{2}\tan\alpha),
with the constant window area
4ak\Delta_{\psi}\Delta_{\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\psi}
in the time-FrFD-frequency plane. The area depends only on the mother wavelet
\psi
and the angle
\alpha
and is independent of the parameters
a
and
b.
Guo et al.
[
86
]
defined a general FrWT, using some particular type of convolutions given in (
3
), as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=

\displaystyle f(t)\star_{\alpha,\alpha,\beta}\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\overline{\psi\left(-\frac{t}{a}\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{i}{2}b^{2}\cot\alpha}\left[f(t)e^{\frac{i}{2}t^{2}\cot\alpha}\star\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\psi\left(-\frac{t}{a}\right)e^{\frac{i}{2}t^{2}\cot\beta}\right](b).

(276)

This definition of the FrWT has the equivalent form

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\psi_{a,b,\alpha,\beta}(t)}dt,

(277)

where

\displaystyle\psi_{a,b,\alpha,\beta}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\psi\left(\frac{t-b}{a}\right)e^{-\frac{i}{2}\left[(t^{2}-b^{2})\cot\alpha-\left(\frac{t-b}{a}\right)^{2}\cot\beta\right]}.

(278)

The window function
\psi_{\alpha,\beta,a,b}(t)
of proposed FrWT has one more free parameter
\beta,
and thus the proposed transform has more degree of freedom of parameter selection. The transform have the scaling property
(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\psi}f(ct))(a,b)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{c}}(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha^{\prime},\beta}_{\psi}f)(ca,cb),
where
c>0
and
\alpha^{\prime}
satisfies
\cot\alpha^{\prime}=\frac{\cot\alpha}{c^{2}}.
Moreover, it reduces to the classical WT when
\alpha=\beta=\frac{\pi}{2}
and to the FrWT (
14
) and (
14
) when
\beta=\frac{\pi}{2}
and
\alpha=\beta
respectively. Also, it can be expressed in the fractional Fourier domain with
\lambda=\frac{\csc\beta}{\csc\alpha}
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\frac{\sqrt{a}}{A_{\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\frac{i}{2}\left(\frac{a\xi}{\lambda}\right)^{2}\cot\beta}\left(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f\right)(\xi)\overline{\left(\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\psi\right)\left(\frac{a\xi}{\lambda}\right)}K_{-\alpha}(\xi,b)d\xi.

(279)

In term of fractional domain, the window and signal are restricted to same fractional domain (i.e.,
\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}\psi
and
\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f
) for the transform (
14
) defined by
[
56
]
, whereas for the transform (
14
), it appears from (
279
), that the window and signal can be transformed freely to different fractional domains (i.e.,
\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\psi
and
\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f
). The following resolution of the identity formula holds for the transform (
14
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\psi}g)(a,b)}\frac{1}{a^{2}}dadb=2\pi\sin\beta c_{\psi,\beta}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{g(t)}dt,

(280)

followed by the inversion formula

\displaystyle f(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sin\beta c_{\psi,\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\psi_{a,b,\alpha,\beta}(t)\frac{1}{a^{2}}dadb.

(281)

From (
280
), it follows that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\psi}f)(a,b)|^{2}\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}=2\pi\sin\beta c_{\psi,\beta}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(t)|^{2}dt,
as a result of which it follows that the range
\mathcal{W}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
of the FrWT given by (
14
) is a RKHS with the kernel given by
K_{\psi,\alpha,\beta}(a_{0},b_{0},a,b)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sin\beta c_{\psi,\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{a,b,\alpha,\beta}(t)\overline{\psi_{a_{0},b_{0},\alpha,\beta}(t)}dt.
In view of equations (
277
) and (
279
), the FrWT (
14
) gives the local information of the signal
f(t)
in the time window
[b+aE_{\psi}-a\Delta_{\psi},b+aE_{\psi}+a\Delta_{\psi}]
and the local FrFD spectral information in the FrFD as
\left[\frac{\lambda}{a}E[\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\psi]-\frac{\lambda}{a}\Delta\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\psi,\frac{\lambda}{a}E[\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\psi]+\frac{\lambda}{a}\Delta\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\psi\right],
with the constant window area
4\lambda\Delta\psi\Delta\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\psi
in the time-FrFD-frequency plane, where
\lambda=\frac{\csc\beta}{\csc\alpha}.
The area depends only on the mother wavelet
\psi
and the parameter
\alpha,\beta
and is independent of the parameters
a
and
b.
Motivated by the work of Pandey et al. (
[
145
]
,
[
150
]
), Verma et al.
[
200
]
extended the FrWT (
14
) to
n-
dimensional Euclidean space with the dilation parameter
\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
a_{i}\neq 0,i=1,2,\cdots,n,
given as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\boldsymbol{t})\overline{\psi_{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b},\alpha}(\boldsymbol{t})}d\boldsymbol{t},

(282)

where
f,\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

\psi_{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b},\alpha}(\boldsymbol{t})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\boldsymbol{a}|_{p}}}\psi\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{t}-\boldsymbol{b}}{\boldsymbol{a}}\right)e^{-\frac{i}{2}(|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2}-|\boldsymbol{b}|^{2})\cot\alpha}
and
\psi
satisfies the admissibility condition

\ C_{\psi,\alpha}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}}\left|\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\left\{e^{-\frac{i}{2}|\cdot|^{2}\cot\alpha}\psi(\cdot)\right\}\right)(\boldsymbol{u})\right|^{2}\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{u}|_{p}}d\boldsymbol{u}<\infty.
They considered two functions
\phi,\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying this admissibility condition with the assumption that

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}}\left|\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\left\{e^{-\frac{i}{2}|\cdot|^{2}\cot\alpha}\phi(\cdot)\right\}\right)(\boldsymbol{\xi})\right|\left|\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\left\{e^{-\frac{i}{2}|\cdot|^{2}\cot\alpha}\psi(\cdot)\right\}\right)(\boldsymbol{\xi})\right|\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{\xi}|_{p}}d\boldsymbol{\xi}<\infty

(283)

and obtained the following inner product relation

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(W^{\alpha}_{\phi}f)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\overline{(W^{\alpha}_{\psi}g)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})}\frac{d\boldsymbol{a}d\boldsymbol{b}}{|\boldsymbol{a}|_{p}^{2}}=\frac{C_{\phi,\psi,\alpha}}{|C_{\alpha}|^{2}}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

(284)

followed by the inversion formula

f(\boldsymbol{t})=\frac{|C_{\alpha}|^{2}}{C_{\phi,\psi,\alpha}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi_{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b},\alpha}(\boldsymbol{t})(W^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\frac{d\boldsymbol{a}d\boldsymbol{b}}{|\boldsymbol{a}|_{p}^{2}},

where
C_{\phi,\psi,\alpha}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}}\overline{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\left\{e^{-\frac{i}{2}|\cdot|^{2}\cot\alpha}\phi(\cdot)\right\}\right)(\boldsymbol{\xi})}\left(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\left\{e^{-\frac{i}{2}|\cdot|^{2}\cot\alpha}\psi(\cdot)\right\}\right)(\boldsymbol{\xi})\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{\xi}|_{p}}d\boldsymbol{\xi}<\infty.
From (
284
), it follows that, if
f=g
and
\phi=\psi
then the range
W^{\alpha}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
is not only the subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\frac{d\boldsymbol{a}d\boldsymbol{b}}{|\boldsymbol{a}|^{2}_{p}})
but also the RKHS with the kernel given by
K_{\psi,\alpha}(\boldsymbol{a}_{0},\boldsymbol{b}_{0};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})=\frac{|C_{\alpha}|^{2}}{C_{\psi,\alpha}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\psi_{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b},\alpha}(\boldsymbol{t})\overline{{\psi_{\boldsymbol{a}_{0},\boldsymbol{b}_{0},\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{t})}d\boldsymbol{t}.
Kaur et al.
[
111
]
, introduced a more general definition of FrWT based on the convolution associated with the multi-dimensional FrFT (
42
) introduced in
[
105
]
, which is defined for the
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and the function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfying the admissibility condition
c_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}=(2\pi)^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{0}}|(\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}}\psi)(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}\frac{d\boldsymbol{\xi}}{|\boldsymbol{\xi}|_{m}}<\infty,
where
\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}}
is given by (
40
), as

\displaystyle(W_{\psi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}f)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})=c(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda})e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},-\lambda^{2}}(\tilde{f}\ast\breve{g})(\boldsymbol{b}),\quad\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{0},\quad\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(285)

where
e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x})=e^{i\lambda^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}a(\alpha_{k}){x_{k}}^{2}},

a(\alpha_{k})=\frac{\cot(\alpha_{k})}{2},

\tilde{\psi}(\boldsymbol{x})=\psi(\boldsymbol{x})e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x}),

\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(\boldsymbol{x})e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x}),

{\breve{g}}(\boldsymbol{t})=\overline{\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\boldsymbol{a}|_{m}}}\tilde{\psi}\left(\frac{-\boldsymbol{t}}{\boldsymbol{a}}\right)},

c(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda})=c(\alpha_{1})c(\alpha_{2})\cdots c(\alpha_{n}),

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda}=\left(\cot^{-1}(\lambda^{2}\cot\alpha_{1}),\cot^{-1}(\lambda^{2}\cot\alpha_{2}),\cdots,\cot^{-1}(\lambda^{2}\cot\alpha_{n})\right)
and
\ast
is the convolution given by

(\mu\ast\nu)(\mathbf{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mu(\boldsymbol{y})\nu(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y})d\boldsymbol{y},

which can equivalently be written as
(W_{\psi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}f)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})=\left\langle f(\boldsymbol{t}),\mathbf{\Psi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda,\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}}(\boldsymbol{t})\right\rangle,
where

\mathbf{\Psi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda,\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}}(\boldsymbol{t})=\overline{c(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda})}e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda^{2}}(\boldsymbol{b})e_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},-\lambda^{2}}(\boldsymbol{t}){\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\boldsymbol{a}|_{m}}}\tilde{\psi}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{t}-\boldsymbol{b}}{\boldsymbol{a}}\right).

They proved basic properties like linearity, anti-linearity, dilation, parity, conjugation, and translation of the FrWT (
285
), followed by the inner product relation

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(W_{\psi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}f)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\overline{(W_{\psi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}g)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})}\frac{d\boldsymbol{a}d\boldsymbol{b}}{|\boldsymbol{a}|_{m}}=c_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

(286)

inversion formula
f(\boldsymbol{t})=\frac{1}{c_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(W_{\psi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}f)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\mathbf{\Psi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda,\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}}(\boldsymbol{t})\frac{d\boldsymbol{a}d\boldsymbol{b}}{|\boldsymbol{a}|_{m}}.
In addition, authors characterized the range
W_{\psi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and proved that it is a RKHS with the kernel
K_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda,\psi}(\boldsymbol{a}_{0},\boldsymbol{b}_{0};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbf{\Psi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda,\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}}(\boldsymbol{t})\overline{\mathbf{\Psi}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda,\boldsymbol{a}_{0},\boldsymbol{b}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{t})}d\boldsymbol{t}.
Definition (
285
) not only combine the advantages of the multidimensional FrFT but also inherit the characteristics of the classical WT in
n-
dimension studied in
[
145
]
. It reduces to (
14
) with a constant multiple
\sqrt{1-i\cot\alpha},
when
n=1,

\lambda=1
and
\alpha_{1}=\alpha
and to the
n-
dimensional WT introduced in
[
145
]
, when
\alpha_{i}=\frac{\pi}{2},i=1,2,\cdots,n
and
\lambda=1.

Bahri et al.
[
22
]
studied the FrWT (
14
) on the generalized Sobolev spaces
H^{s}_{r,\alpha},\penalty\ s,\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
associated with the FrFT which is defined as the space of all measurable functions
f
such that

\|f\|^{2}_{H^{s}_{r,\alpha}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|\xi\csc\alpha|)^{-s}|(\xi\csc\alpha)^{r}|^{2}|(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)|^{2}d\xi<\infty.

They obtained the boundedness of the operator
W_{\psi}^{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}H^{s}_{r,\alpha}\rightarrow H^{s}_{r,\alpha}
given by
f\rightarrow(W_{\psi}^{\alpha}f)(a,\cdot)
with the assumption that
\psi
is integrable and proved that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\|(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{s}_{r,\alpha}}\frac{da}{a^{2}}=2\pi\sin\alpha c_{\psi,\alpha}\|f\|^{2}_{H^{s}_{r,\alpha}}.
The result analogous to (
216
) for the FrWT with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}_{r,\alpha}}
as a counterpart of
\|\cdot\|_{H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})}
is also obtained. They derived the convergence for the convolution for
\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi},
i.e.,
(\psi_{a}\star_{\alpha}f)(\cdot)\rightarrow f(\cdot)
in
H^{s}_{r,\alpha}
as
a\rightarrow 0,
where
\psi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
is a real valued function satisfying
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(t)dt=1
and
\psi_{a}(x)=\frac{1}{a}\psi(\frac{x}{a}),a\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.
Prasad et al.
[
158
]
studied the similar result for the generalized Sobolev space
B^{\eta,\alpha}_{p,k}(\mathbb{R}),
which is defined as

Definition 14.1
.

Let
\eta\in\mathfrak{M}_{c},k\in K_{\eta},
then the generalized Sobolev space
B^{\eta,\alpha}_{p,k}(\mathbb{R})
is defined as the space of all the generalized ultra-distribution
f
in
(S^{\alpha}_{\eta})^{\prime}
such that

\|f\|_{p,k,\alpha}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|k(\xi)(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)|^{p}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty
and
\|f\|_{\infty,k,\alpha}=ess\sup|k(\xi)(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)|,
where
K_{\eta}
the set of positive functions
k
in
\mathbb{R}
satisfying
k(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})\leq e^{\lambda\eta(-\xi)}k(\xi_{2}),
for some
\lambda>0.
The space
B^{\eta,\alpha}_{p,k}(\mathbb{R})
reduces to the Hörmander space
B_{p,k}(\mathbb{R}),
when
\eta(\xi)=\log(1+|\xi|)
and
\alpha=\frac{\pi}{2}.

Here,
\mathfrak{M}_{c}
denotes the set of all real valued continuous functions
\eta
on
\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\eta(0)=0,
for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi,

0\leq\eta(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})\leq\eta(\xi_{1})+\eta(\xi_{2}),

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\eta(\xi)}{(1+|\xi|)^{2}}d\xi<\infty
and
\eta(\xi)>b+a\log(1+|\xi|),\penalty\ a>0,\penalty\ b\in\mathbb{R}
and
\eta(\xi)=\Omega(|\xi|),
where
\Omega
is a concave function on
[0,\infty).

The space
S^{\alpha}_{\xi}
is a collection of
\phi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\phi
and
\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}\phi
are smooth functions and for every
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\lambda\geq 0,

p_{l,\lambda,\alpha}(\phi)=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}e^{\lambda\eta(t)}|\Delta_{t}^{\alpha}\phi(t)|<\infty
and
r_{l,\lambda,\alpha}(\phi)=\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}e^{\lambda\eta(\xi)}|\Delta_{\xi}^{\alpha}\phi(\xi)|<\infty,
where
\Delta_{t}=\frac{d}{dt}-it\cot\alpha.
The topology on
S^{\alpha}_{\eta}
is defined by the semi norms
p_{l,\lambda,\alpha}(\phi)
and
r_{l,\lambda,\alpha}(\phi).
The elements of the dual
(S^{\alpha}_{\eta})^{\prime}
are called the generalized ultra-distributions. Note that for
\eta(\xi)=\log(1+|\xi|),\alpha=\frac{\pi}{2}
and
\eta(\xi)=\xi^{\sigma}(0<\sigma<1),\alpha=\frac{\pi}{2}
the space
S^{\alpha}_{\eta}
reduces to the Schwartz space and the Gevrey space of test functions, respectively.

Authors in
[
200
]
, studied the properties of FrWT (
282
), with the assumption that
\psi
is compactly supported, on Morrey space
L^{1,\nu}_{M}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
define as

Definition 14.2
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and
\nu\geq 0.
The Morrey space, denoted by
L^{p,\nu}_{M}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
is defined as

L^{p,\nu}_{M}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\left\{f\in L^{p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},r>0}\frac{1}{r^{\nu}}\int_{B(\boldsymbol{x},r)}|f(\boldsymbol{t})|^{p}d\boldsymbol{t}<\infty\right\},

which is a Banach space with respect to the norm
\|f\|_{L^{p,\nu}_{M}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},r>0}\left(\frac{1}{r^{\nu}}\int_{B(\boldsymbol{x},r)}|f(\boldsymbol{t})|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

They proved the boundedness of the operator
W_{\psi}^{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L^{1,\nu}_{M}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\rightarrow L^{1,\nu}_{M}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
given by
f\rightarrow(W_{\psi}^{\alpha}f)(\boldsymbol{a},\cdot)
and
the result analogous to (
216
) for the FrWT with the norm
|\cdot|_{L^{1,\nu}_{M}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
as a counterpart of
|\cdot|_{H^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})}.

Uncertainty Principles:
Using the logarithmic UP for the FrFT given by equation (
56
), obtained in
[
79
]
, authors in
[
22
]
obtained the logarithmic UP for the FrWT (
14
) as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|\xi|\left|\left(\mathcal{F}^{\beta}_{b}\{(\mathcal{W}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\}\right)(\xi)\right|^{2}\frac{d\xi da}{a^{2}}+2\pi\sin\alpha

\displaystyle c_{\psi,\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|\xi|\left|(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)\right|^{2}d\xi

\displaystyle\hskip-62.59596pt\geq 2\pi\sin\alpha c_{\psi,\alpha}\left(\ln|\sin(\beta-\alpha)|+\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}(\frac{1}{4})}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{4})}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x)|^{2}dx.

(287)

Using the Heisenberg UP (
54
) in two fractional Fourier domain, Verma et al.
[
200
]
obtained the the Heisenberg type UPs for the FrWT (
282
) as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2}|(\mathcal{F}^{\beta}_{\boldsymbol{b}}\{(W^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\})(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{t}\frac{d\boldsymbol{a}}{|\boldsymbol{a}|^{2}_{p}}\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2}|(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)\geq\frac{n^{2}C_{\psi,\alpha}}{4|C_{\alpha}|^{2}}|\sin(\alpha-\beta)|^{2}\|f\|^{4}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

(288)

and also its local UP, using local UP (
55
) for the FrFT in two fractional Fourier domain, as

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{E}|(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\leq\begin{cases}\frac{A_{\theta}{|C_{\alpha}|^{2}}}{|\sin(\alpha-\beta)|^{2\theta}C_{\psi,\alpha}}(\lambda(E))^{\frac{2\theta}{n}}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\boldsymbol{x}|^{2\theta}|(\mathcal{F}^{\beta}_{\boldsymbol{b}}\{(W^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\})(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\frac{d\boldsymbol{a}}{|\boldsymbol{a}|^{2}_{p}},\ \mbox{if}\ 0<\theta<\frac{n}{2},\\
\frac{A_{\theta}|C_{\alpha}|^{2}}{|\sin(\alpha-\beta)|^{n}C_{\psi,\alpha}}\lambda(E)\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}}\|(W^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(\cdot,\boldsymbol{a})\|^{2-\frac{n}{\theta}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\left\||\boldsymbol{x}|^{\theta}(\mathcal{F}^{\beta}_{\boldsymbol{b}}\{(W^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})\})\right\|^{\frac{n}{\theta}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\frac{d\boldsymbol{a}}{|\boldsymbol{a}|^{2}_{p}},\ \mbox{if}\ \theta>\frac{n}{2}.\end{cases}

Kaur et al.
[
111
]
obtained the same for the FrWT (
285
), using the corresponding UPs (
57
) and (
58
) for the multidimensional FrFT
[
105
]
as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\boldsymbol{b}|^{2}|(W_{\psi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}{f})(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{b}\frac{d\boldsymbol{a}}{|\boldsymbol{a}|_{m}^{2}}\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2}|(\mathcal{F}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)\geq P_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}C_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}\frac{n^{2}}{4}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

where
P_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}=\frac{|c(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda})|^{2}|\sin\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{m}}{M^{2}(\lambda^{2})^{n+2}},

M=\mbox{max}\left\{\frac{1}{|\sin\alpha_{l}|},l=1,2,\cdots,n\right\}
and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\ln|\boldsymbol{b}||(W_{\psi}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}{f})(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{b}\frac{d\boldsymbol{a}}{|\boldsymbol{a}|_{m}^{2}}+C_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}P^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\ln|\boldsymbol{\xi}||(\mathcal{F}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}f)(\boldsymbol{\xi})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\xi}\geq(D-P^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}\ln(\lambda^{2}M))C_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

where
P^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\lambda}=\frac{\lambda^{2n}}{|c(\boldsymbol{\alpha})|^{2}|\sin\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{m}}.
They also obtained the local UP for the FrWT using the same for the multidimensional FrFT (
59
).

14.1
Multiresolution Analysis (MRA)

Multiresolution analysis (MRA) has become an important tool for the construction of a discrete wavelet system, i.e., an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
It was first formulated by Mallet
[
133
]
. Some of the important wavelets that have been obtained from the MRA are Haar wavelet, Shannon wavelet, Meyer wavelet, Daubechies wavelet, etc.
[
57
]
,
[
58
]
. Not all wavelets are derived via MRA; there are some wavelets, like the Mexican hat wavelet and Morlet wavelet, that do not come from an MRA (see
[
152
]
).

Definition 14.3
.

The MRA consists of a sequence of closed subspaces
\{V_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying the properties

1.

V_{m}\subset V_{m+1},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{Z}

2.

f(t)\in V_{m}
if and only if
f(2t)\in V_{m+1},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{Z}

3.

\cap_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{m}=\{0\},

\overline{\cup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{m}}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

4.

there exists a function
\phi\in V_{0}
such that
\{\phi_{0,n}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
form an orthonormal basis of
V_{0},
where

\phi_{m,n}(t)=2^{\frac{m}{2}}\phi(2^{m}t-n),\penalty\ m,n\in\mathbb{Z}.

Shi et al.
[
182
]
developed the MRA associated with the FrWT (
14
) by discretizing the parameters
a
and
b
by dyadic grids, i.e.,
a=a_{0}^{m}
and
b=na_{0}^{m}b_{0},\penalty\ m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
to the family (
263
), results in the following, which contains one extra parameter
\alpha.
As a result of which, a new class of orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
can be obtained

(I)

V^{\alpha}_{m}\subset V^{\alpha}_{m+1},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{Z}

(II)

f(t)\in V^{\alpha}_{m}
if and only if
f(2t)e^{\frac{i}{2}((2t)^{2}-t^{2})\cot\alpha}\in V^{\alpha}_{m+1},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{Z}

(III)

\cap_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{\alpha}_{m}=\{0\},

\overline{\cup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{\alpha}_{m}}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

(IV)

there exists a function
\phi\in V^{\alpha}_{0}
such that
\{\phi_{\alpha,0,n}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
form an orthonormal basis of
V^{\alpha}_{0},
where

\phi_{\alpha,0,n}(t)=2^{\frac{m}{2}}\phi(2^{m}t-n)e^{-i\frac{t^{2}-n^{2}}{2}\cot\alpha},\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{Z}.

Indeed condition
(IV)
can be relaxed by assuming that the set of functions
\{\phi_{\alpha,0,n}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
is a Riesz basis of
V^{\alpha}_{0}=\{\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c[n]\phi_{0,n,\alpha}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}c[n]\in l^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\}.
The fractional MRA so defined consists of one extra parameter
\alpha
compared to the classical and reduces to the later for
\alpha=\frac{\pi}{2}.
With the scaling function
\phi
given by

(\mathcal{F}\phi)(\xi\csc\alpha)=\begin{cases}1,&|\xi|<\xi_{\alpha}\\
\cos\left[\frac{\pi}{2}v\left(\frac{\xi}{2\xi_{\alpha}}-1\right)\right],&\xi_{\alpha}\leq|\xi|<2\xi_{\alpha}\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise},\end{cases}

where
\xi_{\alpha}=\frac{2\pi}{3|\csc\alpha|}
and
v(t)
is a smooth function satisfying
v(t)=\begin{cases}0,&t\leq 0\\
1,&t\geq 1,\end{cases}

satisfying the additional property
v(t)+v(1-t)=1,
authors obtained the wavelet
\psi
by taking the inverse FT in

\left(\mathcal{F}\psi\right)(\xi\csc\alpha)=\left[\left(\mathcal{F}\psi\right)(\xi\csc\alpha+2\pi)+\left(\mathcal{F}\psi\right)(\xi\csc\alpha-2\pi)\right]e^{-\frac{i}{2}\xi\csc\alpha}\left(\mathcal{F}\psi\right)\left(\frac{\xi\csc\alpha}{2}\right).

Also for the scaling function
\phi(t)=\chi_{[0,1)}(t),
the corresponding
\psi
is given by
\psi(t)=\chi_{[0,\frac{1}{2})}(t)-\chi_{[\frac{1}{2},1)}(t).
Guo et al.
[
86
]
developed the fractional MRA associated with the family (
278
) by discretizing the dilation parameter
a
and the translation parameter
b,
with
a=a_{0}^{m}
and
b=na_{0}^{m}b_{0},
where
m,n\in\mathbb{Z},

a_{0}>0,b_{0}\neq 0.
Of particular interest, is the dyadic grid, which occur for
a_{0}=2,b_{0}=1.
Authors considered a sequence of closed sub-spaces
\{V^{\alpha,\beta}_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

V^{\alpha,\beta}_{m}\subset V^{\alpha,\beta}_{m+1},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{Z}

(ii)

f(t)\in V^{\alpha,\beta}_{m}
if and only if
f(2t)e^{\frac{i}{2}((2t)^{2}-t^{2})\cot\alpha}\in V^{\alpha,\beta}_{m+1},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{Z}

(iii)

\cap_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{\alpha,\beta}_{m}=\{0\},

\overline{\cup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{\alpha,\beta}_{m}}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

(iv)

there exists a function
\phi\in V^{\alpha,\beta}_{0}
such that
\{\phi_{\alpha,\beta,0,n}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
form an orthonormal basis of
V^{\alpha,\beta}_{0},
where

\phi_{\alpha,\beta,m,n}(t)=2^{\frac{m}{2}}\phi(2^{m}t-n)e^{-\frac{i}{2}\left[\left(t^{2}-\left(\frac{n}{2^{m}}\right)^{2}\right)\cot\alpha-(2^{m}t-n)^{2}\cot\beta\right]},\penalty\ m,n\in\mathbb{Z}.

If the orthogonality in
(iv)
is relaxed by the assumption that
\{\phi_{\alpha,\beta,0,n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a Riesz basis of
V_{0}^{\alpha,\beta},
the
\phi
generates a generalized MRA associated with the FrWT. Authors obtained a result showing a way to construct an orthonormal basis from Riesz basis
\{\phi_{\alpha,\beta,0,n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of
V_{0}^{\alpha,\beta}.
The MRA so developed reduces to the MRA proposed by Dai et al.
[
56
]
when
\alpha=\beta
and to definition
14.3
if
\alpha=\beta=\frac{\pi}{2}.
The subspace
W^{\alpha,\beta}_{m}
is defined as the orthogonal complement of
V^{\alpha,\beta}_{m}
in
V^{\alpha,\beta}_{m+1},
i.e.,

W^{\alpha,\beta}_{m}\perp V^{\alpha,\beta}_{m},\penalty\ V^{\alpha,\beta}_{m+1}=V^{\alpha,\beta}_{m}\oplus V^{\alpha,\beta}_{m},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{Z}.

Then according to the definition,
\{W^{\alpha,\beta}_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
have the following properties

1.

W^{\alpha,\beta}_{k}\perp V^{\alpha,\beta}_{l},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ k\neq l;

2.

\oplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}W^{\alpha,\beta}_{m}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

3.

g(t)\in W^{\alpha,\beta}_{m}
if and only if
g(2t)e^{\frac{i}{2}((2t)^{2}-t^{2})\cot\alpha}\in W^{\alpha,\beta}_{m+1},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{Z}.

From the second property, it follows that an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
can be constructed by finding out an orthonormal basis of
W^{\alpha,\beta}_{m}.
Third property says that the problem of finding this basis is centered to finding an orthonormal basis of
W^{\alpha,\beta}_{0}.
Thus the crucial point is to construct a function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\{\psi_{\alpha,\beta,0,n}(t)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
form an orthonormal basis of
W^{\alpha,\beta}_{0},
where
\psi_{\alpha,\beta,0,n}(t)=\psi(t-n)e^{-\frac{i}{2}((t^{2}-n^{2})\cot\alpha-(t-n)^{2}\cot\beta)}.
The two free parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
involved in the MRA helps in the construction of several new classes of orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
The presence of these two free parameters makes the MRA developed in
[
86
]
more flexible as compared to that in
[
182
]
and
[
56
]
. Thus, it results in developing a new class of orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
For example, for some scaling function
\phi
taken in the fractional Fourier domain the
corresponding fractional wavelet is given in the fractional Fourier domain as

\left(\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\psi\right)\left(\frac{\xi}{\lambda}\right)=e^{\frac{i}{2}\frac{3}{4}\left(\frac{\xi}{\lambda}\right)^{2}\cot\alpha}(2\pi\sin\alpha)^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{i}{2}\frac{3}{4}\left(\frac{\xi+2\pi\sin\alpha}{\lambda}\right)^{2}\cot\beta}(\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\phi)(\frac{\xi+2\pi\sin\alpha}{\lambda})(\mathcal{F}^{\beta}\phi)(\frac{\xi}{2\lambda}),
where
\lambda=\frac{\csc\beta}{\csc\alpha}.
Authors in
[
3
]
proved that the conditions in the definition of the fractional MRA given in
[
56
]
, which is a particular case when
\alpha=\beta,
are not independent. More precisely, the intersection triviality condition
\cap_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{\alpha,\alpha}=\{0\}
can be obtained from the conditions
(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iv)
for
\alpha=\beta.
In addition to these assumptions if
(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}\phi)(\xi)
is continuous at
\xi=0,
they proved that the union density condition
\overline{\cup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{\alpha,\alpha}}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is equivalent to the condition
(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}\phi)(0)\neq 0.
These results are in fact the counter part of those given in
[
95
]
.

15
Fractional Stockwell Transform (FrST)

In 2012, Xu et al.
[
212
]
first proposed a FrST based on the idea of of the FrFT and the ST with Gaussian window as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}f)(\tau,u)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(t)g(\tau-t,u)R_{\alpha}(t,u)dt,

(289)

where the Gaussian window
g(\tau-t,u)
is a scalable function of
t
and the fractional Fourier frequency (FrFfr)
u

g(t,u)=\frac{|u\csc\alpha|^{p}}{\sqrt{2\pi}q}e^{-\frac{t^{2}(u\csc\alpha)^{2p}}{2q^{2}}}
and
R_{\alpha}(t,u)=\sqrt{1-i\cot\alpha}e^{i\pi(u^{2}\cot\alpha-2ut\csc\alpha+t^{2}\cot\alpha)},\penalty\ \alpha\nin\pi\mathbb{Z}.
It is to be noted that, when
\alpha=\frac{\pi}{2}
and
p=q=1,
then the FrST (
289
) is the classical ST (
224
) given in
[
193
]
. Observe that the window
g(t,u)
width is directed by
u,
i.e., the window width is wider in the time domain at lower FrFfr
u,
whereas it is narrower at higher
u.
Authors proved the linearity of the transform (
289
) followed by the scaling formula

(\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}f(ct))(\tau,u)=\sqrt{\frac{c^{2}(1-i\cot\alpha)}{c^{2}-i\cot\alpha}}e^{i\pi u^{2}\cot\alpha\left(1-\frac{\cos^{2}\beta}{\cos^{2}\alpha}\right)}(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}f)\left(c\tau,u\frac{\sin\beta}{c\sin\alpha}\right),

where
\beta=\arctan(c^{2}\tan\alpha)
followed by the inversion formula

f(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}[\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}f)(\tau,u)d\tau]\overline{R_{\alpha}(t,u)}du.

For more properties and the applications of the fractional Stockwell transform (
289
), we refer the reader to a survey paper
[
165
]
. Recently, Srivastava et al.
[
189
]
introduced the FrST of a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the window function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{-\xi}f\star_{\alpha}\mathcal{D}_{\xi}\tilde{\psi}\right)(b),

(290)

where
\tilde{\psi}(t)=\overline{\psi(-t)},
and the convolution
\star_{\alpha}
is the fractional convolution given by (
3
), after multiplying it by the factor
\sqrt{2\pi}
. Equation (
290
) can be written in the form

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\psi^{\alpha}_{a,b}(t)}dt,

(291)

where
\psi^{\alpha}_{a,b}(t)=|a|\psi(a(t-b))e^{iat-\frac{i(t^{2}-b^{2})\cot\alpha}{2}},\penalty\ a\in\mathbb{R}^{*},b\in\mathbb{R}.
It is a matter of simple verification that
\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f
is linear in
f
and conjugate linear in
\psi.
The following properties called the covariance properties of the FrST give respectively the formula for the FrST of the translation by
x_{0}
and dilation by
c\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
of function
f
with respect to the
\psi

[(\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{\psi}(\tau_{x_{0}}f)](a,b)=e^{ix_{0}((x_{0}-b)\cot\alpha-a)}(\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{\psi}(\mathcal{M}_{x_{0}\cot\alpha}f))(a,b-x_{0})

and

[(\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{\psi}(f(ct))](a,b)=(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{\psi}f)\left(\frac{a}{c},cb\right),\penalty\ \beta=arc\cot\left(\frac{\cot\alpha}{c}\right).

If the function
\psi
is such that it satisfied the admissibility condition, i.e.,
c_{\psi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{*}}\left|\mathcal{F}[e^{iz}\psi(z)]\left(\frac{\xi\csc\alpha}{a}\right)\right|^{2}da
is non-zero finite, then
\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{\psi}
satisfies the resolution of identity formula

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{\psi}g)(a,b)}dadb=c_{\psi}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

(292)

followed by the inversion formula

f(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}c_{\psi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{*}}(\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\psi^{\alpha}_{a,b}(t)dadb.

For
f=g,
(
292
), yields the energy preserving relation
\|(\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{\psi}g)(a,b)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{*}\times\mathbb{R})}=c_{\psi}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.
If further
c_{\psi}=1,
the FrST (
290
) becomes an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}).
Moreover, the range
\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
is a RKHS with the kernel
K^{\alpha}_{\psi}(a,b;a_{0},b_{0})=\frac{1}{2\pi c_{\psi}}\langle\psi^{\alpha}_{a,b},\psi^{\alpha}_{a_{0},b_{0}}\rangle.
In view of equation (
291
) and its representation in the FrFD, the FrST (
290
) gives the local information of
f(t)
in the time window
\left[b+\frac{E_{\psi}}{a}-\frac{\Delta_{\psi}}{a},b+\frac{E_{\psi}}{a}+\frac{\Delta_{\psi}}{a}\right]
and the local FrFD spectral information in the FrFD as
[a\sin\alpha E_{\Psi}-a\sin\alpha\Delta_{\Psi},a\sin\alpha E_{\Psi}+a\sin\alpha\Delta_{\Psi}]
, where
\Psi(\xi)=\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}[e^{iz}\psi(z)](\xi)
with the constant window area
4\sin\alpha\Delta_{\psi}\Delta_{\Psi}
in the time-fractional-frequency plane. The area depends only on
\psi
and the parameter
\alpha
and is independent of the parameter
a
and
b.
Also, it is worth noting that the fractional parameter
\alpha
can be invoked to optimize the concentration of the Stockwell spectrum in the time-fractional-frequency plane. Wei et al.
[
207
]
used the definition of fractional convolution
\star_{\alpha}
(
3
), to give a new definition of FrST for the function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the window function
\psi
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{\alpha}f)(a,b)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}[e^{-iat}f(t)\star_{\alpha}a\tilde{\psi}(at)](b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\psi_{\alpha,a,b}(t)}dt,

(293)

where
\psi_{\alpha,a,b}(t)=a\psi(a(t-b))e^{iat\csc\alpha-\frac{i}{2}(t^{2}-b^{2})\cot\alpha}.
They proved that the transform is linear in
f
and anti-linear in
\psi
and obtained the formula for the FrST of the translation and dilation of
f
as

(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{\alpha}f(t-k))(a,b)=e^{-iak\csc\alpha+i(k^{2}-kb)\cot\alpha}(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{\alpha}(f(t)e^{ikt\cot\alpha}))(a,b-k)

and

(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{\alpha}f(\lambda t))(a,b)=(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{\beta}f)\left(\frac{a}{\lambda},\lambda b\right),\penalty\ \lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\penalty\ \beta=\mbox{arc}\cot\left(\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}\right)

If the window function
\psi
satisfies the admissible conditions
c_{\psi}=\int\left|\mathcal{F}(\psi(t)e^{jt\csc\alpha})(u\csc\alpha/a)\right|da<\infty
then
\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{\alpha}
satisfies the inner product relations

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{\alpha}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{\alpha}g)(a,b)}dadb=\frac{1}{2\pi}c_{\psi}\langle f,g\rangle

followed by the inversion formula

f(t)=\frac{2\pi}{c_{\psi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{\alpha}f)(a,b)\psi_{\alpha,a,b}(t)dadb

In view of (
15
) and its representation in the fractional Fourier domain, it follows that the FrST (
15
) is capable of providing the time and spectral information simultaneous and representing signal in the time-fractional-frequency domain. It gives local information of the signal
f(t)
with the time window
\left[b+\frac{E_{\psi}}{a}-\frac{\Delta_{\psi}}{a},b+\frac{E_{\psi}}{a}+\frac{\Delta_{\psi}}{a}\right]
and the local information about the fractional Fourier domain spectrum of
f(t)
as
\left[aE_{G}-a\Delta_{G},aE_{G}+a\Delta_{G}\right]\sin\alpha,
with the constant window area
4\sin\alpha\Delta_{\psi}\Delta_{G}
in the time-fractional-frequency plane where
G(u)=\mathcal{F}(\psi(t)e^{it\csc\alpha})(u)
. This area depends on the function
\psi
and the rotation angle
\alpha
and is independent of the parameters
a
and
b.
Moreover, the window narrows for the small value and widens for larger values of
a.

Kamalakkannan et al.
[
107
]
proposed a two-dimensional FrST, associated with the coupled FrFT (
35
), of a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with respect to a non-zero window function
\psi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}_{\psi,\alpha,\beta}f)(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v})=(E_{\gamma}D_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\breve{\overline{\psi}}\bigotimes\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}f)(\boldsymbol{z}),

(294)

\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

\boldsymbol{v}\in(\mathbb{R}^{*})^{2}=\{(x_{1},x_{2})^{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x_{1}\neq 0,x_{2}\neq 0\}
and
\boldsymbol{\rho}=(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})^{t}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}\boldsymbol{v}
(see (
35
)), where
\bigotimes
denotes the convolution associated with the coupled FrFT given in (
37
),
E_{\gamma}
is same as that in (
36
) and
(D_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}f)(\boldsymbol{x})=|\det(A_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})|f(A_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\boldsymbol{x}),

A_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=\begin{bmatrix}\rho_{1}&0\\
0&\rho_{2}\end{bmatrix}.
With the assumption that the window function
\psi
satisfies the admissibility condition

0\neq c_{\psi}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{*})^{2}}\left|\hat{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\xi}-(1,1)^{t})\right|^{2}\frac{d\boldsymbol{\xi}}{|\det(A_{\boldsymbol{\xi}})|}<\infty

authors obtained the inner product relation of the FrST

\displaystyle c_{\psi}\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{*})^{2}}(\mathcal{S}_{\psi,\alpha,\beta}f)(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v})\overline{(\mathcal{S}_{\psi,\alpha,\beta}g)(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v})}d\boldsymbol{z}\frac{d\boldsymbol{v}}{|\det(A_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})|},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\rho}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}\boldsymbol{v},

(295)

followed by the inversion formula

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{d}{2\pi\bar{d}c_{\psi}}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{*})^{2}}\mathcal{M}_{-\boldsymbol{\rho}}\left((\mathcal{S}_{\psi,\alpha,\beta}f)(\cdot,\boldsymbol{v})\bigotimes E_{\gamma}D_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\psi\right)(\boldsymbol{x})\frac{d\boldsymbol{v}}{|\det(A_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})|}.

(296)

As a consequence of equation (
295
), it follows that
\mathcal{S}_{\psi_{\alpha},\beta}f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\frac{d\boldsymbol{z}d\boldsymbol{v}}{|\det(A_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})|}\right).
Authors in
[
107
]
, characterized the range of the transform and proved that
\mathcal{S}_{\psi,\alpha,\beta}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is a RKHS with the kernel
K^{\alpha,\beta}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\psi^{\alpha,\beta}_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\psi^{\alpha,\beta}_{\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{x})}d\boldsymbol{x},
where
\psi^{\alpha,\beta}_{\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{x})=2\pi\bar{d}E^{-1}_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{z})(E_{\gamma}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\tau_{\boldsymbol{z}}D_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\psi)(\boldsymbol{x}).
For
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\penalty\ p=1,2
and
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
the formula for the FrST of convolution
\bigotimes
is obtained as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\psi,\alpha,\beta}(f\bigotimes g)(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v})=\left((\mathcal{S}_{\psi,\alpha,\beta}f)(\cdot,\boldsymbol{v})\bigotimes\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}g\right)(\boldsymbol{z}).

(297)

Uncertainty Principles:
Srivastava et al.
[
189
]
obtained the Heisenberg’s uncertainty inequality associated with the FrST (
290
) as

\displaystyle\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\xi|^{2}|(\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{*}}|\omega|^{2}|(\mathcal{F}^{\beta}((\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}_{\psi}f)(a,b)))(\omega)|^{2}dad\omega\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\frac{\sqrt{c_{\psi}}|\sin(\alpha-\beta)|}{2}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

(298)

where
\alpha
and
\beta
are chosen such that
\beta=\alpha-\gamma
and
\sin\alpha,\sin\beta,\sin\gamma\neq 0.
The fractional parameters govern these relations and are fruitful in understanding mutual relations among different transform domains. Using the Heisenberg UP for the coupled FrFT, the author in
[
107
]
obtained the Heisenberg UP for the FrST (
294
), which is given by

\Delta\boldsymbol{z}^{2}_{\alpha,\beta}\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{2}_{\alpha,\beta}\geq c_{\psi}\sin^{2}\gamma\|f\|^{4}_{2},

where
\Delta\boldsymbol{z}^{2}_{\alpha,\beta}=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{*})^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{z}|^{2}|(\mathcal{S}_{\psi,\alpha,\beta}f)(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{z}\frac{d\boldsymbol{v}}{|\det(A_{\boldsymbol{\rho}})|}
and
\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{2}_{\alpha,\beta}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}|(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha,\beta}f)(\boldsymbol{u})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}.

16
Windowed Linear Canonical Transform (WLCT)

Compared to the FT and the FrFT, LCT is more flexible with more extra degrees of freedom but with a similar computational cost as the conventional FT. Because of the mentioned advantages, the LCT has found many applications in filter designing, signal synthesis, optics, radar analysis, pattern recognition, etc. For example, filtering in the linear canonical domain, given in
[
34
]
, can achieve better performance than in the fractional Fourier domain owing to more degree of freedom. Especially when multi-component chirp signals interfere with the desired signal, only one filter is used in the LCT domain usually, but several filters are required in the FRFT domain
[
68
]
. Despite these benefits, it has limitations on its own. The LCT cannot reveal the local LCT-frequency component because of its global kernel. As a result, Kou et al.
[
115
]
introduced the WLCT by substituting the FT kernel with the LCT kernel in the WFT definition, which is defined for the function
f
with respect to
g
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f)(x,u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\tau_{x}g(t)}K_{M}(t,u)dt,

(299)

where
M=(A,B;C,D)
with
\det(M)=1,
and
B>0.
Observe that, if
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\penalty\ g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and the window function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}),\penalty\ g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
then the WLCT (
299
) is well defined. Similarly, if
f\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R})
and
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}),1\leq p,q<\infty
and
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1,
then by Hölder’s inequality it follows that the WLCT (
299
) is well defined. Bahri et al.
[
21
]
proved that the WLCT satisfied the inequality
\|(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f)(\cdot,u)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi B}}\|g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}
for
g\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
and
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}).
They also established the relation between the WLCT and the WFT and obtained the Lieb’s inequality for the WLCT

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f)(x,u)\right|^{p}dxdu\leq\frac{2}{p}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}|B|^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{p}\|f\|^{p}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|g\|^{p}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

using the same (
198
) for the WFT
[
76
]
. The proof is simple as compared to the one given in
[
116
]
. Transform (
299
) is linear in
f,
anti-linear in
g.
It satisfies the time marginal constraints, i.e., integrating the WLCT with respect to the time variable gives the LCT multiplied by a constant, i.e.,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f)(x,u)dx=(\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t)dt)\mathcal{L}^{M}(f)(u)
and the additivity of rotation, i.e.,
\mathcal{L}^{M_{2}}((\mathcal{V}^{M_{1}}_{g}f)(x,u))(x,u^{\prime})=(\mathcal{V}^{M_{2}M_{1}}_{g}f)(x,u^{\prime}),
where
M_{l}=(A_{l},B_{l};C_{l},D_{l}),\penalty\ l=1,2
and
\det(M_{l})=1,\penalty\ B_{l}>0

[
115
]
. Apart from this, it has the following covariance properties

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}\left(\tau_{x_{0}}\mathcal{M}^{M}_{\mu}f\right)\right)(x,u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{i2\pi B}}e^{-\frac{i}{B}\left(\frac{D}{2}u^{2}-\frac{D}{2}\mu^{2}+\frac{D}{2}x_{0}^{2}-2tx_{0}\mu-x_{0}u\right)}\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f(x-x_{0},\frac{1}{B}[u-(\mu+Ax_{0})])

and

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}\left(\mathcal{M}^{M}_{\mu}\tau_{x_{0}}f\right)\right)(x,u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{i2\pi B}}e^{-\frac{i}{B}\left(\frac{D}{2}u^{2}-\frac{D}{2}\mu^{2}+\frac{D}{2}x_{0}^{2}-2tx_{0}\mu-x_{0}u\right)}\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f(x-x_{0},\frac{1}{B}[u-(\mu+Ax_{0})]),

where
\mathcal{M}^{M}_{\mu}f(t)=e^{-i\left(\frac{A}{2B}t^{2}-\frac{1}{B}t\mu+\frac{D}{2B}\mu^{2}\right)}f(t)
is generalized modulation by
\mu.
The covariance property obtained above indeed gives the formula for the WLCT for the time-frequency shift
\tau_{x_{0}}\mathcal{M}^{M}_{\mu}
or
\mathcal{M}^{M}_{\mu}\tau_{x_{0}}
of the function
f.
Specifically, the formula for the parity, conjugation,
\left(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}\left(\tau_{x_{0}}f\right)\right)(x,u)
and
\left(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}\left(e^{iu_{0}x}f\right)\right)(x,u)
can be found in
[
21
]
. For
f_{1},f_{2},g_{1},g_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
the WLCT (
299
) satisfies the following inner product relation
[
115
]
,
[
21
]

\displaystyle\left\langle(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g_{1}}f_{1})(x,u),(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g_{2}}f_{2})(x,u)\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle\overline{\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle}.

(300)

In particular, if
f_{1}=f_{2}=f
and
\|g_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\|g_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=1
then
\|\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
and thus WLCT is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The inversion formula for the WLCT is given as

\displaystyle f(t)=\frac{1}{\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g_{2}}f)(x,u)\overline{K_{M}(t,u)}\tau_{x}g_{1}(t)dudx

(301)

where
g_{1},g_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle\neq 0
and
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
In fact, a stronger version of the inversion formula (
301
) is proved by the authors in
[
115
]
. Its formulation resembles an
L^{2}-
function by an approximation procedure. For that a nested sequence of compact sets
K_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying
\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
K_{n}\subset\mbox{int}K_{n+1}
is considered and that for each
n\in\mathbb{N},

f_{n}(t)=\frac{1}{\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle}\int\int_{K_{n}}(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g_{2}}f)(x,u)\overline{K_{M}(t,u)}\tau_{x}g_{1}(t)dudx
then
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|f-f_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=0.
Bahri et al.
[
21
]
characterized the range
\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
of the transform (
299
) showing that
h\in\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
if and only if it satisfies
h(x^{\prime},u^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x,u)\langle g^{M}_{x,u},g^{M}_{x^{\prime},u^{\prime}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}dxdu.
Consequently, it follows that the range
\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is a RKHS with the kernel
\langle g^{M}_{x,u},g^{M}_{x^{\prime},u^{\prime}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},
where
g^{M}_{x,u}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{i2\pi B}}e^{-\frac{i}{2}\left(\frac{A}{B}t^{2}-\frac{2}{B}tu+\frac{D}{B}u^{2}\right)}\phi(t-x).
Bahri in
[
18
]
obtained a relation between the WLCT (
299
) and the WFT, using which he gave an alternative proof of the properties like orthogonality property and inversion formula. Han et al.
[
91
]
studied the inversion formula for recovering a signal from its WLCT different from the inversion formula (
301
) by invoking the double integral and showing that every signal can be recovered from its WLCT, with a univariate integral, with a suitable choice of window function
g
as

\displaystyle f(x)=\frac{1}{\overline{g(0)}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f)(x,u)\overline{K_{M}(x,u)}du,

(302)

which generalizes a similar result for the WFT
[
194
]
. More precisely, they proved that for
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}),
the integral in (
302
) is convergent in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
for all
1<p<\infty
by showing that

\lim_{u_{1},u_{2}\rightarrow\infty}\|R_{u_{1},u_{2}}f-\overline{g(x_{0})}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}=0

and they also proved
\lim_{u\rightarrow\infty}(R_{u,u}f)(x)=\overline{g(x_{0})}f(x),\penalty\ a.e.
, by applying the Carleson-Hunt theorem, showing that the integral (
302
) converges a.e. on
\mathbb{R},
where where
R_{u_{1},u_{2}}f(x)=\int_{-u_{1}}^{u_{2}}(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f)(x-x_{0},u)\overline{K_{M}(x,u)}du,

u_{1},u_{2}>0,

x_{0}
is a fixed real number and
g
is a continuous function such that
g,\hat{g}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}).
With the same assumption on
g,
authors in
[
91
]
obtained the recovery formula of function in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
from its WLCT by using the Cesàro summability method by showing that

\lim_{h\rightarrow\infty}\|\sigma_{h}f-\overline{g(x_{0})}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}=0,\penalty\ 1\leq p<\infty

and
\lim_{h\rightarrow\infty}(\sigma_{h}f)(x)=\overline{g(x_{0})}f(x),\penalty\ a.e.,

where
\sigma_{h}f(x)=\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{h}(R_{u,u}f)(x)du.
Using the convolution (
4
) given by Shi et al.
[
181
]
, Zhang et al.
[
226
]
introduced a novel WLCT, which is defined for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and the parameter
M_{l}=(A_{l},B_{l};C_{l},D_{l}),\penalty\ l=1,2,3
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{V}^{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}_{g}f)(t,u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tau)\overline{g_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}(t,u,\tau)}d\tau,

(303)

where
g_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}(t,u,\tau)=g(\tau-t)e^{i\frac{u\tau}{B_{1}}-i\frac{A_{1}}{2B_{1}}\tau^{2}-i\frac{A_{2}}{2B_{2}}(t-\tau)^{2}+i\frac{A_{3}}{2B_{3}}t^{2}}.
Equation (
303
) can be written in the generalized canonical convolution (
4
) form as

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{V}^{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}_{g}f\right)(t,u)=\left[\left(f(v)e^{-i\frac{u}{B_{1}}v}\right)\star_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}\overline{g(-v)}\right](t).

With a particular choice of the matrices i.e.,
M_{1}=M_{3}
and
M_{2}=(0,1,-1,0),
the transform reduces to the WLCT mentioned in
[
185
]
,
[
201
]
and for the choices
M_{1}=M_{3}=(\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha,-\sin\alpha,\cos\alpha),

M_{2}=(0,1,-1,0),
the transform (
303
) reduces to the novel WFrFT (
13
) which analyze the signal in one linear canonical and one Fourier domain, whereas it can be seen using the convolution theorem (
76
) that the transform analyzes the signal in two distinct linear canonical domains. Authors in
[
185
]
only defined the WLCT, for which the mathematical properties were recently explored by Wei et al.
[
201
]
. The linear transformation (
303
) have the covariance property

(\mathcal{V}^{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}_{g}(\tau_{t_{0}}f))(t,u)=e^{-i\frac{ut_{0}}{B_{1}}+i\frac{A_{1}}{2B_{1}}t_{0}^{2}+i\frac{A_{3}}{2B_{3}}t_{0}^{2}-i\frac{A_{3}}{B_{3}}tt_{0}}(\mathcal{V}^{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}_{g}f)(t-t_{0},u-A_{1}t_{0})

for classical time shift operator and for the linear canonical time shift
\tau^{M_{1}}_{t_{0}}f
the novel WLCT is given by

(\mathcal{V}^{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}_{g}(\tau^{M_{1}}_{t_{0}}f))(t,u)=e^{-i\frac{ut_{0}}{B_{1}}+i\frac{A_{3}}{2B_{3}}t_{0}^{2}-i\frac{A_{3}}{B_{3}}tt_{0}}(\mathcal{V}^{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}_{g}f)(t-t_{0},u),

where the operator is
\tau^{M_{1}}_{t_{0}}
is given by
\tau^{M_{1}}_{t_{0}}f(t)=f(t-t_{0})e^{-i\frac{A_{1}}{B_{1}}\left(t-\frac{t_{0}}{2}\right)t_{0}}.
Also the transform for the classical modulation of
f
is given as
(\mathcal{V}^{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}_{g}(\mathcal{M}_{u_{0}}f))(t,u)=(\mathcal{V}^{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}_{g}f)(t,u-B_{1}u_{0}).
Authors in
[
226
]
obtained the inner product relation

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{V}^{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}_{g}f_{1})(t,u)\overline{(\mathcal{V}^{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}_{g}f_{2})(t,u)}dtdu=2\pi|B_{1}|\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

(304)

followed by the inversion formula given by
f(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi|B_{1}|}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{V}^{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}_{g}f)(t,u)g_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}(t,u,x)dtdu.
With the assumption that
f_{1}=f_{2}=f
and
\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\frac{1}{2\pi},
it follows from (
304
), that the novel WLCT (
303
) is an isometry. Moreover, the range
\mathcal{V}^{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}_{g}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
is a reproducing kernel with the kernel given by
K(t,u;t_{0},u_{0})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}(t,u,\tau)\overline{g_{M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}}(t_{0},u_{0},\tau)}d\tau.
In view of equation (
303
) and its representation in the linear canonical domain obtained using (
76
), the WLCT gives the local information of
f(\tau)
in the time window
\left[E_{g}+t-\Delta_{g},E_{g}+t+\Delta_{g}\right]
and the local information of
f(\tau)
in the linear canonical domain as

\left[B_{3}E_{\tilde{\tilde{G}}}+B_{3}\left(\frac{u}{B_{1}}-\frac{A_{1}}{B_{1}}t+\frac{A_{3}}{B_{3}}t\right)-B_{3}\Delta_{\tilde{\tilde{G}}},B_{3}E_{\tilde{\tilde{G}}}+B_{3}\left(\frac{u}{B_{1}}-\frac{A_{1}}{B_{1}}t+\frac{A_{3}}{B_{3}}t\right)+B_{3}\Delta_{\tilde{\tilde{G}}}\right],

where
\tilde{\tilde{G}}(\xi)
is the FT of
\tilde{\tilde{g}}(y)=g(y)e^{-i\frac{A_{1}}{2B_{1}}y^{2}-i\frac{A_{2}}{2B_{2}}y^{2}+i\frac{A_{3}}{2B_{3}}y^{2}},
having the constant window area
4B_{3}\Delta_{g}\Delta_{\tilde{\tilde{G}}}
in the time-linear-canonical-frequency plane with the optimal window function as

g(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma}e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}e^{i\frac{A_{1}}{2B_{1}}t^{2}+i\frac{A_{2}}{2B_{2}}t^{2}-i\frac{A_{3}}{2B_{3}}t^{2}},

where
\sigma
denoted the window width in time domain.

Uncertainty Principles:
Bahri et al.
[
21
]
obtained the Heisenberg’s UP for the WLCT (
299
) using the Heisenberg’s UP
[
80
]
for the LCT and adopting the method used by Wilczok
[
209
]

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}|(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f)(x,u)|^{2}dxdu\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{2}|f(x)|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\frac{B}{2}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

and also gave the logarithmic UP for the transform

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|u||(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f)(x,u)|^{2}dxdu+\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|x||f(x)|^{2}dx\geq(D+\ln|B|)\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

(305)

Bahri in
[
18
]
proved that if
\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f
is
\epsilon-
concentrated on a measurable set
V\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R},
then for
r>0,

\mu(V)\geq(1-\epsilon^{2})^{\frac{r}{r-2}}(2\pi B).
Moreover, the author obtained the local UP for the WLCT (
299
), using its relation with the FT and the local UP for the FT, as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{BX}|(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f)(x,u)|^{2}dxdu\leq\begin{cases}C_{\alpha}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}[\mu(X)]^{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x|^{2\alpha}|f(x)|^{2}dx,\penalty\ 0<\alpha<\frac{1}{2}\\
C_{\alpha}\mu(X)\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x)|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x|^{2\alpha}|f(x)|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}},\penalty\ \alpha>\frac{1}{2}\end{cases}

(306)

where
C_{\alpha}
is some constant. Hleili
[
100
]
obtained the entropy UP for the WLCT,

E\left(|\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f|^{2}\right)\geq\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\left(\ln\left(2\pi|B|\right)-\ln\left(\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\right)\right).

Using the logarithmic Sobolev-type UP for the FT give by Kubo et al.
[
119
]
, author in
[
18
]
also obtained the logarithmic Sobolev-type inequality for the WLCT

\displaystyle\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x)|^{2}\ln\left(\frac{1+|x|^{2}}{2}\right)dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|u||(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f)(x,u)|^{2}dxdu\geq\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}+\ln|B|\right)\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x)|^{2}dx

(307)

for the window function
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{1}^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
where the Sobolev space
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})=\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Df\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\},

D
stands for differential operator and the weighted Lebesgue space
L_{s}^{p}(\mathbb{R})=\{f\in L_{loc}^{p}(\mathbb{R})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(1+|x|^{2})^{\frac{s}{2}}f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\},\penalty\ s>0.
Using the property that
\ln
function is an increasing function, equation (
305
) can be obtained from (
307
). Using the method adopted by Wilczok
[
209
]
, Hleili
[
100
]
proved that
\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f
cannot have a support of finite measure. The author also proved that the portion of
\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f
lying outside some set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
cannot be arbitrarily small. For sufficiently small
S,
this fact is justified by the following theorem

Theorem 16.1
.

(Concentration of
\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f
on a small set) Let
g
be a window function and
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\frac{\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{2\pi|B|}\lambda^{(2)}(S)<1.
Then, for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
arbitrary

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f-\chi_{S}\cdot\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\geq\left(\sqrt{1-\frac{\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{2\pi|B|}\lambda^{(2)}(S)}\right)\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

(308)

Apart from this, the author also studied the UP for the orthonormal sequence. They proved that if the WLCT of each member of an orthonormal sequence is
\epsilon-
concentrated in a set of finite measure then the sequence is necessarily finite. More precisely, if
s>0,0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}},

G_{s}=\{(x,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x^{2}+u^{2}\leq s^{2}\},

\{\phi_{n}\}_{1\leq n\leq N}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is an orthonormal sequence and
\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}\phi_{n}
is
\epsilon-
concentrated in
G_{s}
then
N
is bounded above by
\frac{s^{2}\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{2|B|\left(1-\epsilon\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\right)}.
As a consequence the Shapiro’s dispersion theorem for the WLCT is obtained, which states that if
\{\phi_{n}\}_{1\leq n\leq N}
is an orthonormal sequence in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
then for every
p>0
and
N\in\mathbb{N},

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left\{\rho_{p}\left(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}\phi_{n}\right)\right\}^{p}\geq N^{\frac{p}{2}+1}\left(\frac{3|B|}{2^{\frac{4}{p}}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}},

where
\rho_{p}\left(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f\right)=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}|(x,u)|^{p}|(\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f)(x,u)|^{2}dxdu\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},\penalty\ |(x,u)|=\sqrt{x^{2}+u^{2}},
is the generalized
p^{th}
time-frequency dispersion of
\mathcal{V}^{M}_{g}f.

17
Linear canonical wavelet transform (LCWT)

Compared to the FrFT with one extra degree of freedom, LCT is more flexible for its extra degree of three degrees of freedom and has been used frequently in time-frequency analysis and non-stationary signal processing. Inspired by the FrWT, Wei et al.
[
202
]
introduced a new transform by combining the idea of LCT and the WT, called the LCWT. This definition is obtained by replacing the classical convolution in (
208
) by the linear canonical convolution (
73
) and is defined for a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and the function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying the admissibility condition
c_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\hat{\psi}(\xi)|^{2}}{\xi}d\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\hat{\psi}(-\xi)|^{2}}{\xi}d\xi<\infty,
where
\hat{\psi}(\xi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)e^{-it\xi}dt,
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=

\displaystyle\left(f\star^{\prime}_{M}\delta_{a}\tilde{\psi}\right)(b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\psi^{M}_{a,b}(t)}dt,

(309)

where
\psi^{M}_{a,b}(t)=e^{-i\frac{A(t^{2}-b^{2})}{2B}}\psi_{a,b}(t),\penalty\ a\in\mathbb{R}^{+},b\in\mathbb{R}.
In particular, for
M=(\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha,-\sin\alpha,\cos\alpha),
it reduces to the FrWT (
14
). The LCWT (
17
) can be expressed as
(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=e^{-i\frac{Ab^{2}}{2B}}\left(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}[f(t)e^{-\frac{Ab^{2}}{2B}}]\right)(a,b),
in term of the classical WT. The transform is linear and satisfy the following convolution formula
(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}(f\star_{M}g))(a,b)=(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}g)(a,b),
for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
Based on the convolution theorem (
74
) and the inverse of LCT, the transform (
17
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\sqrt{2\pi a}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}f\right)(\xi)\overline{\hat{\psi}\left(\frac{a\xi}{B}\right)}K_{M^{-1}}(\xi,b)d\xi.

(310)

As a consequence of which the authors in
[
202
]
,
[
85
]
obtained the inner product theorem

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}g)(a,b)}\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}=2\pi|B|c_{\psi}\langle f,g\rangle,

(311)

followed by the inversion formula

f(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi|B|c_{\psi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\psi^{M}_{a,b}(t)\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}.

given in
[
85
]
. In view of (
17
) and (
310
), it follows that the linear canonical wavelet transform is capable of providing the time and spectral information simultaneous and representing signal in the time-fractional-frequency domain. It gives local information of the signal
f(t)
with the time window
\left[b+aE_{\psi}-a\Delta_{\psi},b+aE_{\psi}+a\Delta_{\psi}\right]
and the local information about the linear canonical spectrum of
f(t)
as
\left[\frac{E_{\hat{\psi}}}{a}-\frac{\Delta_{\hat{\psi}}}{a},\frac{E_{\hat{\psi}}}{a}+\frac{\Delta_{\hat{\psi}}}{a}\right]B,
with the constant window area
4B\Delta_{\psi}\Delta_{\hat{\psi}}
in the time-LCT-frequency plane. This area depends on the function
\psi
and
B
and is independent of the parameters
a
and
b.
Thus, the LCWT (
17
) is capable of providing time and linear canonical domain information simultaneously and representing the signal in the time-LCT-frequency plane with the transform parameter
M.
Prasad et al.
[
156
]
explored further the LCWT (
17
), with the alternative admissible condition of
\psi
as
C^{M}_{\psi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}[e^{-\frac{\char 16\relax a}{2b}(\cdot)}\psi]\right)(\xi)\right|^{2}}{|\xi|}d\xi<\infty.
They studied the properties like dilation property, parity and also obtained the formula
(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(\lambda a,\lambda b)=\lambda^{n+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{W}^{M^{\prime}}_{\psi}f)(a,b),\penalty\ M^{\prime}=(\lambda^{2}A,B,C,D)
when
\psi
is a homogeneous function of degree
n.
They also generalized the inner product relation (
311
) in the context of two linear canonical wavelet as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi_{1}}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi_{2}}g)(a,b)}\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}=2\pi|B|c^{M}_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}\langle f,g\rangle,

where
c^{M}_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\overline{\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}[e^{-\frac{\char 16\relax A}{2B}(\cdot)}\psi_{1}]\right)(a)}\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}[e^{-\frac{\char 16\relax A}{2B}(\cdot)}\psi_{2}]\right)(a)\frac{da}{a}<\infty.
With the assumption that
c^{M}_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}=1,
Ansari et al.
[
10
]
obtained the Calder̀on’s reproducing formula given as

\displaystyle f(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi|B|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left(\left(f(\cdot)\star_{M}(\psi_{1})_{a}(\cdot)\right)\star_{M}\overline{(\psi_{2})}(-(\cdot))\right)(t)\frac{da}{a^{2}},

where
\psi_{a}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\overline{\psi}\left(\frac{-t}{a}\right).
Authors also established the Abelian theorem, which relates the behavior of LCWT
(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)
as
a\rightarrow\infty(a\rightarrow 0)
to the behavior of function
(\mathcal{L}^{M}f)(\xi)
as
\xi\rightarrow\infty(\xi\rightarrow 0).
These results are also referred to as initial value and final value Abelian theorems. Guo et al.
[
85
]
considered the test function space
\mathcal{S}_{M}(\mathbb{R})
defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{M}(\mathbb{R})=\{f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}f\in C^{\infty},\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|t^{\alpha}\Delta_{t}^{\beta}f(t)|<\infty,\penalty\ \alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}\},

(312)

where
\Delta_{t}=\frac{d}{dt}-it\frac{A}{B}.
It is equipped with the topology generated by the collection of seminorms
\gamma^{M}_{\alpha,\beta}
defined by
\gamma^{M}_{\alpha,\beta}(f)=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|t^{\alpha}\Delta_{t}^{\beta}f(t)|.
It is a Frèchet space and reduces to the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}),
when
M=(0,1,-1,0)
and proved that the LCT is a homeomorphism from
\mathcal{S}_{M}(\mathbb{R})
onto itself. As a subset of the dual
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{M}(\mathbb{R})
of
\mathcal{S}_{M}(\mathbb{R})
authors in
[
85
]
also considered a generalized Sobolev space
H^{p,s}_{M}
defined by

H^{p,s}_{M}=\{f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{M}(\mathbb{R}),\xi^{\frac{s}{p}}(\mathcal{L}^{M}f)(\xi)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\},\penalty\ 1\leq p<\infty,\penalty\ s\in\mathbb{R},

with norm given as
\|f\|_{H^{p,s}_{M}}=\|\xi^{\frac{s}{p}}(\mathcal{L}^{M}f)(\xi)\|_{L^{p}}
and proved that the operator
\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}
(
17
) is a bounded linear operator from
H^{p,s}_{M}
into
H^{p,s}_{q,M}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
for
1\leq p\leq 2,\penalty\ \frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1,
with
\psi
satisfying
c^{s,q}_{\psi,M}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{|\mathcal{L}^{M}[e^{-\frac{-iA\xi^{2}}{2B}}\psi(\xi)](a\xi)|^{q}}{a|\xi|^{s}}\frac{da}{a}
is a constant independent of
\xi,
where
H^{p,s}_{q,M}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
is the set of all measurable functions
\varphi(a,b)
satisfying
\|\varphi(a,b)\|_{H^{p,s}_{q,M}}=\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left(|\varphi(a,b)|^{p}db\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}a^{-s-1}da\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}<\infty.
. With the notation
\mathfrak{M}_{c}
and
K_{\eta}
given in definition
14.1
, Prasad et al.
[
155
]
considered the space
S^{M}_{\eta},
the collection of
\phi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\phi
and
\hat{\phi}
are smooth functions and for every
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\lambda\geq 0,

p_{l,\lambda,M}(\phi)=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}e^{\lambda\eta(t)}|\Delta_{t}^{l}\phi(t)|<\infty
and
r_{l,\lambda,M}(\phi)=\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}e^{\lambda\eta(\xi)}|\Delta_{\xi}^{l}\hat{\phi}(\xi)|<\infty,
where
\Delta_{t}=\frac{d}{dt}-i\frac{A}{B}t.
The topology on
S^{M}_{\eta}
is defined by the semi norms
p_{l,\lambda,M}(\phi)
and
