r_{l,\lambda,M}(\phi).
The elements of the dual
(S^{M}_{\eta})^{\prime}
are called the generalized ultra-distributions. Note that for
\eta(\xi)=\log(1+|\xi|),M=(0,1,-1,0)
and
\eta(\xi)=\xi^{\sigma}(0<\sigma<1),M=(0,1,-1,0)
the space
S^{M}_{\eta}
reduces to the Schwartz space and the Gevrey space of test functions, respectively. Author also considered generalized Sobolev space
B^{\eta,M}_{p,k}(\mathbb{R}),
defined as the space of all the generalized ultra-distribution
f
in
(S^{M}_{\eta})^{\prime}
such that
\|f\|_{p,k,M}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|k(\xi)(\mathcal{L}^{M}f)(\xi)|^{p}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty
and
\|f\|_{\infty,k,M}=ess\sup|k(\xi)(\mathcal{L}^{M}f)(\xi)|.
The space
B^{\eta,M}_{p,k}(\mathbb{R})
reduces to the Hörmander space
B_{p,k}(\mathbb{R}),
when
\eta(\xi)=\log(1+|\xi|)
and
M=(0,1,-1,0).
They determine the
B^{\eta,M}_{p,k}(\mathbb{R})-
distance of two LCWT with different argument functions and the wavelet to study the dependence of the transform on its wavelets and its argument and determining the LCWT in terms of linear canonical convolution they proves that if
f\in B^{\eta,M}_{p,k}(\mathbb{R})
and
\psi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(t)dt=1,
then
\psi_{a}\star_{M}f\rightarrow f
in
B^{\eta,M}_{p,k}(\mathbb{R})
as
a\rightarrow 0.
Apart from this authors in
[
155
]
defined the convolution of LCWTs
(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi_{1}}f)(a,b)
and
(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi_{1}}f)(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
for
\psi_{1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

\psi_{2}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
denoted by
\left[\left(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi_{1}}\circ\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi_{2}}\right)f\right](a,a^{\prime},b)=\left(\mathcal{W}^{M}f\right)(a,a^{\prime},b)
is defined by

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{W}^{M}f\right)(a,a^{\prime},b)=\left(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi_{1}}\left(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi_{2}}\right)(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\right)(a,a^{\prime},b),

(313)

which can equivalently written as

\left(\mathcal{W}^{M}f\right)(a,a^{\prime},b)=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi Baa^{\prime}}{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i\frac{D}{2B}\xi^{2}+\frac{i}{B}b\xi-i\frac{A}{2B}b^{2}+i\frac{D}{2B}(a^{2}+a^{\prime 2})\xi^{2}}\overline{\mathcal{L}^{M}[e^{-i\frac{A}{2B}(\cdot)^{2}}\psi_{1}](a\xi)}\overline{\mathcal{L}^{M}[e^{-i\frac{A}{2B}(\cdot)^{2}}\psi_{2}](a^{\prime}\xi)}(\mathcal{L}^{M}_{b^{\prime}}f)(\xi)d\xi,

where
\mathcal{L}^{M}_{b^{\prime}}
denoted the LCWT with respect to the variable
b^{\prime}.
Authors obtained several important properties like translation, parity, dilation, etc for the composition (
313
) of the LCWTs along with the inner product relation

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left(\mathcal{W}^{M}f\right)(a,a^{\prime},b)\overline{\left(\mathcal{W}^{M}g\right)(a,a^{\prime},b)}\frac{dada^{\prime}db}{aa^{\prime}}=4\pi^{2}Bc^{M}_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}\langle f,g\rangle,

with the assumption that
A=B
and
c^{M}_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}|\mathcal{L}^{M}[e^{-i\frac{A}{2B}(\cdot)^{2}}\psi_{1}](a\xi)|^{2}|\mathcal{L}^{M}[e^{-i\frac{A}{2B}(\cdot)^{2}}\psi_{2}](a^{\prime}\xi)|^{2}\frac{dada^{\prime}}{aa^{\prime}}<\infty.
Furthermore, if
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is a continuous function at
t\in\mathbb{R},
then
f
can be reconstructed by the formula

f(t)=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}B^{2}c^{M}_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left(\mathcal{W}^{M}f\right)(a,a^{\prime},b)\overline{\left(\mathcal{W}^{M}(\tau_{B}\delta)\right)(a,a^{\prime},b)}\frac{dada^{\prime}db}{aa^{\prime}},

where
\tau_{B}\delta
denote the translation of Dirac delta function. Prasad et al.
[
157
]
considered that the generalized Sobolev space
H^{p,s}_{q,M}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
is the set of all measurable functions
\varphi(a,a^{\prime},b)
satisfying
\|\varphi(a,a^{\prime},b)\|_{H^{p,s}_{q,M}}=\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left(|\varphi(a,a^{\prime},b)|^{p}db\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}(aa^{\prime})^{-s-1}dada^{\prime}\right]^{\frac{1}{q}}<\infty,
where
1\leq p,\penalty\ q<\infty,\penalty\ s\in\mathbb{R}
and proved that the composition operator
\mathcal{W}^{M}
(
313
) is a bounded linear operator from
H^{p,s}_{M}
into

H^{p,s}_{q,M}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
for
1\leq p\leq 2,\penalty\ q=\frac{p}{p-1},
with the linear canonical wavelet
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
satisfying
c^{s,q}_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2},M}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}\left[e^{-\frac{iAt^{2}}{2B}}\psi_{1}(t)\right]\right)(a\xi)\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}\left[e^{-\frac{iAt^{2}}{2B}}\psi_{2}(t)\right]\right)(a^{\prime}\xi)\right|^{q}}{\left(aa^{\prime}|\xi|\right)^{s}}\frac{dada^{\prime}}{aa^{\prime}}
is independent of
\xi
where
H^{p,s}_{M}
is defined in (
312
). Gupta et al.
[
90
]
introduced a novel LCWT for a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the linear canonical wavelet
\psi,
i.e.,
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying the admissible condition
c^{M}_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|(\mathcal{L}^{M}\psi)\left(\frac{\xi}{a}\right)\right|^{2}\frac{da}{a}
is a positive constant independent of
\xi
satisfying
|\xi|=1,
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{iA}{2B}t}\left\{f(t)e^{\frac{iA}{2B}t}\star\overline{\sqrt{a}\psi(-at)e^{-]\frac{iA}{2B}t}}\right\}(b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\psi^{M}_{a,b}(t)}dt,

(314)

where
\psi^{M}_{a,b}(t)=e^{-\frac{iA}{2B}\{(t^{2}-b^{2})-(a(t-b))^{2}\}}\sqrt{a}\psi(a(t-b))

which can equivalently be written in the linear canonical domain as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\frac{\sqrt{-2\pi iB}}{\sqrt{a}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\frac{iD}{2B}\left(\frac{\xi}{a}\right)^{2}}(\mathcal{L}^{M}f)(\xi)\overline{(\mathcal{L}^{M}\psi)(\frac{\xi}{a})}K_{M^{-1}}(b,\xi)d\xi.

(315)

Apart from the basic properties of the (
17
) like linearity, anti-linearity and the for the translation and dilation formula of the transform author obtain the inner product relation
\langle\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi_{1}}f,\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi_{2}}g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R})}=2\pi c^{M}_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},
followed by the inversion formula
f(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi|B|c^{M}_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi_{1}}f)(a,b)(\psi_{2})^{M}_{a,b}(t)dadb,
a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R},
where
c^{M}_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\overline{(\mathcal{L}^{M}\psi_{1})\left(\frac{\xi}{a}\right)}(\mathcal{L}^{M}\psi_{2})\left(\frac{\xi}{a}\right)\frac{da}{a}
is a non-zero complex constant independent of
\xi
satisfying
|\xi|=1.

In view of (
17
) and (
315
), it follows that the LCWT (
17
) localizes the signal
f
in the time window

\left[\frac{1}{a}E_{\psi}+b-\frac{1}{a}\Delta_{\psi},\frac{1}{a}E_{\psi}+b+\frac{1}{a}\Delta_{\psi}\right]
and the linear canonical domain window
\left[aE_{\mathcal{L}^{M}\psi}-a\Delta_{\mathcal{L}^{M}\psi},aE_{\mathcal{L}^{M}\psi}+a\Delta_{\mathcal{L}^{M}\psi}\right],
respectively, with the joint resolution in the time and linear canonical domain window as

\left[\frac{1}{a}E_{\psi}+b-\frac{1}{a}\Delta_{\psi},\frac{1}{a}E_{\psi}+b+\frac{1}{a}\Delta_{\psi}\right]\times\left[aE_{\mathcal{L}^{M}\psi}-a\Delta_{\mathcal{L}^{M}\psi},aE_{\mathcal{L}^{M}\psi}+a\Delta_{\mathcal{L}^{M}\psi}\right],

with constant area
4\Delta_{\psi}\Delta_{\mathcal{L}^{M}\psi}
in the time-LCT-frequency plane. Thus, for a given parameter
M,
the window area depends on the linear canonical admissible wavelets and is independent of the parameters
a
and
b.
But it is noted that the window gets narrower for a large value of
a
and wider for a small value of
a.
Thus, the window given by the transform is flexible, and hence, it can simultaneously provide the time-linear canonical domain information.

Uncertainty Principles:
Bahri et al.
[
31
]
studied the UPs associated with the LCWT (
17
). Adopting the method in
[
209
]
, they obtained the generalized Heisenberg and logarithmic UP for the transform using the same for the LCT given, respectively as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}b^{p}\left|(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\right|^{p}db\frac{da}{a^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi^{p}\left|(\mathcal{L}^{M}f)(\xi)\right|^{p}d\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\geq B^{1+\frac{1}{p}}\sqrt{\frac{\pi c^{M}_{\psi}}{2}}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|b|\left|(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\right|^{2}db\frac{da}{a^{2}}+2\pi Bc^{M}_{\psi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|\xi|\left|(\mathcal{L}^{M}f)(\xi)\right|^{2}d\xi\geq(D+\ln|B|)2\pi Bc^{M}_{\psi}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

followed by the local UP for which states that if
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}
and
B_{r_{0}}=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|(a,b)|<r_{0}\},

r_{0}\in(0,1]
such that
0<\frac{m(E)}{2\pi Bc^{M}_{\psi}},\frac{m(B_{r_{0}})}{2\pi Bc^{M}_{\psi}}<1
and
\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=1
then for all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
l>0
then

\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi Bc^{M}_{\psi}-m(E)}}\left(\int\int_{E^{c}}\left|(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\right|^{2}db\frac{da}{a^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

and

\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\frac{1}{r_{0}^{l}\sqrt{2\pi Bc^{M}_{\psi}-m(B_{r_{0}})}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|(a,b)|^{2l}\left|(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\right|^{2}db\frac{da}{a^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

where
m(E)
represents the Lebesgue measure of
E
and
E^{c}=\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}\setminus E.
Authors in
[
90
]
obtained the Donoho-Stark’s UP for the LCWT (
17
), which gives a lower bound of the measure of essential support of the
\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f,
, i.e., if
\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f
is
\epsilon-
concentrated on a measurable set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}
, then

|\Omega|\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\geq 2\pi|B|c^{M}_{\psi}(1-\epsilon^{2}),\penalty\ 0\leq\epsilon<1.
Using the method adopted by Wilczok
[
209
]
, they proved that
\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f
cannot have a support of finite measure. The author also proved that the portion of
\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f
(
17
) lying outside some set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
cannot be arbitrarily small. For sufficiently small
\Omega,
this fact is justified by the following theorem

Theorem 17.1
.

(Concentration of
\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f
on a small set)
Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

\psi
is an ALCW and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}
such that
|\Omega|<\frac{2\pi|B|C_{\psi,M}}{\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}.
Then

\|\chi_{\Omega^{c}}W_{\psi}^{M}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R})}\geq\sqrt{2\pi|B|C^{M}_{\psi}-|\Omega|\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

Apart from this, the author also studied the UP for the orthonormal sequence. They proved that if the LCWT (
17
) of each member of an orthonormal sequence is
\epsilon-
concentrated in a set of finite measure, then the sequence is necessarily finite. More precisely, if
s>0,0<\epsilon<1,

G_{s}=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}a^{2}+b^{2}\leq s^{2}\},

\{\phi_{n}\}_{n\in\wedge}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\penalty\ \wedge\subset\mathbb{N},
is an orthonormal sequence and
\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}\left(\frac{\phi_{n}}{\sqrt{2\pi|B|c^{M}_{\psi}}}\right)
is
\epsilon-
concentrated in
G_{s}
for all
n\in\wedge,
then the cardinality of
\wedge
is bounded above by
\frac{s^{2}\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{4|B|c^{M}_{\psi}\left(1-\epsilon\right)}.
As a consequence of which the Shapiro’s dispersion theorem for the LCWT (
17
) is obtained, which states that if
\{\phi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an orthonormal sequence in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
then for every
p>0
and non-empty finite
\wedge\subset\mathbb{N},

\sum_{n\in\wedge}\left\{\rho_{p}\left(W_{\psi}^{M}\left(\frac{\phi_{n}}{\sqrt{2\pi|B|C_{\psi,M}}}\right)\right)\right\}^{p}\geq\frac{(Card(\wedge))^{\frac{p}{2}+1}}{2^{p+1}}\left(\frac{3|B|C_{\psi,M}}{2^{\frac{4}{p}+2}\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}.

where
\rho_{p}\left(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f\right)=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}}|(a,b)|^{p}|(\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)|^{2}dadb\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},\penalty\ |(a,b)|=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}},
is the generalized
p^{th}
time-frequency dispersion of
\mathcal{W}^{M}_{\psi}f.

18
Linear Canonical Stockwell Transform (LCST)

Bahri et al.
[
32
]
replaced the Fourier kernel in (
228
) by the kernel
K^{M}
of the LCT to define the LCST for the function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the window function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=|a|\int_{\mathbb{R}}K^{M}(x,a)f(x)\overline{\psi(a(x-b))}dx,\penalty\ x\in\mathbb{R},\penalty\ a\in\mathbb{R}_{0}.

(316)

and obtained the formula for the translation and modulation of the function, including the inner product relation and the reconstruction formula. Later, Bahri et al.
[
17
]
established the Heisenberg uncertainty and logarithmic UPs for the transform (
316
). Replacing the classical convolution in (
231
) by the linear canonical convolution (
4
), Shah et al.
[
175
]
defined the LCST of a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the window function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{-a}f\star_{M}\mathcal{D}_{\xi}\tilde{\psi}\right)(b),

(317)

where
\tilde{\psi}(t)=\overline{\psi(-t)}
and
\star_{M}
is the convolution given by (
4
). It is a matter of simple verification that
\mathcal{S}_{\psi}f
is linear in
f
and conjugate linear in
\psi.
Transform (
317
) has an equivalent form

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}^{M}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\psi^{M}_{a,b}(t)}dt,

(318)

where
\psi^{M}_{a,b}(t)=a\psi\left(a(t-b)\right)e^{iat+\frac{iAt(b-t)}{B}},

a\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\penalty\ b\in\mathbb{R}.
It reduces to the FrST for
M=(\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha,-\sin\alpha,\cos\alpha),

\alpha\neq n\pi,\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{Z}
, Fresnel-Stockwell transform for
M=(1,B,0,1),\penalty\ B\neq 0
and the classical ST for
M=(0,1,-1,0).
Authors obtained the formula for the LCST of the translation and dilation of
f
as

(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{M}f(t-k))(a,b)=e^{-i\left(\frac{Ak}{B}(k+b)+ak\right)}(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{M}(\mathcal{M}_{-\frac{Ak}{B}}f))(a,b+k)

and

(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{M}f(\lambda t))(a,b)=(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{\tilde{M}}f)\left(\frac{a}{\lambda},\lambda b\right),\penalty\ \lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\penalty\ \tilde{M}=(A,\lambda^{2}B,C,D)

If further, the function
\psi
satisfies the admissible conditions
c_{\psi}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}\left[e^{i\left(t-\frac{At^{2}}{2B}\left(1+\frac{1}{a^{2}}\right)\right)}\psi(t)\right]\right)(\frac{\xi}{a})\right|da<\infty
then
\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{M}
satisfies the inner product relations

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{M}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{M}g)(a,b)}dadb=c_{\psi}\langle f,g\rangle

followed by the inversion formula

f(t)=\frac{1}{c_{\psi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{M}f)(a,b)\psi^{M}_{a,b}(t)dadb,\penalty\ a.e.
As a consequence of the inner product relation it follows that, if
c_{\psi}=1
then the operator
\mathcal{S}^{M}_{\psi}
is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R})
and the range
\mathcal{S}^{M}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
is a RKHS with the kernel
K^{M}_{\psi}(a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime})=\langle\psi^{M}_{a,b},\psi^{M}_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\rangle.
In view of (
318
) and its representation in the linear canonical domain, it follows that the LCST is capable of providing the time and spectral information simultaneous and representing signal in the time-LCT-frequency domain. It gives local information of the signal
f(t)
with the time window
\left[b+\frac{E_{\psi}}{a}-\frac{\Delta_{\psi}}{a},b+\frac{E_{\psi}}{a}+\frac{\Delta_{\psi}}{a}\right]
and the local information about the linear canonical spectrum of
f(t)
as
\left[aE_{H}-a\Delta_{H},aE_{H}+a\Delta_{H}\right],
where
H(\xi)=\left(\mathcal{L}^{M}\left[e^{i\left(t-\frac{At^{2}}{2B}\left(1+\frac{1}{a^{2}}\right)\right)}\psi(t)\right]\right)(\xi)
with the constant window area
4B\Delta_{\psi}\Delta_{H}
in the time-LCT-frequency plane. This area depends on the the function
\psi
and
B
and is independent of the parameters
a
and
b.
Thus the LCST is capable of providing time and linear canonical domain information simultaneously and representing signal in the time-LCT-frequency plane with the transform parameter
M.
Wei et al.
[
208
]
extended the Gaussian function
g(t,v)
to the linear canonical domain as
g(t,v)=\frac{|\frac{v}{B}|^{p}}{\sqrt{2\pi}q}e^{-\frac{t^{2}\left(\frac{v}{B}\right)^{2p}}{2q^{2}}}
and used this function as the window function and the linear canonical convolution (
73
) to define the LCST of a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}^{M}f)(\tau,v)=

\displaystyle[e^{-i2\pi\frac{v}{B}t}f(t)]\star^{\prime}_{M}g(-t,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)g_{M}(t-\tau,v)e^{-i2\pi\frac{v}{B}t}dt

(319)

where the window function
g_{M}(t-\tau,v)
is given by
g_{M}(t-\tau,v)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}q}|\frac{v}{B}|^{p}e^{-\frac{(t-\tau)^{2}}{2q^{2}}\left(\frac{v}{B}\right)^{2p}}e^{i\frac{A}{2B}(t^{2}-\tau^{2})}.
This transform is linear and have the translation and scaling formula as
(\mathcal{S}^{M}f(t-t_{0}))(\tau,v)=e^{i\frac{A}{B}(-\tau t_{0}+t_{0}^{2})-i2\pi\frac{v}{B}t_{0}}(\mathcal{S}^{M}[f(t)e^{i\frac{A}{B}t_{0}t}])(\tau-t_{0},v)=
and
(\mathcal{S}^{M^{\prime}}f(kt))(\tau,v)=(\mathcal{S}^{M}[f(t)e^{-i2\pi\frac{v}{B}\left(\frac{1}{k}-1\right)t}])(k\tau,v),

M^{\prime}=(\frac{A}{k^{2}},B,C,D).
Moreover, it satisfies the inner product relation
\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}^{M}f)(\tau,v)\overline{(\mathcal{S}^{M}g)(\tau,v)}=C_{g}\langle f,g\rangle
with the
1-D
inversion formula

f(t)=\frac{1}{C_{g}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{g(0,v)}(\mathcal{S}^{M}f)(\tau,v)e^{i2\pi\frac{v}{B}t}d\frac{v}{B},\penalty\ g(0,v)\neq 0

and the 2-D inversion formula as

f(t)=\frac{1}{C_{g}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}^{M}f)(t-\tau,v)e^{-i\frac{A}{2B}(t^{2}-\tau^{2})}e^{i2\pi\frac{v}{B}t}d\tau\frac{dv}{v},

where
C_{g}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|G\left(\frac{u-2\pi v}{B}\right)\right|^{2}\frac{dv}{v}
is independent of
u
in
\mathbb{R}_{0}
with
G\left(\frac{u-2\pi v}{B}\right)=e^{-2\pi^{2}q^{2}\frac{(u-2\pi v)^{2}}{v^{2}}}.
Authors in
[
208
]
obtained the convolution theorem and the cross-correlation theorem for the transform (
18
) along with that they also showed that the range
\mathcal{S}^{M}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
is a RKHS with the kernel
K_{g_{M}}(\tau_{0},v_{0};\tau,v)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\overline{g_{M}(t-\tau,v)}g_{M}(t-\tau_{0},v_{0})e^{i2\pi\frac{(v-v_{0})}{B}t}dt
and proved that the transform (
18
) is capable of representing the signal in the time-linear canonical domain plane by jointly time and linear canonical domain information with the time-linear canonical-frequency window
[E_{g}+\tau-\Delta_{g},E_{g}+\tau+\Delta_{g}]\times[BE_{G}+2\pi v-B\Delta_{G},BE_{G}+2\pi v+B\Delta_{G}],
with constant window area
2B\Delta_{e}\Delta_{G}
in the time-LCT domain-frequency plane. Gupta et al.
[
gupta2024newST
]
gave a more general definition of a LCST. They considered the matrix parameters
M_{l}=(A_{l},B_{l};C_{l},D_{l}),\penalty\ l=1,2
with
A_{l}D_{l}-B_{l}C_{l}=1\penalty\ \mbox{and}\penalty\ B_{l}\neq 0,\penalty\ l=1,2
and defined the LCST for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the window function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{\psi}f\right)(a,b)=

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{iA_{1}}{2B_{1}}b^{2}}\left\{\tilde{f}(t)e^{\frac{iA_{1}}{2B_{1}}t^{2}}\star\overline{a\psi(-at)e^{\frac{iA_{2}}{2B_{2}}(at)^{2}}}\right\}(b),\penalty\ a\in\mathbb{R^{+}},b\in\mathbb{R}

(320)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\psi^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{a,b}(t)}dt,

(321)

where
\tilde{f}(t)=e^{-i\frac{at}{B_{1}}}f(t),

\psi^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{a,b}(t)=e^{-\frac{iA_{1}}{2B_{1}}(t^{2}-B^{2})+\frac{iA_{2}}{2B_{2}}\{a(t-b)\}^{2}+\frac{iat}{B_{1}}}a\psi(a(t-b))
and
\star
denotes the classical convolution. Depending upon the different choices of the matrices
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
we have different families of novel integral transforms, i.e.,

•

For
M_{1}=(\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha;-\sin\alpha,\cos\alpha),\penalty\ M_{2}=(\cos\beta,\sin\beta;-\sin\beta,\cos\beta),\penalty\ \alpha,\beta\neq n\pi\penalty\ (n\in\mathbb{Z}),
(
320
) give rise to a novel FrST
\left(\mathcal{S}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\psi}f\right)(a,b)=a\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\psi\left(a(t-b)\right)}e^{\frac{i}{2}(t^{2}-b^{2})\cot\alpha-\frac{i}{2}\left\{a(t-b)\right\}^{2}\cot\beta-iat\csc\alpha}dt.

•

For
M_{1}=(1,B_{1};0,1),\penalty\ M_{2}=(1,B_{2};0,1),\penalty\ B_{1},B_{2}\neq 0,
(
320
) give rise to novel Fresnel Stockwell transform
\left(\mathcal{S}^{B_{1},B_{2}}_{\psi}f\right)(a,b)=a\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\psi\left(a(t-b)\right)}e^{\frac{iA_{1}}{2B_{1}}(t^{2}-b^{2})-\frac{iA_{2}}{2B_{2}}\left\{a(t-b)\right\}^{2}-\frac{iat}{B_{1}}}dt.

•

For
M_{1}=M_{2}=(0,1;-1,0),
(
320
) reduces to the classical ST

\left(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}f\right)(a,b)=a\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\psi\left(a(t-b)\right)}e^{-iat}dt.

Authors obtained the LCST of the translation and dilation of
f
as

\left(\mathcal{S}^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{\psi}f(\lambda t)\right)(a,b)=\left(\mathcal{S}^{\tilde{M_{1}},M_{2}}_{\psi}f\right)\left(\frac{a}{\lambda},b\lambda\right)

and
\left(\mathcal{S}^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{\psi}f(t-y)\right)(a,b)=e^{-\frac{iay}{B_{1}}-\frac{iA_{1}}{B_{1}}y(b-y)}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\tilde{M_{1}},M_{2}}_{\psi}\left\{e^{\frac{iA_{1}}{B_{1}}yt}f(t)\right\}\right)(a,b-y)
where
\tilde{M_{1}}=\left(\frac{A_{1}}{\lambda^{2}},B_{1};C_{1},D_{1}\lambda^{2}\right).
If further, the window function
\psi
satisfies the admissible conditions
c^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{\psi}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{2}}\left[e^{\frac{it}{B_{1}}}\psi(t)\right]\right)(\frac{B_{2}\xi}{B_{1}a})\right|\frac{da}{a}
is a non zero positive constant then
\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{M_{1},M_{2}}
satisfies the inner product relations

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{M_{1},M_{2}}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{M}g)(a,b)}\frac{dadb}{a}=2\pi|B_{1}|c^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{\psi}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

followed by the inversion formula

f(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi|B_{1}|c^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{\psi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}_{\psi}^{M_{1},M_{2}}f)(a,b)\psi^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{a,b}(t)\frac{dadb}{a},\penalty\ a.e.
As a consequence of the inner product relation it follows that, if
c^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{\psi}=\frac{1}{2\pi|B_{1}|}
then the operator
\mathcal{S}^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{\psi}
is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R},\frac{dadb}{a})
and the range
\mathcal{S}^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{\psi}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
is a RKHS with the kernel
K^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{\psi}(a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime})=\langle\psi^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{a,b},\psi^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.
Author studied the time-LCT-frequency resolution and proved that the joint resolution of the LCST in the time and linear canonical domain is

\displaystyle\left[\frac{1}{a}E_{\psi}+b-\frac{1}{a}\Delta_{\psi},\frac{1}{A}E_{\psi}+b+\frac{1}{a}\Delta_{\psi}\right]\times\left[\frac{B_{1}a}{B_{2}}E_{\Psi}-\frac{B_{1}a}{B_{2}}\Delta_{\Psi},\frac{B_{1}a}{B_{2}}E_{\Psi}+\frac{B_{1}a}{B_{2}}\Delta_{\Psi}\right],

with constant window area
4\frac{B_{1}}{B_{2}}\Delta_{\psi}\Delta_{\Psi}
in the time-LCT-frequency plane, where
\Psi(\xi)=\left(\mathcal{L}^{M_{2}}\{e^{\frac{it}{B_{1}}}\psi(t)\}\right)(\xi).
Author also developed the MRA
\{V^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
which satisfies the properties

(i)

V^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{m}\subset V^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{m+1},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{Z}

(ii)

f(t)\in V^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{m}
if and only if
f(2t)e^{\frac{iA_{1}}{2B_{1}}((2t)^{2}-t^{2})}\in V^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{m+1},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{Z}

(iii)

\cap_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{m}=\{0\},

\overline{\cup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{m}}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

(iv)

there exists a function
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, called the scaling function
V^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{0}
such that
\{\phi_{M_{1},M_{2},0,n}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
form an orthonormal basis of
V^{M_{1},M_{2}}_{0},
where

\phi_{M_{1},M_{2},m,n}(t)=2^{\frac{m}{2}}\phi(2^{m}t-n)e^{-\frac{i}{2}\left[\left(t^{2}-\left(\frac{n}{2^{m}}\right)^{2}\right)\frac{A_{1}}{B_{1}}-(2^{m}t-n)^{2}\frac{A_{2}}{B_{2}}-\frac{2(2^{m}t+n)}{B_{1}}\right]},\penalty\ m,n\in\mathbb{Z}.

and obtained a function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
corresponding to the function
\phi(t)
such that
\{\psi_{M_{1},M_{2},m,n}(t)\}_{m,n\in\mathbb{Z}}
forms a novel orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

19
Quadratic Phase Windowed Fourier Transform (QPWFT)

The importance of arbitrary real parameters involved in the definition of QPFT (
87
) lies in the fact that an appropriate choice of the parameters can be used to inculcate a sense of rotation and shift into both the time and frequency axes, resulting in an efficient analysis of the chirp-like signals which are ubiquitous in nature. Thus, the chirp-like signals arising in radar and other communication systems reveal their characteristics better in the quadratic-phase Fourier domain than in the usual Fourier domain, fractional Fourier, and linear canonical domain. Despite several merits of the QPFT, one of its severe limitations is that it is inept for a localized analysis of the quadratic-phase spectrum of non-transient signals. To circumvent this limitation, Shah et al.
[
173
]
introduced the notion of short-time QPFT, which can effectively localize the quadratic phase spectrum of a non-transient signal in the time-frequency plane for the function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the window function
\phi
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{V}^{\wedge}_{\phi}f)(x,u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\overline{\tau_{x}g(t)}e^{i(At^{2}+Btu+Cu^{2}+Dt+Eu)}dt,

(322)

where
\wedge=(A,B,C,D,E)
with
B\neq 0.
For the parameter
\wedge=\left(\frac{A}{2B},-\frac{1}{B},\frac{C}{2B},0,0\right),

\wedge=\left(\frac{\cot\alpha}{2},-\csc\alpha,\frac{\cot\alpha}{2},0,0\right),

\alpha\neq n\pi,\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\wedge=\left(0,1,0,0,0\right)
and multiplying the right of the equation (
322
) by
\frac{1}{\sqrt{iB}},

\sqrt{1-i\cot\alpha}
and
1
respectively, it can be seen that the WQPFT (
322
) reduces to the WLCT, WFrFT and WFT. Observe that, if
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\penalty\ g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
or
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}),\penalty\ g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
then the WQPFT (
322
) is well defined. Similarly, if
f\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R})
and
g\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}),1\leq p,q<\infty
and
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1,
then by Hölder’s inequality it follows that the transform (
322
) is well defined. Based on the relation between the WQPFT and the WFT, they obtained the Lieb’s inequality for the WQPFT

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|(\mathcal{V}^{\wedge}_{\phi}f)(x,u)\right|^{p}dxdu\leq\frac{2}{p|B|}\|f\|^{p}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\phi\|^{p}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

Transform (
322
) is linear in
f,
anti-linear in
g
and has the following covariance properties

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{V}^{\wedge}_{\phi}\left(\mathcal{M}^{M}_{\mu}\tau_{x_{0}}f\right)\right)(x,u)=e^{i\left(Bx_{0}(u-\mu)+C(u^{2}-\mu^{2})+E(u-\mu)\right)}\mathcal{V}^{\wedge}_{\phi}f(x-x_{0},B(u-\mu))

and

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{V}^{\wedge}_{\phi}\left(\tau_{x_{0}}\mathcal{M}^{M}_{\mu}f\right)\right)(x,u)=e^{i\left(Ax_{0}^{2}+Bx_{0}(\mu+u)+C\left(u^{2}-\mu^{2}\right)+Dx_{0}+E(u-\mu^{2})+2Ax_{0}-AB\right)}\mathcal{V}_{\phi}f(x-x_{0},2Ax_{0}-B(u-\mu)),

where
\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}_{\mu}f(t)=e^{i(At^{2}+Bt\mu+Cu^{2}+Dt+E\mu)}f(t)
is generalized modulation by
\mu.
The covariance property obtained above indeed gives the formula for the WQPFT for the time-frequency shift
\tau_{x_{0}}\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}_{\mu}
or
\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}_{\mu}\tau_{x_{0}}
of the function
f.
Specifically, the formula for the parity, conjugation,
\left(\mathcal{V}^{\wedge}_{\phi}\left(\tau_{x_{0}}f\right)\right)(x,u)
and
\left(\mathcal{V}^{\wedge}_{\phi}\left(e^{iu_{0}x}f\right)\right)(x,u)
can be found in
[
173
]
. For
f_{1},f_{2},\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
the WQPFT (
322
) satisfies the following inner product relation

\displaystyle\left\langle(\mathcal{V}^{\wedge}_{\phi_{1}}f_{1})(x,u),(\mathcal{V}^{\wedge}_{\phi_{2}}f_{2})(x,u)\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\frac{1}{|B|}\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle\overline{\langle\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\rangle}.

(323)

In particular, if
f=g,

\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}=\phi
and
\|\phi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\|\phi_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=1
then
\|\mathcal{V}^{\wedge}_{\phi}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}=\frac{1}{|B|}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}
and thus WQPFT is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The inversion formula for the WQPFT is given as

\displaystyle f(t)=\frac{|B|}{\sqrt{2\pi}\penalty\ \overline{\langle\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\rangle}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{V}^{\wedge}_{\phi_{2}}f)(x,u)e^{-i(At^{2}+Btu+Cu^{2}+Dt+Eu)}\tau_{x}\phi_{1}(t)dudx

(324)

where
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

\langle\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\rangle\neq 0
and
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
Authors in
[
173
]
also characterized the range of the transform (
322
) and obtained the Heisenberg’s uncertainty principle for the transform, for the functions
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
using corresponding UP (
118
) for the QPFT as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}\left|(\mathcal{V}^{\wedge}_{\phi}f)(x,u)\right|^{2}dxdu\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}t^{2}|f(t)|^{2}dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2|B|}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

(325)

For the choice of the parameter
\wedge=\left(\frac{A}{2B},-\frac{1}{B},\frac{C}{2B},0,0\right),

\wedge=\left(\frac{\cot\alpha}{2},-\csc\alpha,\frac{\cot\alpha}{2},0,0\right),\penalty\ \alpha\neq n\pi,\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\wedge=\left(0,1,0,0,0\right)
the inequality (
325
) reduces to the corresponding Heisenberg uncertainty inequality for the WLCT, WFrFT and WFT respectively.

Uncertainty Principles:
They also obtained the logarithmic UP for the transform (
322
), for the function
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}),
using the same (
119
) for the QPFT as

\displaystyle|B|^{2}\|\phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|t||f(t)|^{2}dt+|B|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ln|u|\left|(\mathcal{V}^{\wedge}_{\phi}f)(x,u)\right|^{2}dxdu\geq\left[\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}-\ln\pi-\ln|B|\right]\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

(326)

20
Quadratic Phase Wavelet Transform (QPWT)

The QPFT is a generalization of classical WT, continuous FrWT, and the LCWT. The transform is a more beneficial tool than the QPFT and the QPWFT. Prasad et al.
[
161
]
first introduced the definition, which was based on the quadratic phase convolution (
93
) and is defined for the function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and the parameter
\wedge=(A,B,C,D,E)
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f)(a,b)=

\displaystyle\left(f\star_{\Omega^{A}_{D}}\psi_{a}\right)(b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\overline{\psi^{\Omega^{A}_{D}}_{a,b}(x)}dx,\penalty\ a\in\mathbb{R}^{+},b\in\mathbb{R},

(327)

where
\psi_{a}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\overline{\psi\left(-\frac{t}{a}\right)},

\psi^{\Omega^{A}_{D}}_{a,b}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\psi\left(\frac{t-b}{a}\right)e^{-iA(t^{2}-b^{2})-iD(t-b)},\penalty\ b\in\mathbb{R},\penalty\ a\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.
The transform (
20
) is linear in
f,
anti-linear in
\psi.
With the definition of the translation operator
\tau^{\prime}_{Ar}f(t)=e^{-2Airt}f(t-r),
author in
[
161
]
obtained the translational formula
(\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}\tau_{Ar}f)(a,b)=e^{-2Aibr}(\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f)(a,b-r)
and the scaling formula
(\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}[\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}f\left(\frac{t}{m}\right)])(a,b)=e^{-2Aibr}(\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f)(\frac{a}{m},\frac{b}{m}),\penalty\ m>0.
With the assumption that the functions
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying the condition

c_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}^{\Omega^{A}_{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\overline{\left(\mathcal{Q}^{A,B,C}_{D,E}[e^{-iA(\cdot)^{2}-iD(\cdot)}\psi_{1}]\right)(a)}\left(\mathcal{Q}^{A,B,C}_{D,E}[e^{-iA(\cdot)^{2}-iD(\cdot)}\psi_{2}]\right)(a)\frac{da}{a}

is non-zero complex number, they proved the inner product relation for the functions
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}(\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi_{1}}f)(a,b)\overline{(\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi_{2}}g)(a,b)}\frac{dbda}{a^{2}}=\frac{2\pi}{B}c_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}^{\Omega^{A}_{D}}\langle f,g\rangle,

(328)

followed by the reconstruction formula
f(x)=\frac{B}{2\pi c^{\Omega^{A}_{D}}_{\psi,\psi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}(\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f)(a,b)\psi^{\Omega^{A}_{D}}_{a,b}(x)\frac{dbda}{a^{2}},\penalty\ a.e.
Motivated by the Shannon sampling theorem (
113
), Gupta et al.
[
87
]
studied the MRA associated with QPWT given by (
20
) and obtain a novel class of orthonormal bases. Sharma et al.
[
179
]
established the Abelian theorem, which relates the behavior of the QPWT
(\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f)(a,b)
as
a\rightarrow\infty(a\rightarrow 0)
to the behavior of function
(\mathcal{Q}^{A,B,C}_{D,E}f)(\xi)
as
\xi\rightarrow\infty(\xi\rightarrow 0).
These results are also referred to as initial value and final value Abelian theorems. Based on the convolution (
94
), of the QPFT introduced in
[
177
]
, Shah et al.
[
171
]
introduced a new definition of the QPWT for a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),
with respect to the function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and the parameter set
\wedge=(A,B,C,D,E),
which we denote by
{}^{(1)}\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}
and is defined as

\displaystyle\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f\right)(a,b)=

\displaystyle\left(f(x)\star^{\prime}_{\wedge}\right(\mathcal{D}_{a}\hat{\psi}\left)(-x)\right)(b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\overline{\psi^{\wedge}_{a,b}(x)}dx,\penalty\ a\in\mathbb{R}^{+},b\in\mathbb{R}

(329)

where
\mathcal{D}_{a}
is the usual dilation operator and the family
\psi^{\wedge}_{a,b}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi a}}\psi\left(\frac{x-b}{a}\right)e^{2iAx(x-b)}.
For the choice of the parameter
\wedge=\left(\frac{A}{2B},-\frac{1}{B},\frac{C}{2B},0,0\right),

\wedge=\left(\frac{\cot\alpha}{2},-\csc\alpha,\frac{\cot\alpha}{2},0,0\right),\penalty\ \alpha\neq n\pi,\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\wedge=\left(0,1,0,0,0\right)
the QPWT (
20
) reduces to the novel LCWT, novel FrWT and the classical WT respectively. The transform (
20
) is linear in
f
and anti linear in
\psi
and has the following covariance property

\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f(x-k)\right)(a,b)=e^{2iAk(b-k)}\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}\left[e^{-2iAxk}f(x)\right]\right)(a,b)

and

\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f(\mu x)\right)(a,b)=\frac{1}{\mu}\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f(\mu x)\right)(\mu a,\mu b),\penalty\ \wedge^{\prime}=\left(\frac{A}{\mu^{2}},B,C,D,E\right),

which gives the formula for the QPWT of the translation and scaling of a function
f
, respectively. Apart from this, using the machinery of the QPFT, authors in
[
161
]
obtained orthogonality relation

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f\right)(a,b)\overline{\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}g\right)(a,b)}\frac{dbda}{a^{2}}=c_{\psi}\langle f,g\rangle,

(330)

followed by the reconstruction formula
f(x)=\frac{1}{c_{\psi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f\right)(a,b)\psi^{\wedge}_{a,b}(x)\frac{dbda}{a^{2}},\penalty\ a.e,
with the assumption that the function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies the admissibility condition, i.e.,
0<c_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{\left|\left(\mathcal{Q}_{\wedge}\Psi\right)(a\omega)\right|^{2}}{a}da<\infty,
where
\Psi(x,a)=e^{-i\left(A^{2}a^{2}+Dxa^{2}-Dx\right)}\tilde{\psi}(x),

\tilde{\psi}(x)=\bar{\psi}(-x).
For
f=g
and
c_{\psi}=1,
it follows from (
330
) that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f\right)(a,b)\right|^{2}\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}=\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},
which means that the QPWT is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
into
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}).
They also obtained the reproducing kernel for the range
\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}\left(L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\right)
to be
\mathbb{K}_{\psi}(a,b;a^{\prime},b^{\prime})=\left\langle\psi^{\wedge}_{a,b},\psi^{\wedge}_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\right\rangle.

Uncertainty Principles:
Using the properties of the QPFT and its relation with the QPWT, authors in
[
49
]
,
[
161
]
obtained the Heisenberg’s UP for the transform (
20
) using equation (
118
) as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}b^{2}\left|\left({}^{(1)}\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f\right)(a,b)\right|^{2}\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega^{2}\left|(\mathcal{Q}_{\wedge}f)(\omega)\right|^{2}d\omega\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{c_{\psi}}}{2|B|}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

Moreover, using the logarithmic UP (
119
) authors in
[
49
]
obtained the logarithmic UP for the QPWT (
20
) as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\log|b|\left|\left(\mathcal{W}^{\wedge}_{\psi}f\right)(a,b)\right|^{2}\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}+|B|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega^{2}\left|(\mathcal{Q}_{\wedge}f)(\omega)\right|^{2}d\omega\leq\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}-\ln\pi-\ln|B|\right)\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

In addition to these, using the local and entropy-based UP for the QPFT
[
174
]
, authors also obtained the corresponding UP for the QPWT.

21
Quaternion Windowed Fourier transform (QWFT)

The QFT generalizes the real and complex FT to the quaternion case. However, the QFT is ineffective in studying the local behavior of the quaternion signal. To overcome this, Bülow and Sommer constructed one such extension by studying a particular case of QWFT with quaternionic Gabor filters
[
47
]
,
[
46
]
. Their generalization was obtained using the inverse (two-sided) quaternion Fourier kernel.
Bahri et al.
[
29
]
generalized the classical WFT to quaternion-valued functions of two variables using the right-sided QFT. Before we present here the definition of the QWFT, let us recall that a quaternion window function is a function
\phi\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
|\boldsymbol{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi(\boldsymbol{x})\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
Moreover,
\phi_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{j\omega_{2}x_{2}}e^{i\omega_{1}x_{1}}\phi(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b})
will be called as a quaternionic window daughter function. Thus the QWFT of a function
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with respect to the quaternion window function
\phi
is defined as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}_{\phi}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=

\displaystyle(f,\phi_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{\phi(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b})}e^{-i\omega_{1}x_{1}}e^{-j\omega_{2}x_{2}}d\boldsymbol{x}.

(331)

where the inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)
is defined in (
8
). The QWFT (
21
) is different from
[
28
]
in the definition of the exponential kernel. It is worth mentioning that the order of the exponentials in (
21
) is fixed because of the non-commutativity of the product of quaternions. An example of a quaternion window function is the two-dimensional Haar function

\phi(\boldsymbol{x})=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{for}\penalty\ 0\leq x_{1}<\frac{1}{2}\penalty\ \mbox{and}\penalty\ 0\leq x_{2}<\frac{1}{2}\\
-1,&\mbox{for}\penalty\ \frac{1}{2}\leq x_{1}<1\penalty\ \mbox{and}\penalty\ \frac{1}{2}\leq x_{1}<1\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The transform (
21
) is linear in
f
i.e.,
(\mathcal{G}_{\phi}(\lambda f+\mu g))(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=\lambda(\mathcal{G}_{\phi}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})+\mu(\mathcal{G}_{\phi}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}),\penalty\ \lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{H},

(\mathcal{G}_{P\phi}Pf)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=(\mathcal{G}_{\phi}f)(-\boldsymbol{\omega},-\boldsymbol{b})
and for specific choice of functions
f=f_{0}+if_{2},\penalty\ \phi=\phi_{0}+i\phi_{1}
the transform have the formula
(\mathcal{G}_{\phi}\tau_{x_{0}}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=e^{-i\omega_{1}x_{0}}\left((\mathcal{G}_{\phi}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\right)e^{-j\omega_{2}x_{0}},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{x}_{0}=(x_{0},x_{1}).
Apart from this, using the machinery of the right-sided QFT
[
97
]
,
[
30
]
, authors in
[
29
]
obtained the reconstruction formula
f(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{C_{\phi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\mathcal{G}_{\phi}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\phi_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}}(\boldsymbol{x})d\boldsymbol{b}d\boldsymbol{\omega},
with the assumption that
C_{\phi}=\|\phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
and
0<C_{\phi}<\infty
and proved the orthogonality relation

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\mathcal{G}_{\phi}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\overline{(\mathcal{G}_{\phi}g)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b}=\frac{C_{\phi}}{(2\pi)^{2}}(f,g).

In particular, for
f=g,
and
C_{\phi}=(2\pi)^{2}
gives
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|(\mathcal{G}_{\phi}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{b}d\boldsymbol{\omega}=\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},
this shows that the QWFT is an isometry from
L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
into
L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}),
i.e., the energy of the quaternion-valued signal computed in the spatial domain is equal to the total energy computed in the QWFT domain. They also obtain the reproducing kernel for the range to be
\mathbb{K}_{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b};\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime})=\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{C_{\phi}}(\phi_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}},\phi_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime}}).
Bahri et al. in
[
24
]
explored further properties and some important inequalities by considered the transform (
21
) with an extra factor
(2\pi)^{2},
we call it for our convenience
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\phi}f=(2\pi)^{2}\mathcal{G}_{\phi}f.
Using the relation between the
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\phi}f
and the right-sided Fourier transform authors in
[
24
]
gave the alternate proofs of these properties. They obtained the orthogonality relation and the inversion formula depending on two window functions. They also obtained the Lieb’s inequality as
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\left(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\phi}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\right|^{p}_{Q}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b}\penalty\ \begin{cases}\leq(\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})})^{p}&\mbox{for}\penalty\ 2\leq p<\infty\\
\geq(\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})})^{p}&\mbox{for}\penalty\ 1\leq p<2\end{cases}.
Authors in
[
29
]
also pointed out that the extension of the WFT to the quaternion case by means of the two-sided QFT is complicated because of the non-commutativity. As a result, a major problem for the representation of the kernel of the two-sided QWFT arises. Fu et al.
[
72
]
defined the two-sided QWFT of
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to a non-zero windowed function
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
as

\displaystyle(\mathtt{Q}_{g}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-2\pi ix_{1}\omega_{1}}f(\boldsymbol{x})g(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b})e^{-2\pi jx_{2}\omega_{2}}d\boldsymbol{x}.

(332)

It can be seen from the above definition that the classical way of expressing it as a linear inner product between function and kernel is not possible. So authors defined the right multiplicative operator
\mathcal{C}[\lambda,\cdot]
and
\mathcal{C}[\cdot,\lambda],

\lambda
being a quaternion-valued function, as
\mathcal{C}[\lambda,f]=f(\boldsymbol{x})\lambda(\boldsymbol{x})
and
\mathcal{C}[f,\lambda]=\lambda(\boldsymbol{x})f(\boldsymbol{x}),
respectively and expressed the kernel of the QWFT as an atom operator
g_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}}=e^{2\pi j\omega_{2}x_{2}}g(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b})\mathcal{C}[e^{2\pi i\omega_{1}x_{1}},\cdot],

\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.
The kernel operator is such that for an arbitrary function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{H},

g_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}}\overline{f(\boldsymbol{x})}=e^{2\pi j\omega_{2}x_{2}}g(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b})\overline{f(\boldsymbol{x})}e^{2\pi i\omega_{1}x_{1}}\penalty\ \mbox{and}\penalty\ f(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{g_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}}}=e^{-2\pi i\omega_{1}x_{1}}g(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b})f(\boldsymbol{x})e^{-2\pi j\omega_{2}x_{2}}

and so
g
being real valued,
f(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{g_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}}}=\overline{g_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}}\overline{f(\boldsymbol{x})}}.
Thus the integral representation formula for the transform (
332
) as

(\mathtt{Q}_{g}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=(f,g_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{g_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}}}d\boldsymbol{x}\penalty\ \mbox{and}\penalty\ (\mathtt{Q}_{g}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=(f,g_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}})=\overline{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}}\overline{f(\boldsymbol{x})}d\boldsymbol{x}}.

Authors in
[
72
]
obtained several properties of the QWFT (
332
) like
(\mathtt{Q}_{Pg}Pf)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=(\mathtt{Q}_{g}f)(-\boldsymbol{\omega},-\boldsymbol{b}),

(\mathtt{Q}_{g}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}f))(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=e^{-2\pi ix_{1}\omega_{1}}(\mathtt{Q}_{g}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0})e^{-2\pi jx_{2}\omega_{2}}

(\mathtt{Q}_{g}[e^{2\pi ix_{1}\omega_{01}}f(\boldsymbol{x})e^{2\pi jx_{2}\omega_{02}}])(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=(\mathtt{Q}_{g}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0},\boldsymbol{b}),\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}=(\omega_{01},\omega_{02}).
Apart from this author also obtained the reconstruction formula

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{C_{g}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\mathtt{Q}_{g}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\overline{g_{-\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}}}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b},

(333)

and the orthogonality relation
\langle\mathtt{Q}_{g}f_{1},\mathtt{Q}_{g}f_{2})\rangle=C_{g}\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle,
where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is defined in (
2
) and
C_{g}=\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})}.
For more properties and examples of QWFT (
332
) one can refer
[
65
]
. With the window function,
g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
recently Kamel et al.
[
110
]
considered a two sided QWFT for the function
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as

\displaystyle\left(\mathit{G}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-i\omega_{1}x_{1}}f(\boldsymbol{x})\overline{g(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b})}e^{-j\omega_{2}x_{2}}d\boldsymbol{x}

(334)

and expressed the transform in term of the two-sided QFT
\mathcal{F}_{s}
(
122
) as
\left(\mathit{G}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=\left(\mathcal{F}_{s}\left(f\overline{\tau_{\boldsymbol{b}}g}\right)\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega})
using which they proved the orthogonality relation for QWFT (
334
) depending on two windowed functions
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
as
\langle\mathit{G}_{g_{1}}f_{1},\mathit{G}_{g_{2}}f_{2}\rangle=\langle f_{1}(\bar{g_{1}},\bar{g_{2}}),f_{2}\rangle,
where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and
(\cdot,\cdot)
are respectively given by (
2
) and (
8
). With the assumption that the windowed function
g
is real valued authors obtained the reconstruction formula for the transform in (
334
) as
f(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{2\pi\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{ix_{1}\omega_{1}}\left(\mathit{G}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})g(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b})e^{jx_{2}\omega_{2}}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b}
and proved that the range
\mathit{G}_{g}f(L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is a RKHS with the kernel
K(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime})=\frac{2\pi}{\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})}}(\mathit{G}_{g}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{b}}f))(\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\omega}).
Li et al.
[
124
]
expressed the two sided QWFT (
332
), also given in
[
65
]
, in terms of the convolution (
142
) for the function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and proved the linearity and anti-linearity with respect to the real scalars, translation, scaling and parity properties along with the Parseval’s formula and with the quaternion window they also obtained the inversion formula and characterized the range of the transform. Brahim et al.
[
44
]
further explored the properties of the transform (
334
) and proved the inequality

\displaystyle\|\mathit{G}_{g}f\|_{L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq(2\pi)^{\frac{2}{p}-1}C_{p,q}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|g\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\penalty\ C_{p,q}=\left(\frac{4}{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\frac{1}{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},\penalty\ \frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1,\penalty\ p\geq 2

(335)

called the Lieb’s inequality for the QWFT.

In the Cayley-Dickson form representation of the quaternion
q=(a_{1}+ib_{1})+j(a_{2}+ib_{2})
, two imaginary numbers
i
and
j
are involved. In view of the above definitions of the QWFT, it is observed that for each
i
and
j
, the Fourier kernels
e^{-ix_{1}\omega_{1}}
and
e^{-jx_{2}\omega_{2}}
are used to define it. Thus, it is necessary to assume that
f
must be a function of two variables (or even dimension in general). Akila et al.
[
6
]
gave two definitions of the QWFT for the function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d},
which are given by

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{G}^{(1)}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=\left(f_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\ostar\check{\tilde{g}}\right)(\boldsymbol{b}),\penalty\ (\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d},

(336)

and

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{G}^{(2)}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=\left(G_{g_{1}}f_{1}+G_{\bar{g}_{2}}\bar{f}_{2}+j\left(G_{g_{1}}f_{2}+G_{\bar{g}_{2}}\bar{f}_{1}\right)\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})

(337)

where
\ostar
denotes the quaternion convolution given by (
142
),
f=f_{1}+jf_{2}\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
f_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(\boldsymbol{x})e^{-i\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}},

\check{f}(\boldsymbol{x})=f(-\boldsymbol{x})
and
g=g_{1}+jg_{2}\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is window function with
\tilde{g}(\boldsymbol{x})=\hat{g}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})-j\check{g}_{2}(\boldsymbol{x}).
Although both definitions can be defined for the case when
d=2,
none of these coincides with those discussed earlier. Moreover, they are both consistent with the WFT on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C}).
The properties like linearity, scaling and pairity given respectively by
\mathcal{G}^{(l)}_{g}(\alpha f_{1}+\beta f_{2})=\alpha\mathcal{G}^{(l)}_{g}f_{1}+\beta\mathcal{G}^{(l)}_{g}f_{2},

\mathcal{G}^{(l)}_{g}(f(\lambda\boldsymbol{x}))=\frac{1}{|\lambda|^{d}}\left(\mathcal{G}^{(l)}_{g(\lambda\boldsymbol{x})}f(\boldsymbol{x})\right)(\frac{\boldsymbol{\omega}}{\lambda},\lambda\boldsymbol{b})
and
\left(\mathcal{G}^{(l)}_{\check{g}}\check{f}\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=\left(\mathcal{G}^{(l)}_{g}f\right)(-\boldsymbol{\omega},-\boldsymbol{b}),
for
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{H},\penalty\ \lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{0},
are common to both the transforms (
336
) and (
337
), but the translation formula are different and are given respectively by
\left(\mathcal{G}^{(1)}_{g}(f(\boldsymbol{x}-\textbf{a}))\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=\left(\mathcal{G}^{(1)}_{g_{1}}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}-\textbf{a})e^{-i\textbf{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}}+\left(\mathcal{G}^{(1)}_{jg_{2}}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}+\textbf{a})e^{i\textbf{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}}
and
\left(\mathcal{G}^{(2)}_{g}(f(\boldsymbol{x}-\textbf{a}))\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})=\left(\mathcal{G}^{(2)}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}-\textbf{a})e^{-i\textbf{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}}.
The following Parseval’s formula holds for both the transforms

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\mathcal{G}^{(l)}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\overline{\left(\mathcal{G}^{(l)}_{g}h\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b}=(2\pi)^{d}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}(f,h),\penalty\ l=1,2

thus the transforms maps
L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
into
L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
and is one-one. Furthermore, if the window function
g
satisfies the condition
(2\pi)^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\hat{g}_{1}\check{g}_{1}+\hat{g}_{2}\check{g}_{2}\right)=C\neq 0,
then the following reconstruction formula holds for the transform (
336
)

f(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{C}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\mathcal{G}^{(1)}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})e^{i\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}}\bullet\check{g}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b})d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b},

where
(f_{1}+jf_{2})\bullet(g_{1}+jg_{2})=f_{1}g_{1}-\check{\bar{f}}_{2}g_{2}+j\left(\check{\bar{f}}_{1}g_{2}+f_{2}g_{1}\right)
and for the transform (
337
), the reconstruction formula is given by
f(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\mathcal{G}^{(2)}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})e^{i\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}}g(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b})d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b}.
Authors in
[
6
]
, also characterized the range of the transform (
337
) and proved that
H\in\mathcal{G}^{(2)}_{g}(L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
if and only if it satisfies the relation
H(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}H(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\left(g_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}},g_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime}}\right)d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b},
where
g_{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b}}(\boldsymbol{x})=e^{i\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}}g(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b}).

Uncertainty Principles:
Using the Heisenberg UP for the right sided QFT
[
30
]
and following the methods adopted by Wilczok
[
209
]
, authors in
[
29
]
obtained the Heisenberg UP for the QWFT (
21
) as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{l}^{2}\left|(\mathcal{G}_{\phi}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}\boldsymbol{b}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x_{l}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\frac{1}{4\pi}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\penalty\ l=1,2.

Bahri et al. in
[
24
]
considered the function
\phi,f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{H})
and using the Pitt’s inequality and logarithmic UP for the right-sided QFT, obtained, respectively, the Pitt’s inequality

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{\omega}|^{-\alpha}\left|(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\phi}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\right|^{2}_{Q}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b}\leq C_{\alpha}\|\phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{x}|^{\alpha}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x},\penalty\ 0\leq\alpha<2

and the logarithmic UP for the QWFT (
21
) as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|\boldsymbol{\omega}|\left|(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\phi}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b}+\|\phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|\boldsymbol{x}||f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\geq\left(\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}-\ln\pi\right)\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|\phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

where
C_{\alpha}
and the module
|\cdot|_{Q}
are same as in (
131
).
Fu et al.
[
72
]
(see also
[
65
]
) obtained the Heisenberg UP for the two sided QWFT (
332
) as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega^{2}_{l}\left|(\mathtt{Q}_{g}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b}\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x^{2}_{l}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\right)\geq\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\|f\|^{4}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})}

and also considering the discrete version of the kernel of the inverse QWFT (
333
), authors established a new Gabor system
\overline{g_{-\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{n}}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathcal{C}[\cdot,e^{2\pi im_{1}x_{1}}]g(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{n})e^{2\pi jm_{2}x_{2}},\penalty\ \boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\boldsymbol{m}=(m_{1},m_{2}),\boldsymbol{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},
which induces an orthonormal basis with respect to the scalar inner product. Furthermore, they also proved the corresponding Balian-Low theorem, which can be regarded as the strong version of the Heisenberg UP for the Gabor system. More precisely, if the system
\{\overline{g_{-\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{n}}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}
form a Gabor orthonormal basis, then

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x^{2}_{l}|g(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{l}|(\mathcal{F}_{Q}g)(\boldsymbol{\omega})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)=\infty,\penalty\ l=1,2,

where
\mathcal{F}_{Q}
is given by (
125
). Moreover, the author also pointed out that the Balian-Low theorem also holds for the right-sided QWFT (
21
) discussed in
[
29
]
. Kamel et al.
[
110
]
studied the UPs for the QWFT (
334
). Using the logarithmic UP (
132
) for the two-sided QFT
\mathcal{F}_{s}
, authors obtained the logarithmic UP for the QWFT as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|\boldsymbol{\omega}||\left(\mathit{G}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})|^{2}_{Q}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b}+\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ln|\boldsymbol{x}||f(\boldsymbol{t})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{x}\geq\left(\ln(2\pi^{2})-2\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}(\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}\right)\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

(338)

They also proved that for a subset
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying the condition
|\Omega|<(2\pi)^{2},

\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\sqrt{1-\frac{|\Omega|}{(2\pi)^{2}}}\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\chi_{\Omega^{c}}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\left|\left(\mathit{G}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

which says that the portion of
\mathit{G}_{g}f
lying outside some set
\Omega
cannot be arbitrary small, followed by the local Price’s inequality which says that for
p>1,

0<\epsilon<2
and a measurable subset
\Omega_{0}
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
there exists a constant
M_{\epsilon,p}
depending on
p
and
\epsilon
such that

\|\chi_{\Omega_{0}}\mathit{G}_{g}f\|^{p(p+1)}_{L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq M_{\epsilon,p}|\Omega_{0}|\||(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})|^{\epsilon}\mathit{G}_{g}f\|^{p(p+1)}_{L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})}\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|g\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\right)^{p\left(p-\frac{2-\epsilon}{2+\epsilon}\right)}.

The logarithmic UP for the QWFT (
332
), with the module
|\cdot|_{Q}
replaced with the quaternion module
|\cdot|
in (
338
) can be found in
[
65
]
. Using Lieb’s inequality (
335
), authors in
[
44
]
, proved that if
f\neq 0,

\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\penalty\ \epsilon\geq 0
and is
\epsilon-
concentrated on
\Omega
then for every
p>2,

\left(C_{p,q}\right)^{\frac{2p}{2-p}}(1-\epsilon^{2})^{\frac{p}{p-2}}\leq\frac{|\Omega|}{(2\pi)^{2}},
which is Lieb’s UP for the QWFT (
334
). They also proved the Beckner’s UP in terms of entropy as
E\left(\left|\mathit{G}_{g}f\right|^{2}\right)\geq\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\left(2\ln(2)-\ln\left(\frac{\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\right)\right)

using which they obtained the generalized Heisenberg’s UP for the QWFT (
334
) as

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{\omega}|^{p}\left|\left(\mathit{G}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b}\right)^{\frac{q}{p+q}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\boldsymbol{b}|^{q}\left|\left(\mathit{G}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b}\right)^{\frac{p}{p+q}}\geq D_{p,q}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

(339)

where
D_{p,q}=\frac{2}{p^{\frac{q}{p+q}}q^{\frac{p}{P+q}}}e^{\frac{pq\left(2\ln(2)+\ln\left(\frac{pq}{\Gamma(\frac{2}{p})\Gamma(\frac{2}{q})}\right)\right)}{2(p+q)}-1}
and
p,q
are positive real numbers.. Akila et al.
[
6
]
obtained the Heisenberg UPs for the QWFT (
336
) and (
337
) as

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\omega_{k}^{2}\left|\left(\mathcal{G}^{(l)}_{g}f\right)(\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{b})\right|^{2}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\boldsymbol{b}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x_{k}^{2}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\frac{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}{2}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|g\|_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\penalty\ l=1,2

22
Quaternion Wavelet transform (QWT)

Let
\mathcal{G}
denotes the similitude group
SIM(2)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined as
\mathcal{G}=\mathbb{R}^{+}\times SO(2)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}=\{(a,r_{\theta},\boldsymbol{b})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}a\in\mathbb{R}^{+},r_{\theta}\in SO(2),\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\},

SO(2)
being special orthogonal group of
\mathbb{R}^{2}.
The group law associated with
\mathcal{G}
is given by

(a,\boldsymbol{b},r_{\theta})(a^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime},r_{\theta^{\prime}})=(aa^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}+ar_{\theta}\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime},r_{\theta+\theta^{\prime}}).
The rotation
r_{\theta}\in SO(2)
acts on
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
as
r_{\theta}(\boldsymbol{x})=(x_{1}\cos\theta-x_{2}\sin\theta,x_{1}\sin\theta+x_{2}\cos\theta),\penalty\ 0\leq\theta\leq 2\pi
and the left Haar measure on
\mathcal{G}
is given by
d\lambda(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b})=d\mu(a,\theta)d\boldsymbol{b},
where
d\mu(a,\theta)=\frac{dad\theta}{a^{3}}
and
d\theta
is the Haar measure on
SO(2).

A function
\psi\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is said to be an admissible quaternion wavelet if it satisfies the admissibility condition
c_{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{SO(2)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left|\hat{\psi}\left(ar_{-\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\right)\right|^{2}\frac{dad\theta}{a}
is a real positive constant independent of
\boldsymbol{\omega}
satisfying
|\boldsymbol{\omega}|=1,
and hence independent of
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.
Bahri et al.
[
25
]
, defined the unitary linear operator
U_{a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\rightarrow L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})
as
U_{a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b}}(\psi)=\psi_{a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{a}\psi\left(r_{-\theta}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b}}{a}\right)\right),\penalty\ a\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
r_{-\theta}\in SO(2)
and called the family
\psi_{a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b}}
as the quaternion daughter wavelets. They defined the QWT of
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with respect to the quaternion admissible wavelet
\psi
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b})=(f,\psi_{a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\boldsymbol{x})\frac{1}{a}\overline{\psi\left(r_{-\theta}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b}}{a}\right)\right)}d\boldsymbol{x}.

(340)

Here,
a
is a dilation parameter,
\boldsymbol{b}
a translation parameter and
\theta
an
SO(2)
rotation parameter. It is to be noted that the order of the terms on (
340
) is fixed because of the non-commutativity of the quaternion multiplication. Moreover, with the assumption that
(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi_{l})(\boldsymbol{\omega}),\penalty\ l=0,1,2,3
are real-valued, the transform (
340
), has the quaternion Fourier representation of the form

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b})=\frac{a}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\mathfrak{F}_{q}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega})e^{ib_{2}\omega_{1}}\left[\overline{(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi_{0l})(ar_{-\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))}e^{ib_{1}\omega_{1}}+\overline{(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi_{1l})(ar_{-\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))}e^{-ib_{1}\omega_{1}}\right]d\boldsymbol{\omega},

(341)

where
(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi_{0l})(ar_{-\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))=(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi_{0})(ar_{-\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))+i(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi_{1})(ar_{-\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega})),

(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi_{1l})(ar_{-\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))=j(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi_{2})(ar_{-\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))+k(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi_{3})(ar_{-\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))
and
(\mathfrak{F}_{q}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\boldsymbol{x})e^{-ix_{1}\omega_{1}}e^{-jx_{2}\omega_{2}}d\boldsymbol{x}.
The transform (
340
) is linear in
f
and anti-linear in
\psi
with respect to the quaternion scalars and satisfies the following covariance properties
(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f(\cdot-\boldsymbol{x}_{0}))(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b})=(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0}),

(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f(c\cdot))(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b})=\frac{1}{c}(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(ac,\theta,\boldsymbol{b}c),\penalty\ c>0
and
(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f(r_{\theta_{0}}\cdot))(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b})=(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,\theta^{\prime},r_{\theta_{0}}\boldsymbol{b}),\penalty\ r_{\theta^{\prime}}=r_{\theta_{0}}r_{\theta}
which are respectively called the translation, dilation and rotational covariance properties. Similarly, results can be obtained with changes in the admissible quaternion wavelet
\psi.
Apart from these basic properties authors in
[
25
]
obtained the inner product relation

\displaystyle\int_{SO(2)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b})\overline{(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}g)(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b})}d\boldsymbol{b}\right)\frac{dad\theta}{a^{3}}=c_{\psi}(f,g)

(342)

for
f,g\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with the assumption that
\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}).
Bahri
[
13
]
gave an alternative proof for (
342
). In particular if
f=g,
then
\|\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})}=c_{\psi}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},
as a result of which it follows that, except for the factor
c_{\psi},
the transform
\mathcal{W}_{\psi}
is an isometry from
L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to
L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G}).
With similar assumptions on
\psi
as in (
342
), the reconstruction formula for the transform (
340
) is given by

f(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{c_{\psi}}\int_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b})\psi_{a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b}}(\boldsymbol{x})d\lambda(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b}),

where the integral converges in the weak sense. Moreover, the range of the transform is a RKHS with the kernel
K_{\psi}(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b};a^{\prime},\theta^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{c_{\psi}}(\psi_{a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b}},\psi_{a^{\prime},\theta^{\prime},\boldsymbol{b}^{\prime}}).
Bahri et al.
[
26
]
,
[
27
]
studied the properties of the QWT (
340
) using the machinery of the QFT given by
(\mathbf{F}f)(\boldsymbol{\omega})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\boldsymbol{x})e^{\frac{I+j+k}{\sqrt{3}}\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}}d\boldsymbol{\omega}
(see
[
27
]
for more details). With the assumption that the functions
\{\psi,\phi\}
in
L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is an admissible quaternion wavelet pair, i.e.,
C_{\{\psi,\phi\}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{SO(2)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\overline{(\mathbf{F}\psi)(ar_{\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))}(\mathbf{F}\phi)(ar_{\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))\frac{dad\theta}{a}
is a non-zero quaternion constant independent of
|\boldsymbol{\omega}|=1,
and
\phi
and
\psi
satisfies the conditions
e^{\frac{i+j+k}{\sqrt{3}}}(\mathbf{F}\phi)(ar_{\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))=(\mathbf{F}\phi)(ar_{\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))=e^{\frac{i+j+k}{\sqrt{3}}}
and
e^{\frac{i+j+k}{\sqrt{3}}}(\mathbf{F}\psi)(ar_{\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))=(\mathbf{F}\psi)(ar_{\theta}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))=e^{\frac{i+j+k}{\sqrt{3}}}
respectively, authors obtained the following inner product relation

\int_{SO(2)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b})\overline{(\mathcal{W}_{\phi}g)(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b})}d\boldsymbol{b}\right)\frac{dad\theta}{a^{3}}=(fC_{\{\psi,\phi\}},g)

followed by the inversion formula
f(\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{W}_{\psi}f)(a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b})\phi_{a,\theta,\boldsymbol{b}}(\boldsymbol{x})C_{\{\psi,\phi\}}^{-1}\frac{dad\theta}{a^{3}}d\boldsymbol{b},
where the integral converges in the weak sense. Tefjeni et al.
[
197
]
considered an admissible quaternion wavelet pair as function
\phi,\psi\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
such that

C^{\prime}_{\psi,\phi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\overline{(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi)(a\boldsymbol{\omega})}(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\phi)(a\boldsymbol{\omega})\frac{da}{a}

is a non-zero quaternion constant independent of
\boldsymbol{\omega}
satisfying
|\boldsymbol{\omega}|=1.
In particular, a function
\psi\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
is said to be admissible quaternion wavelet if
C^{\prime}_{\psi}=C^{\prime}_{\psi,\psi}
is a positive constant independent of
\boldsymbol{\omega}
satisfying
|\boldsymbol{\omega}|=1.
With the assumption that the admissible quaternion wavelet
\psi
(and also
\phi
) satisfies the conditions

\displaystyle\psi=\psi_{0}+i\psi_{1}\penalty\ \mbox{and}\penalty\ e^{jb_{2}\omega_{2}}\overline{(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi)(a\boldsymbol{\omega})}=\overline{(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi)(a\boldsymbol{\omega})}e^{jb_{2}\omega_{2}}

(343)

or

\displaystyle\psi=j\psi_{2}+k\psi_{3}\penalty\ \mbox{and}\penalty\ e^{jb_{2}\omega_{2}}\overline{(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi)(a\boldsymbol{\omega})}=\overline{(\mathfrak{F}_{q}\psi)(a\boldsymbol{\omega})}e^{-jb_{2}\omega_{2}}

(344)

authors in
[
197
]
proved that the QWT for the function
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
given by

\displaystyle(T_{\psi}f)(a,\boldsymbol{b})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f(\boldsymbol{x})\frac{1}{a^{d}}\overline{\psi\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b}}{a}\right)}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{x}),

(345)

where
d\mu_{d}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{d\boldsymbol{x}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}},
satisfies the Parseval’s theorem

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(T_{\psi}f)(a,\boldsymbol{b})\overline{(T_{\phi}g)(a,\boldsymbol{b})}\frac{da}{a^{2d+1}}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{b})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f(\boldsymbol{x})C^{\prime}_{\psi,\phi}\overline{g(\boldsymbol{x})}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{x})

followed by the reconstruction formula
f(\boldsymbol{x})=\{C^{\prime}_{\psi}\}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(T_{\psi}f)(a,\boldsymbol{b})\frac{1}{a^{d}}\psi\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{b}}{a}\right)\frac{da}{a^{2d+1}}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{b}).

If
\phi,\psi
are two admissible quaternion wavelet satisfying (
343
) or (
344
) and
p\geq 1,
then for every
f,g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
the function
(a,\boldsymbol{b})\rightarrow(T_{\psi}f)(a,\boldsymbol{b})(T_{\phi}g)(a,\boldsymbol{b})
belongs to
L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2d})
and satisfies the Lieb’s inequality

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|(T_{\psi}f)(a,\boldsymbol{b})(T_{\phi}g)(a,\boldsymbol{b})|^{p}\frac{da}{a^{2d+1}}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{b})\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sqrt{C^{\prime}_{\phi}C^{\prime}_{\psi}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left\{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\psi(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{x})\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\phi(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{x})\right)\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{x})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|g(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{x})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Moreover, if
\psi
be an admissible quaternion wavelet satisfying the assumptions (
343
) or (
344
), then for every
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
the transform
T_{\psi}f
given by (
345
) belong to
L^{p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2d})
for
2\leq p\leq\infty
and for
p\geq 2,
it satisfies

\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|(T_{\psi}f)(a,\boldsymbol{b})|^{p}\frac{da}{a^{2d+1}}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{b})\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq(C^{\prime}_{\psi})^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\psi(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{x})\right)^{1-\frac{2}{p}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{x})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

as a consequence of which it follows that, if
T_{\psi}f,
for
f\neq 0,
is
\epsilon-
concentrated on a measurable set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2d}=G
of non zero finite measure
\mu_{G}(\Omega),
where
\mu_{G}
is the measure defined on
G
by
dw(a,\boldsymbol{b})=\frac{da}{a^{2d+1}}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{b}),
then for every
p>2,

\mu_{G}(\Omega)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|\psi(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{x})\geq C^{\prime}_{\psi}(1-\epsilon^{2})^{\frac{p}{p-2}}.

This is the Lieb’s UP associated to the QWT (
345
). Apart from this author in
[
197
]
also obtained the associated Beckner’s UP in terms of entropy as

E\left(|T_{\psi}f|^{2}\right)\geq C^{\prime}_{\psi}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{x})\right)\ln\left(\frac{1}{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{x})\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|f(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{x})\right)}\right),

where
E\left(|T_{\psi}f|^{2}\right)=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\ln\left(|(T_{\psi}f)(a,\boldsymbol{b})|^{2}\right)|(T_{\psi}f)(a,\boldsymbol{b})|^{2}\frac{da}{a^{2d+1}}d\mu_{2d}(\boldsymbol{b}).
Using the Heisenberg’s and logarithmic UPs for the QFT
\mathfrak{F}_{q},
Bahri et al.
[
23
]
obtained the Heisenberg and logarithmic UPs for the QWT (
345
) for the case when
d=1.
So far, we have discussed the QWT for the function with the variable in 2 or even dimension. The QWT for the function
f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
can be found in
[
93
]
. He et al.
[
93
]
decomposed the space
L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})=H^{(+,+)}\oplus H^{(-,-)},
where
H^{(+,+)}=\{f=f_{1}+jf_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}f_{1},f_{2}\in H^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})\}
and
H^{(-,-)}=\{f=f_{1}+jf_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}f_{1},f_{2}\in H^{2}_{-}(\mathbb{R})\}
with the Hardy space
H^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})=\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\hat{f}(\xi)=0,if\penalty\ \xi\nin\mathbb{R}^{+}\}
and the conjugate Hardy space
H^{2}_{-}(\mathbb{R})=\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\hat{f}(\xi)=0,if\penalty\ \xi\nin\mathbb{R}^{-}\}.
They obtained the characterization of the quaternion admissible wavelet for the function
\phi\in H^{(+,+)}\left(\mbox{or}\penalty\ H^{(-,-)}\right)
as
c_{\phi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\hat{\phi}(\xi)|^{2}}{|\xi|}d\xi<\infty
and studied the properties of the QWT
(\mathcal{W}_{\phi}f)(a,b)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\phi\left(\frac{x-b}{a}\right)\right)^{c}dx
for the function
f\in H^{(+,+)}\left(\mbox{or}\penalty\ H^{(-,-)}\right)
and the admissible quaternion wavelet
\phi\in H^{(+,+)}\left(\mbox{or}\penalty\ H^{(-,-)}\right).
With any one of the assumptions, for the admissible quaternion wavelets
\phi=\phi_{1}+j\phi_{2},\psi=\psi_{1}+j\psi_{2}\in H^{(+,+)}
and
f=f_{1}+jf_{2},g=g_{1}+jg_{2}\in H^{(+,+)},
among the following

1.

\bar{\hat{\phi}}_{l}(\xi)\bar{\hat{\psi}}_{l}(\xi),\penalty\ l=1,2
are real functions.

2.

f=f_{1},\penalty\ g=g_{1}
or
g=jg_{2}

3.

f=jf_{2},\penalty\ g=jg_{2}
or
g=g_{1},

authors in
[
93
]
obtained the inner product relation
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{W}_{\phi}f)(a,b)\left((\mathcal{W}_{\phi}f)(a,b)\right)^{c}\frac{dadb}{a^{2}}=c_{\phi,\psi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)(g(x))^{c}dx,
where
c_{\phi,\psi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\bar{\hat{\phi}}_{1}(\xi)\hat{\psi}(\xi)+\bar{\hat{\phi}}_{2}(\xi)\hat{\psi}_{2}(\xi)\right)\frac{d\xi}{|\xi|},
which was not in general true for the an arbitrary quaternion wavelet
\phi
and functions
f
in
H^{(+,+)}.
They also obtained the following reproducing formula for the admissible quaternion wavelet
\phi
and
f\in H^{(+,+)}

f(x)=\frac{1}{c_{\phi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}(\mathcal{W}_{\phi}f)(a,b)\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\phi\left(\frac{x-b}{a^{2}}\right)\frac{dxda}{a^{2}}

with the convergence of the integral in the weak sense. Akila et al.
[
5
]
introduced the QWT with respect to
\phi=\phi_{1}+j\phi_{2}\in L^{1}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying
c_{\phi}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\hat{\phi_{1}}(\xi)|^{2}+|\hat{\phi_{2}}(\xi)|^{2}}{\xi}d\xi<\infty
as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}_{\phi}f)(a,b)=(f\ostar\check{\tilde{\phi}}_{a})(b),\penalty\ (a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}

(346)

where
\tilde{\phi}=\bar{\phi}_{1}-j\check{\phi}_{2},

\phi_{a}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\phi\left(\frac{1}{a}\right),

f\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
and
\ostar
denotes the quaternion convolution given by (
142
). The definition (
346
) is consistent with the definition of the classical WT on
L^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}).
Using the convolution theorem associated with the convolution
\ostar
(
142
), authors obtained the Parseval’s formula for the QWT (
346
) for the function
f,g\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{R})
as
